Mahāmati, the distinct kinds of cause of form and space should be understood as being related as what is supported and its support. Mahāmati, when the elements arise, they are distinct from one another through the particularity of their own characteristics, and they do not abide in space either. Yet in them too there is no space."

Mahāmati, in the same way, the horn of a hare is posited in relation to the horn of an ox. Yet if the ox's horn is analyzed into atoms, and the atoms too are broken down, so that it does not remain even in the characteristic of an atom, then in relation to what could the hare's horn be nonexistent? And if it were an entity posited in relation to something else, that too would be of just the same nature.

Then the Blessed One spoke these words to the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati: "Mahāmati, you and the other bodhisattvas should be free from the conceptuality that views the horns of the hare and the ox, space, and form. Mahāmati, you should also become one whose mind understands that conceptuality concerns the appearances of your own mind. Among all the children of the Victor and throughout all the maṇḍalas of buddha-fields, you should teach the yoga of the appearances of one's own mind."

Then, at that time, the Blessed One spoke these verses:

The manifold appearances do not exist;
from appearance, mind arises.
As if it were body, enjoyments, and abode,
the all-base appears to people.

Mind, mentation, and consciousness,
the natures and the five dharmas,
and the two selflessnesses, purified:
these the Guide proclaims.

From mutual relation
arise long and short and the rest.
Existence establishes nonexistence,
and nonexistence establishes existence.

Break matter down into atoms,
and form is not to be imagined.
The establishing of mind-only
is not made clear by those with wrong views.

What the protectors teach
is the domain of individual self-awareness.
It is not the domain of the conceptual-minded,
nor is it that of the śrāvakas.

Then the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, again made a request to the Blessed One, for the sake of purifying the continuum of the appearances of one's own mind: "Blessed One, how is the continuum of the appearances of one's own mind purified? Is it all at once, or by gradual entry?"

The Blessed One said: "Mahāmati, the continuum of the appearances of one's own mind is purified by gradual entry, not all at once. Mahāmati, it is like this: the fruit of the mango tree ripens gradually, not all at once. Mahāmati, in the same way, the continuum of the appearances of beings' own minds is purified gradually, not all at once. Mahāmati, it is like this: a potter makes vessels gradually, not all at once. Mahāmati, in the same way, the Tathāgata purifies the continuum of the appearances of beings' own minds gradually, not all at once. Mahāmati, it is like this: from the great earth, grasses, shrubs, medicinal herbs, and forests grow by gradual entry, not all at once. In the same way, Mahāmati, the Tathāgata purifies the continuum of the appearances of beings' own minds gradually, not all at once. Mahāmati, it is like this: skill in comedy, music, song, the lute, cymbals, and painting comes about gradually, not all at once. In the same way, Mahāmati, the Tathāgata purifies the continuum of the appearances of all beings' own minds gradually, not all at once.

"Mahāmati, it is like this: in a mirror, all the reflections of forms appear all at once, without conceptuality. In the same way, Mahāmati, the Tathāgata purifies all at once the continuum of the appearances of all beings' own minds, domains that are free of conceptuality and without appearance. Mahāmati, it is like this: the orbs of the moon and the sun, with their rays, make manifest all at once everything that appears as form. In the same way, Mahāmati, to beings who are free from the habitual tendencies of the dysfunction belonging to the appearances of their own minds, the Tathāgata truly reveals all at once the domain of the inconceivable wisdom of the Victors, and its sphere of activity. Mahāmati, it is like this: the all-base consciousness displays all at once the body, the abode, and the objects of enjoyment that are the appearances of one's own mind. In the same way, Mahāmati, the buddha of natural outflow (niṣyanda) also ripens all at once the spheres of beings and establishes them as yogins of the yoga of the abode of the palace and the celestial mansion of Akaniṣṭha. Mahāmati, it is like this: the buddha of the nature of reality (dharmatā) shines all at once with the rays of the buddha of natural outflow and of the emanation. In the same way, Mahāmati, the characteristic of the Dharma realized by the individual self-awareness of the noble ones also shines all at once, by turning back the wrong views of existence and nonexistence.

"Furthermore, Mahāmati, the buddha who flows forth in accordance with the nature of reality teaches that, because the attachment to the manifold things that are not the true nature of that illusory person with its many kinds of illusory limbs is not to be found, all phenomena included within particular and general characteristics arise from the cause, namely attachment to the imputed nature, which is bound up with the causal characteristic of the habitual tendencies of the appearances of one's own mind.

"Furthermore, Mahāmati, the characteristic of the arising of the imputed nature comes from attachment to the dependent nature. Mahāmati, it is like this: relying on grass, wood, and pebbles, by means of an illusion spell together with a person who wields it, there appears the form of an illusory person, complete with the limbs of the forms of all beings and bearing manifold forms, a body of a being not established as a single nature, imagined through manifold kinds of conceptuality. Mahāmati, although it appears in that way, it is not of that nature. In the same way, Mahāmati, in the dependent nature, the imputed nature appears as the characteristics of the many and manifold kinds of conceptualizing mind. Mahāmati, through imagining the habitual tendencies of attachment to things as having the characteristic of imagination, it becomes the characteristic of the imputed nature. Mahāmati, this is the teaching of the buddha of natural outflow."

Mahāmati, the buddha of the nature of reality establishes the domain of the noble ones' individual self-realization, which is free from the characteristics of the intrinsic nature of mind. Mahāmati, the buddhas emanated by magical transformation set forth the course of generosity, meditative concentration, moral discipline, meditative absorption, mind, wisdom, gnosis, the aggregates, the elements, the sense sources, the liberations, and the analyzed characteristics of the functioning of consciousness. They also teach the characteristics of the stages of formless absorption, which go beyond the views of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas). Mahāmati, the buddha of the nature of reality is without an object and free from any object. It has turned away from all the characteristics of activity, of the faculties, and of valid cognition. It is taught as not being the domain of fools, of śrāvakas, of pratyekabuddhas, or of non-Buddhists, who cling because they are attached to the characteristic of a self. Therefore, Mahāmati, one should strive for the distinctive characteristic of the noble ones' individual self-realization. You must regard even the appearance of the characteristics of your own mind as something to be turned away from.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, the characteristics that distinguish the approach of the Śrāvaka Vehicle are of two kinds, namely the distinctive characteristic of the noble ones' individual self-realization, and the characteristic of clinging to an intrinsic nature conceptualized as entities.

Mahāmati, what, then, is the śrāvakas' distinctive characteristic of the noble ones' individual self-realization? It is this. Through weariness with the pleasure of objects that are empty, selfless, suffering, and impermanent, and through the cutting off of the continuum, and by fully knowing, just as it truly is, the characteristic of the non-destruction of the aggregates, the elements, the sense sources, and the external in their individual and general characteristics, they place the mind in equipoise. Mahāmati, having placed their own minds in equipoise, the śrāvakas come to realize fully the meditative concentrations, the liberations, the meditative absorptions, the paths, the fruits, the meditative attainments, release, and the abiding in the bliss of the noble ones' individual self-realization, in which inconceivable habitual tendencies are transformed and which is free from death and transmigration. Mahāmati, this is the śrāvakas' characteristic of the noble ones' individual self-realization.

Mahāmati, having understood the bliss of the abode of the śrāvakas' noble individual self-realization, the bodhisattva mahāsattva, because of having long regarded sentient beings with compassion and in order to fulfill the aspirations made in the past, should not actualize the bliss of the gateway of cessation or the bliss of meditative equipoise. Mahāmati, this is the bliss of the śrāvakas' characteristic of noble individual self-realization, and the bodhisattva should not train in that bliss of the characteristic of noble individual self-realization.

Again, Mahāmati, what is the śrāvakas' clinging to an intrinsic nature conceptualized as entities? It is this. Having seen that the great elements, blue, yellow, warmth, moisture, motion, and solidity, arise without a maker, and having seen individual and general characteristics, reasoning, scripture, and valid cognition as closely connected, there arises a conceptuality that clings to their intrinsic nature. Mahāmati, the bodhisattva, having understood this, should reverse it. By entering into the characteristic of the selflessness of phenomena, one turns back the view of the characteristic of the selflessness of persons, and should abide in the progressive linking of the stages of the grounds. Mahāmati, this is the explanation of what was spoken of as the śrāvakas' characteristic of clinging to an intrinsic nature conceptualized as entities.

Then the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati said this to the Blessed One: "The Blessed One has spoken of the domain of the noble ones' individual self-realization as ultimate, permanent, and inconceivable. Blessed One, in just the same way there are also non-Buddhists who say that causes are permanent and inconceivable."

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, the cause of the non-Buddhists does not attain permanence and inconceivability. Why is that? Mahāmati, the permanence and inconceivability of the non-Buddhists are not endowed with the own-characteristic of a cause. Mahāmati, as for one whose permanence and inconceivability are not endowed with the own-characteristic of a cause, by what and in what way could it be shown to be called permanent and inconceivable? Mahāmati, if the doctrine of permanence and inconceivability were endowed with the own-characteristic of a cause, then, since it would have the characteristic of a cause that depends on causes, it would become impermanent; it would not be permanent and inconceivable.

"Mahāmati, my ultimate permanence and inconceivability is endowed with the characteristic of an ultimate cause and is free from existence and nonexistence. It is endowed with a characteristic because it has the characteristic of the noble ones' individual self-realization. It is endowed with a cause because ultimate gnosis has become its cause. Being free from existence and nonexistence, it is permanent, because its qualities resemble the examples of unconditioned space, nirvāṇa, and cessation. Therefore, Mahāmati, this is not in accord with the non-Buddhists' doctrine of permanence and inconceivability.

"Mahāmati, this permanence and inconceivability is the suchness to be realized by the Tathāgatas' noble individual self-gnosis. Therefore, Mahāmati, the bodhisattva mahāsattva should strive in order to understand permanence and inconceivability through noble individual self-gnosis.

"Furthermore, Mahāmati, the permanence and inconceivability of the non-Buddhists are impermanent, because they have arisen from a cause that has the characteristic of impermanence, of nonexistent things. They are not permanent, because they are brought forth by the characteristic of a cause that they themselves have fabricated."

Mahāmati, suppose the permanence and inconceivability of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) were held to be permanent through inference, by seeing permanence in the absence of produced things. Then, Mahāmati, by this very reason my own as well, seen as permanent in the absence of produced things, would be permanent because it is taught as being without a cause.

Mahāmati, suppose the permanence and inconceivability of the tīrthikas were something endowed with a cause and a characteristic. Then, since its cause and its own characteristic do not exist, Mahāmati, that permanence and inconceivability would be like the horn of a hare. Mahāmati, for the tīrthikas it becomes mere verbal conceptualization. Why is that? Because, Mahāmati, the horn of a hare, lacking its own cause and characteristic, is mere verbal conceptualization.

Mahāmati, my permanence and inconceivability is permanent because its cause is the characteristic of the noble ones' individual self-realization, and because it is free from the produced, from things and from non-things. It is not permanent through inference from the absence of external things or from permanence.

Mahāmati, those who hold to permanence because the external does not exist, and to inconceivability by inference from permanence, do not know the cause and the characteristic proper to permanence and inconceivability. Mahāmati, they stand outside the domain realized by the noble ones' individual self-wisdom, and they are not fit to be spoken of.

Moreover, Mahāmati, those who do not know the difference between saṃsāra and nirvāṇa, terrified by the fear that comes from conceptualizing the suffering of saṃsāra, seek nirvāṇa. Mahāmati, they imagine that nirvāṇa is attained through the absence of all conceptualization about things and through the cutting off of the future objects of the sense faculties. Mahāmati, they do not place first the wisdom of individual self-awareness that has become the all-base. Therefore, Mahāmati, those foolish people speak of three vehicles; they do not speak of the non-appearance of conceptualization as mind-only. Mahāmati, in this way they do not recognize the domain of the Tathāgatas of the past, the future and the present, the domain of what appears as one's own mind. Mahāmati, those who cling to external appearances as the domain of mind revolve on the wheel of the destinies of saṃsāra.

Moreover, Mahāmati, the Tathāgatas of the past, the future and the present teach that all phenomena are unborn. Why is that? Because, Mahāmati, what appears as one's own mind is not a thing, and there is no arising from existence or from nonexistence; therefore all things are unborn. Mahāmati, all phenomena are like the horns of a hare, a horse, a donkey or a camel. Mahāmati, all things are unborn, because childish ordinary beings conceive of them as having a falsely imagined nature. Mahāmati, the arising of the characteristic of the intrinsic nature of all things is the domain of the wisdom of the noble ones' individual self-awareness; it is not of the nature of the domain of the dualistic conceptualization of the childish.

Mahāmati, the all-base consciousness, whose characteristic is the nature of the forms of body, enjoyments and abode, operates with the characteristics of grasped and grasper. With regard to it, the childish, whose minds have fallen into the duality of views of arising, abiding and perishing, imagine that all things arise from existence and nonexistence. Mahāmati, you should exert yourself in this.

Moreover, Mahāmati, there are five lineages of realization. What are the five? They are the lineage of realization of the śrāvaka vehicle, the lineage of realization of the pratyekabuddha vehicle, the lineage of realization of the Tathāgata vehicle, the lineage that is not fixed in any of them, and those without a lineage: these five.

Mahāmati, how is the lineage of realization of the śrāvaka vehicle to be recognized? When the teaching that brings understanding through thorough knowledge of the aggregates, the elements, the sense sources, and their specific and general characteristics is taught, the hair of such a person's body stands on end, and his mind enters into the knowledge that makes him thoroughly familiar with characteristics, but not into thorough familiarity with the characteristic of non-difference from dependent origination. This, Mahāmati, is the lineage of realization of the śrāvaka vehicle. Through the view of realization of the śrāvaka vehicle, having abandoned the manifest afflictions on the sixth and fifth grounds, though not having abandoned the afflictions of habitual tendencies, and being subject to inconceivable death and transmigration, they truly roar the lion's roar: "My births are exhausted, I have lived the holy life, what was to be done is done." Saying this and the like, they come to have an understanding that extends from familiarity with the selflessness of the person up to nirvāṇa.

Mahāmati, others seek nirvāṇa by understanding that a self, a sentient being, a life, a nourisher, a man and a person exist. Mahāmati, still others, seeing all things as dependent on causes, come to have the thought of desiring nirvāṇa. Mahāmati, because they do not see the selflessness of phenomena, there is no liberation for them. Mahāmati, this is the one of the lineage of realization of the śrāvaka vehicle, and of the tīrthikas, who has the idea of deliverance where there is no deliverance. Mahāmati, you should strive in this in order to turn back wrong views.

Mahāmati, as for those of the lineage that will realize the vehicle of the pratyekabuddhas: when realization in solitude is taught, they shed tears, their hearts thrill, and the hair on their bodies stands on end. Through the condition of non-association they become attached to things. When the miracle of displaying their own bodies' many and various magical powers, and the twin miracle, are explained, they are delighted. Knowing them to be of the lineage that will realize the vehicle of the pratyekabuddhas, one should give them talk that accords with realizing the vehicle of the pratyekabuddhas. Mahāmati, this is the characteristic of those of the lineage that will realize the vehicle of the pratyekabuddhas.

Mahāmati, the lineage that will realize the vehicle of the Tathāgata is of three kinds: the lineage that will realize the dharma that is devoid of intrinsic nature, the lineage that will realize one's own individual inner attainment, and the lineage that will realize the vast external buddha fields. Mahāmati, when any one of these three is taught, or when the inconceivable domain of body, ālaya, enjoyments and abode, which are appearances of one's own mind, is taught, if one is not afraid, not frightened, and does not become terrified, that one should be known as being of the lineage that will realize the vehicle of the Tathāgata. Mahāmati, this is the characteristic of those of the lineage that will realize the vehicle of the Tathāgata.

Mahāmati, those of undetermined lineage should be guided toward whichever of these three they delight in when these three are taught. Mahāmati, this establishing of the lineages is a ground of purification; it is to be established in order that they may enter the ground of non-appearance. Once the habitual tendencies of their own afflictions in their own individual ālaya have been purified, because they see the selflessness of phenomena, they attain dwelling in the bliss of samādhi, and then the śrāvakas too will fully attain the very embodiment of the Victor's body.

Then the Blessed One spoke these verses:

The fruit of stream-entry,
and likewise that of once-returning,
the fruit of non-returning,
and arhatship as well, are confusions of the mind.

To childish ones of feeble intellect
I speak of three vehicles, of one vehicle,
and even of no vehicle;
for the noble ones there is solitude alone.

The abandonment of the two kinds of cognizance
is the gateway to the ultimate meaning.
Where could the establishing of three vehicles
exist for one who dwells in non-appearance?

The meditative concentrations, the immeasurables,
the formless states and samādhi,
and the cessation of perception, without exception,
do not exist in mind-only.

Mahāmati, how does the icchantikas' not desiring liberation come about? It is through abandoning all roots of virtue, and through making aspirations for the sake of all sentient beings since beginningless time.

What is abandoning all roots of virtue? It is rejecting and denigrating the Bodhisattva Piṭaka by saying, "These are not sūtras and Vinaya that accord with liberation." Because they have abandoned all roots of virtue, they do not pass into nirvāṇa.

Mahāmati, in the second case, the bodhisattva great being has previously made, with regard to existence, the aspiration "Until all sentient beings have passed into parinirvāṇa, I too shall not pass into nirvāṇa," along with skillful means; for that reason he does not pass into nirvāṇa. Mahāmati, this is the specific characteristic of those whose nature is not to pass into parinirvāṇa; by it they come to take up the way of the icchantikas.

Mahāmati asked again, "Blessed One, which of these never passes into nirvāṇa at all?"

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, it is the bodhisattva icchantika who, knowing all phenomena to be in nirvāṇa from the very beginning, never passes into nirvāṇa at all; it is not the icchantika who abandons all roots of virtue. Mahāmati, even the icchantika who abandons all roots of virtue may at some time, through the blessing of the tathāgatas, purify the roots of virtue. Why is that? Mahāmati, because the tathāgatas never forsake any sentient being. Mahāmati, it is for this reason that the bodhisattva icchantikas do not pass into parinirvāṇa.

"Furthermore, Mahāmati, the bodhisattva great being should become skilled in the three characteristics of intrinsic nature. Mahāmati, the imputed nature arises from signs. Mahāmati, how does the imputed nature arise from signs? Mahāmati, the dependent nature appears as though it had the characteristic of the signs of things. Mahāmati, attachment to the characteristic of the signs of things is established by the tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly complete buddhas, as the imputed nature in two forms: the characteristic of attachment to names, and the characteristic of attachment to the signs of things. Mahāmati, the characteristic of attachment to the signs of things is attachment to inner and outer phenomena. Mahāmati, attachment to the characteristic of signs is the full knowing and comprehending of the specific and general characteristics of those very inner and outer phenomena. Mahāmati, that is the twofold characteristic of the imputed nature. That which arises from the basis and object of this is the dependent.

"Mahāmati, what then is the perfected nature? It is suchness, free from conceptualization of signs, names and the characteristics of things, the domain of the noble ones' wisdom of individual self-awareness, reached by attaining the realization of noble wisdom. Mahāmati, this perfected nature is the very heart essence of the tathāgatas."

Then at that time the Blessed One spoke this verse:

"Names, signs, and conceptual thought
Are the characteristic of the two natures;
Right knowledge and suchness
Are the characteristic of the perfected."

Mahāmati, this Dharma discourse called "The Analysis of the Characteristics of the Intrinsic Nature of the Five Dharmas" is the domain of the noble wisdom realized individually within oneself. You and the other bodhisattvas should train in it.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, the bodhisattva mahāsattva should become skilled in analyzing the knowledge of the characteristics of the two kinds of selflessness. Mahāmati, what are the characteristics of the two kinds of selflessness? Because of clinging to the grasping of name, form and so forth, consciousness arises from all the faculties. That is to say, it cognizes the aggregates, elements and sense sources that arise from ignorance, karma and craving, which are without self and without anything belonging to a self, and which one's own mind has imagined as the vessel of the world, the body and the abode, though they are only appearances of one's own mind. Like a river, a seed, a lamp, the wind and clouds, it is distinct from one moment to the next and so is ever separate; restless like monkeys and flies; it roams among impure objects; it is without protector; like fire it is never satisfied; it arises from the cause of the habitual tendencies toward the objects of proliferation from beginningless time. Like the wheel of a waterwheel device, it turns in the wheel of the destinies of saṃsāric existence, and bearing the forms of bodies of many different kinds, it enters and arises like an illusion, a vetāla, or a mechanical puppet. Mahāmati, the knowledge that is skilled in this characteristic is called the knowledge of the selflessness of persons.

Mahāmati, what then is the knowledge of the selflessness of phenomena? It is the realization of the nature of the imputed characteristic of the aggregates, elements and sense sources. Mahāmati, just as the aggregates, elements and sense sources are devoid of a self and the like, being merely a heap of aggregates bound together by the thread of the cause, karma and craving, and arise without any mover through serving as conditions for one another, likewise, Mahāmati, the aggregates are also devoid of particular and general characteristics. Childish beings conceptualize them as differentiated by the conceptuality of unreal imputed characteristics, but the noble ones do not. Mahāmati, the bodhisattva mahāsattva who meditates on all phenomena as devoid of mind, mentation and consciousness, of the five dharmas and of the natures, is skilled in the selflessness of phenomena.

Mahāmati, the bodhisattva mahāsattva who is skilled in the selflessness of phenomena, because he fully realizes the absence of appearances, before long attains the first ground of a bodhisattva. Because he understands the analysis of the characteristics of the grounds, after the Joyous ground he becomes skilled in the nine grounds as well, and attains the Cloud of Dharma. Abiding there, he is accomplished through discerning the domain of the illusion-like nature, and in a great celestial palace like a lotus he sits upon a great king of precious lotuses, richly adorned with many jewels and pearls. Surrounded by children of the Victor who are of like kind, he is consecrated by the empowerment of the hands of the buddhas who come from all the buddhafields, like the son of a universal monarch. Passing beyond the ground of the children of the Buddha, because he understands the Dharma realized individually by the noble ones, through seeing the selflessness of phenomena he becomes a Tathāgata who possesses the Dharma body. Mahāmati, this is the characteristic of the selflessness of phenomena itself. Mahāmati, you and the other bodhisattvas should train in it.

The Noble Descent into Laṅkā, the third fascicle.

Then once again the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati addressed the Blessed One, saying: "Blessed One, please explain to me the characteristics of superimposition and denial, so that I and the other bodhisattvas, with minds that have cast off the bad views of superimposition and denial, may swiftly and fully awaken to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, and having fully awakened, may cast off the views of permanence, superimposition, denial and annihilation, and so never disparage your way of the buddhas."

Then the Blessed One, perceiving that the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati had made his request once more, spoke these verses:

"Superimposition and denial
Do not exist in mind-only.
Those who do not know the mind
That appears as body, enjoyments and abode,
Unskilled, are fully engaged
In superimposition and denial."

Then, in order to show the meaning of these very verses, the Blessed One further declared: "Mahāmati, superimposition of what does not exist is of four kinds. What are the four? They are superimposing a nonexistent characteristic, superimposing a nonexistent view, superimposing a nonexistent cause, and superimposing a nonexistent entity. Mahāmati, these are the four kinds of superimposition.

Mahāmati, what is denial? It is this: when this very bad view of superimposition is not found, then, for lack of discernment, it turns into denial. Mahāmati, these are the characteristics of superimposition and denial.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, what is the characteristic of superimposing a nonexistent characteristic? It is clinging to the particular and general characteristics of the aggregates, elements and sense sources, thinking, 'This is just so, and not otherwise.' Mahāmati, this is the characteristic of superimposing a nonexistent characteristic. Mahāmati, this conceptuality that superimposes a nonexistent characteristic arises from clinging to the various habitual tendencies of the dysfunction of proliferation from beginningless time. Mahāmati, this is the characteristic of superimposing a nonexistent characteristic.

Mahāmati, superimposing a nonexistent view is superimposing on those very aggregates, elements and sense sources the bad view of a self, a sentient being, a life, a nourisher, a man, or a person. Mahāmati, this is called superimposing a nonexistent view."

Mahāmati, superimposing a nonexistent cause is this. A consciousness that was not previously born from a cause later arises, like an illusion, out of what had not come into being before and did not exist, from the eye, form, light as a cause, and mindfulness going before. Having arisen from the nonexistent, it then perishes again. Mahāmati, this is superimposing a nonexistent cause.

Mahāmati, superimposing a nonexistent entity is this: clinging to space, cessation, and nirvāṇa as unconditioned entities, and superimposing them as such. Mahāmati, all these phenomena are beyond entity and non-entity. Like the horns of a hare, a horse, a donkey, or a camel, or like a tangled net of hairs, they are free from the positions of existence and nonexistence. Mahāmati, it is fools, whose minds do not understand that all this is merely the appearance of their own mind, who conceive in terms of superimposition and denial; the noble ones do not. This, Mahāmati, is the characteristic of superimposition and denial that comes from conceiving what is not an entity. Therefore, Mahāmati, one should be free from the views of superimposition and denial.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, bodhisattvas, having realized and fully comprehended the characteristics of mind, mentation, and mental consciousness, of the five dharmas, of the natures, and of the twofold selflessness, take on forms and guises for the benefit of others. Just as the imputed nature rests upon the dependent nature, they are like a wish-fulfilling jewel that takes on all kinds of forms. They go into the maṇḍalas of all the buddha fields, and there, in the presence of all the tathāgatas, they hear directly the teachings of the Dharma: teachings that phenomena are like a dream, an illusion, a mirage, a reflection, and the moon in water, free from all arising and perishing, permanence and annihilation, and teachings that are free from the vehicles of all the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. They attain a hundred thousand samādhis, and go on to attain as many as a hundred thousand koṭis of niyutas of samādhis. Having attained them, devoted to worshipping the buddhas, they travel by means of those samādhis from field to field. In every birth, in the palaces of the gods, and in every abode, they correctly teach the Three Jewels. Taking the form of a buddha and surrounded by assemblies of śrāvakas and bodhisattvas, in order to turn beings back from the positions of existence and nonexistence, they lead them into the mere appearance of their own mind and so show that external entities do not exist.

Then, on that occasion, the Blessed One spoke this verse:

When the Victor's children clearly see
The world as nothing but mind,
Then a body that is an assembly,
Fashioned, yet free of all activity,
Endowed with powers, superknowledges and mastery,
Is what they come to attain.

Then once again the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati made a request to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, please explain to me the characteristics of emptiness, non-arising, nonduality, and the absence of intrinsic nature in all phenomena, by realizing which I and other bodhisattvas will completely abandon conceiving of existence and nonexistence, and will swiftly and fully awaken to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening."

Then the Blessed One spoke these words to the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati: "Mahāmati, then listen very well and keep it in mind, and I shall explain it to you."

"Excellent, Blessed One," said the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati, and he listened to the Blessed One.

The Blessed One said to him: "Mahāmati, this saying 'emptiness, emptiness' is a word of the imputed nature. Mahāmati, it is through clinging to the imputed nature that people speak of emptiness, non-arising, the absence of entities, nonduality, and the absence of intrinsic nature as though they were entities.

"Mahāmati, in brief, emptiness is of seven kinds, namely: the emptiness of characteristics, the emptiness of the intrinsic nature of entities, the emptiness of becoming, the emptiness of non-becoming, the emptiness of the inexpressibility of all phenomena, the great emptiness of the ultimate wisdom of the noble ones, and the emptiness of each of the other. These are the seven.

"Mahāmati, what is the emptiness of characteristics? It is this: all entities are empty of individual and general characteristics. Mahāmati, because in combinations there is no mutual dependence and no distinction to be analyzed, there is no entering into individual and general characteristics. Mahāmati, because self, other, and both do not exist, characteristics do not abide; therefore all entities are said to be empty of characteristics.

"Mahāmati, what is the emptiness of the intrinsic nature of entities? It is this: Mahāmati, because entities do not arise by their own intrinsic nature, all phenomena are empty of the intrinsic nature of entities. Therefore it is called the emptiness of the intrinsic nature of entities.

"Mahāmati, what is the emptiness of becoming? It is this: the aggregates are devoid of a self and of what belongs to a self, and they are born and arise from cause, reasoning, agency, karma, and the full presence of conditions. Therefore it is called the emptiness of becoming.

"Mahāmati, what is the emptiness of non-becoming? It is this: in the aggregates, nirvāṇa has never come to be from the very beginning. Therefore it is called the emptiness of non-becoming.

"Mahāmati, what is the emptiness of the inexpressible? It is this: because the imputed nature is inexpressible, all phenomena are empty in being inexpressible. Therefore it is called the emptiness of the inexpressible.

Mahāmati, what is the great emptiness of the ultimate noble wisdom? It is this: the realization of my own individual noble wisdom is empty of views and of all the habitual tendencies of faults. Therefore it is called the great emptiness of the ultimate noble wisdom.

Mahāmati, what is mutual emptiness? It is this: where something is not, that place is called empty of it. For example, Mahāmati, I have explained that in the mansion of Mṛgāramātā there are no elephants, cattle, sheep and so on, but that it is not empty of monks, nuns and so on. It is called empty of those animals. Yet, Mahāmati, the mansion is not without the being of a mansion, nor are the monks without the nature of monks, nor is it the case that those things, the elephants, cattle, sheep and so on, do not abide somewhere else. Mahāmati, in the same way, the particular and general characteristics of all phenomena are not found in one another. Therefore it is called mutual emptiness.

Mahāmati, these are the seven kinds of emptiness. Mahāmati, this mutual emptiness is the lowest of them all, and you should abandon it completely.

Mahāmati, a thing does not arise of itself, nor does it arise outside the state of meditative absorption, and yet it is not non-arising either. Therefore, with reference to the unborn, to the absence of intrinsic nature, and to what is untenable, it is said that all things lack intrinsic nature. Mahāmati, because the momentary series has no continuity, and because things appear to change into something else, all phenomena lack intrinsic nature. Therefore it is said that all things lack intrinsic nature.

Mahāmati, what is the characteristic of nonduality? It is this: things divided into two, such as shade and sunlight, long and short, or black and white, are not separate and distinct from one another. Mahāmati, in the same way, all phenomena are nondual, like saṃsāra and nirvāṇa. Mahāmati, this is because there is a cause of distinct characteristics. Where nirvāṇa is, there saṃsāra is not; and where saṃsāra is, there too nirvāṇa is not. Therefore it is said that all phenomena are nondual, like saṃsāra and nirvāṇa. Therefore, Mahāmati, you should apply yourself to the characteristics of emptiness, non-arising, nonduality and the lack of intrinsic nature.

Then, at that time, the Blessed One (the Buddha) spoke these verses:

Though I always teach that saṃsāra is like a dream and an illusion,
And teach emptiness, which abandons permanence and annihilation,
Even so,
Karma does not perish.

Nirvāṇa and space,
And the two cessations as well,
The childish imagine as uncompounded;
The noble see them as free of existence and nonexistence.

Then again the Blessed One said this to the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati: Mahāmati, this characteristic of emptiness, non-arising, nonduality and the lack of intrinsic nature is contained within the sūtras of all the buddhas. Whatever sūtra it may be, in all of them this very meaning should be understood. Mahāmati, a sūtra taught in accordance with the dispositions of all sentient beings is misleading in its meaning; it is not a discourse that establishes reality. Mahāmati, it is like a mirage that deceives deer: it makes them fixate on it by fixating on it as water, yet there is no water in it. Mahāmati, in the same way, all the teachings set out in the sūtras serve to gratify the childish in their own conceptual thought; they are not discourses that present noble wisdom. Therefore, Mahāmati, you should follow the meaning and not become attached to the expression of the teaching.

Then the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, said this to the Blessed One: In the course of the sūtras, the Blessed One has taught the tathāgatagarbha. The Blessed One describes it as naturally luminous and pure, and thus pure from the very beginning, endowed with the thirty-two major marks, and present within the bodies of all sentient beings. The Blessed One says that, like a precious jewel of great value wrapped in a soiled cloth, it is wrapped in the cloth of the aggregates, the elements and the sense sources, overwhelmed by attachment, aversion and delusion, and stained by the stain of conceptualization, and yet it is permanent, stable and everlasting. If so, Blessed One, how is this teaching of the tathāgatagarbha not like the self-doctrine of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas)? Blessed One, the non-Buddhists too teach a doctrine of the self, saying, "It is permanent, not an agent, without qualities, all-pervading and imperishable."

The Blessed One said: Mahāmati, my teaching of the tathāgatagarbha is not the same as the self-doctrine of the non-Buddhists. Mahāmati, the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly complete buddhas, teach the meanings of such words as emptiness, the limit of reality, nirvāṇa, the unborn, the signless and the wishless as the tathāgatagarbha. Then, so that the childish may avoid the state of being terrified by selflessness, they teach, through the doorway of the tathāgatagarbha, the nonconceptual state, the domain free of appearances. Mahāmati, bodhisattva great beings of the future and the present should not cling to this as a self.

Mahāmati, it is like a potter who, from a single mass of clay particles, makes vessels of many different kinds with his hands, his craft, a rod, water, string and diligent effort.

Mahāmati, in the same way the tathāgatas also teach that very selflessness of phenomena, which is the turning away from all the characteristics of conceptual thought. They do so with many kinds of wisdom and skill in means, whether by teaching it as the tathāgatagarbha or by teaching it as selflessness. Like the potter, they teach with many kinds of words and many forms of expression. Thus, for that reason, Mahāmati, the teaching of the tathāgatagarbha is not like the teaching of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) who proclaim a self.

Mahāmati, the tathāgatas teach the tathāgatagarbha in order to draw in the non-Buddhists who cling to proclaiming a self. One may ask how those whose minds have fallen into the view that imagines a false self might come to have minds that abide in the sphere of the three gateways to liberation, and how they might quickly awaken to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. Mahāmati, it is for that purpose that the tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly complete buddhas, teach the tathāgatagarbha. Therefore this is not the same as the non-Buddhists' proclamation of a self. Mahāmati, therefore, in order to reverse the views of the non-Buddhists, one should follow the tathāgatagarbha in accordance with selflessness.

Then, at that time, the Blessed One spoke these verses:

The person, the continuum, the aggregates,
likewise conditions and atoms,
the Principal, the Lord, the Creator:
these are imagined in what is mind alone.

Then the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, looking toward the multitude of people of the future, again made a request to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, please explain to me the realization of yoga by which bodhisattva great beings become yogins of the great yoga."

The Blessed One declared: "Mahāmati, bodhisattva great beings who possess four qualities become yogins of the great yoga. If you ask what the four are, they are these: contemplating appearances as one's own mind; abandoning the views of arising, abiding and perishing; realizing that external things are without existence; and aspiring to realize the personal wisdom of the noble ones. Mahāmati, bodhisattva great beings who possess these four qualities become yogins of the great yoga.

"Mahāmati, how is a bodhisattva great being skilled in contemplating appearances as his own mind? He reflects in this way: 'These three realms are only my own mind. They are free of self and what belongs to a self, unmoving, and free of taking up and casting away. They are imbued with clinging to the habitual tendencies of the corrupting dispositions of conceptual proliferation from beginningless time. They are bound up with the experience of the many kinds of forms of the three realms. They appear as bodies, possessions, abodes and destinies, following conceptual thought.' Mahāmati, in this way a bodhisattva great being is skilled in contemplating appearances as his own mind.

"Mahāmati, how does a bodhisattva great being abandon the views of arising, abiding and perishing? It is like this. All things are like an illusion, like forms arising in a dream. Because they do not come from self, from other, or from both, they do not arise. By following mere appearances of his own mind, and seeing that external things do not exist, the consciousnesses no longer engage. He sees that conditions too are not heaped up or accumulated, and he sees that the three realms arise from the condition of conceptual thought. Because he does not apprehend any inner or outer phenomena and sees that they lack intrinsic nature, the view of arising turns away. Realizing that phenomena have the nature of an illusion and so forth, he attains forbearance toward the non-arising of phenomena. Abiding on the eighth ground, he comprehends the transformation of his understanding of mind, mentation, mental consciousness, the five dharmas, the natures, and the two kinds of selflessness, and for that reason he also attains the mind-made body."

"Blessed One, why is it called a mind-made body?"

The Blessed One declared: "Mahāmati, it is called a mind-made body because, like the mind, it is unobstructed and moves swiftly. Mahāmati, it is like this: the mind, unobstructed by mountains, walls, rivers, trees and the like, travels many hundreds of thousands of leagues, recalling objects it has seen and experienced before; the body of one's own unbroken mind-stream moves with unobstructed motion. Mahāmati, in the same way, as soon as the mind-made body is attained, through the illusion-like samādhi it is in full flower with the powers, the masteries, the superknowledges, wisdom and the marks; it is born together with the class of the noble ones; it moves without obstruction; and, recalling the objects of its former aspirations, it goes forth like the mind in order to bring sentient beings to maturity. In this way, Mahāmati, a bodhisattva great being has abandoned the views of arising, abiding and perishing.

"Mahāmati, how is a bodhisattva great being skilled in realizing that external things are without existence? Mahāmati, it is like this. All things are like a mirage, a dream, or a tangle of hairs. All things arise from the cause of clinging to the many kinds of habitual tendencies of conceptual thought, the corrupting dispositions of conceptual proliferation from beginningless time. Seeing correctly that they are without intrinsic nature, he aspires to the domain realized by the personal wisdom of the noble ones. Mahāmati, a bodhisattva great being who possesses these four qualities is a yogin of the great yoga."

"Mahāmati, you should strive in that."

Then once again the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, made a request to the Blessed One (the Buddha): "Blessed One, please explain to me the characteristics of the causes and conditions of all phenomena. By understanding these characteristics of causes and conditions, I and the other bodhisattvas would be free from the conceptuality of views that things exist or do not exist, and would not conceive of any thing as arising either gradually or all at once."

The Blessed One said: "Mahāmati, the characteristic of the dependent origination of all phenomena is of two kinds: external and internal. Mahāmati, as for external dependent origination, a pot comes about through conditions such as a lump of clay, a stick, a wheel, string, water, and human effort. Mahāmati, just as a pot comes about from a lump of clay, so too cloth comes from threads, a mat from grass, a sprout from a seed, and butter from curd through human effort with a churning stick and the like. This, Mahāmati, is external dependent origination, in which each later thing comes about from the one before it.

"Mahāmati, as for internal dependent origination, it is this: ignorance, craving, karma, and phenomena such as these receive the name 'dependent origination.' Mahāmati, the phenomena called aggregates, elements, and sense sources that are born from these also receive the name 'dependent origination.' Although these are without distinction, the childish imagine them.

"Mahāmati, causes are of six kinds: the cause of what is to come about, the connecting cause, the characteristic cause, the agent cause, the manifesting cause, and the cause of dependence. These are the six.

"Mahāmati, the cause of what is to come about performs the function of a cause for the arising of outer and inner phenomena. Mahāmati, the connecting cause performs the function of an objective support for the coming about of outer and inner aggregates, seeds, and so on. Mahāmati, the characteristic cause produces a connection with the characteristic of immediately succeeding activity. Mahāmati, the agent cause performs a function of sovereign power, like a wheel-turning monarch. Mahāmati, the manifesting cause performs the function of making manifest the characteristic of a conceptualized thing once it has arisen, like a lamp for forms and the like. Mahāmati, the cause of dependence, at the time of cessation, keeps the activity of the continuum from being cut off, for the sake of the coming about of a nonconceptual thing.

"These, Mahāmati, are imagined by childish ordinary beings through the conceptuality of their own minds. They do not come about by gradual progression, nor do they come about all at once. Why is that? Mahāmati, if they came about all at once, then, because the characteristic of a cause would not be found, there would be no distinction between action and agent. If they came about by gradual progression, then gradual progression could not come about from a nature whose characteristic is not found. Mahāmati, just like designating someone a father when no son has been born, the logicians' mode of connection through gradual progression is also untenable. Mahāmati, they are not born by gradual progression in the manner of producer and produced through the conditions such as the causal condition, the objective condition, the immediately preceding condition, and the dominant condition. Mahāmati, because of the characteristic of clinging to the imputed nature, they are born neither gradually nor all at once. Mahāmati, because beings have bodies and enjoyments that are appearances of their own mind, and because the specific and general characteristics are without external things, they are born neither gradually nor all at once. Rather, consciousness comes about because things are imagined by the conceptuality of what appears to one's own mind. Therefore, Mahāmati, you should be free of the view that the characteristic of possessing causes, conditions, and agency is gradual or all at once."

On this it is said:

Here nothing at all is born
Through conditions, nor does anything cease;
It is only imputed conditions
That arise and come to cease.

Arising and perishing bound up with conditions
Are not to be rejected;
What the childish imagine through conditions,
That is what is to be rejected.

Because the mind, deluded by habitual tendencies,
Appears within the three realms of existence,
Phenomena do not come about
From conditions, whether existent or nonexistent.

What has not come about is not born through conditions,
Nor does anything come to cease.
When conditioned things are seen
As sky-flowers and a barren woman's son,
Then grasped object and grasping subject too
Are seen as error, and the mind turns back.

The unborn is not something to be produced;
Conditions, whatever they may be,
Exist nowhere and at no time;
They are spoken of as mere conventions.

Then once again the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, please explain to me the Dharma discourse called 'the essence of the characteristics of the conceptuality of speech.' Through thoroughly understanding the distinctions among its various divisions, I and the other bodhisattva great beings would comprehend both meanings, the expressed and the inexpressible, and would quickly awaken fully into unsurpassed, perfect, complete enlightenment; and the understanding of all sentient beings regarding both meanings, the expressed and the inexpressible, would be purified."

The Blessed One said: "Mahāmati, then listen very well and keep it in mind, and I shall explain it to you."

Saying "Excellent, Blessed One," the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, listened to the Blessed One. The Blessed One then spoke to him as follows: "Mahāmati, there are four kinds of characteristics of the conceptuality of speech: the speech of characteristics, the speech of dreams, the speech of clinging to negative tendencies (dauṣṭhulya), and the speech of beginningless conceptuality."

Mahāmati, among these, the speech of characteristics arises from clinging, as to signs, to the forms that one's own conceptuality has fashioned. Mahāmati, the speech of dreams arises from recollecting objects experienced before, and from the absence of the object once one has awakened. Again, Mahāmati, the speech of the conceptuality of negative tendencies arises from recollecting actions an enemy did in the past. Mahāmati, the speech of conceptuality from beginningless time arises from one's own seeds, the negative tendencies of clinging to the conceptual proliferation of beginningless time. These, Mahāmati, are the four kinds of characteristics of verbal conceptuality. This is the further explanation of what was stated.

Then once more the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, made a request to the Blessed One on this very matter: "Blessed One, may the Blessed One please explain to me further the sphere in which verbal conceptuality clings: where, from what, how, and by what the speech of human beings, verbal expression and conceptuality, arises."

The Blessed One said: "Mahāmati, speech arises from the coming together of the head, the chest, the nose, the throat, the palate, the lips, the tongue, and the teeth."

Mahāmati asked: "Blessed One, are conceptuality and speech different, or are they one, or how is it?"

The Blessed One said: "Mahāmati, speech is neither different from conceptuality nor one with it. Why is that? It is because verbal conceptuality arises with the characteristic of having arisen from that as its cause. Mahāmati, if this speech were different from conceptuality, it would not have arisen from conceptuality as its cause. If it were one with it, speech would not illuminate meaning; but it does illuminate it. Therefore it is neither different nor one."

Again Mahāmati asked: "Blessed One, is speech itself the ultimate? Or is what speech expresses the ultimate, or how is it?"

The Blessed One said: "Mahāmati, speech is not the ultimate, nor is what speech expresses the ultimate. Why is that? It is because the ultimate, the noble wisdom that is the ultimate truth, is entered by way of verbal expression. Speech itself is therefore not the ultimate. Mahāmati, the ultimate is to be realized by the wisdom of the noble ones' individual self-awareness. It is not the sphere of a mind of verbal conceptuality. Therefore conceptuality does not express the ultimate. Mahāmati, speech arises and perishes, is unstable, and arises from mutual causes and conditions. Mahāmati, whatever arises from mutual causes and conditions does not express the ultimate. Mahāmati, because there is no characteristic of self or other, speech does not express characteristics. Moreover, Mahāmati, because conceptuality follows what is merely the appearance of one's own mind, and because diverse and various external things have no characteristics, conceptuality does not conceive the ultimate. Therefore, Mahāmati, you should be free of conceptuality about the various kinds of speech."

On this it is said:

No thing at all has intrinsic nature;
Speech likewise does not exist.
Emptiness, the meaning of emptiness,
Fools do not see, and so they run about.
All things lack intrinsic nature;
Human speech too is imagined,
And that also does not exist.
One who examines their essence
As like a dream or an emanation
Neither circles in saṃsāra nor passes into nirvāṇa.
Just as kings and merchants
Lure their sons with various clay animals,
Set them to play,
And then give them real animals,
So I too, with reflections
Of the various characteristics of phenomena,
Teach my sons
The true limit of individual self-awareness.

Then again the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, please explain to me the characteristic that distinguishes the realization that all phenomena are the sphere of the appearance of one's own mind: that which has abandoned existence, nonexistence, oneness, difference, both, neither, neither existence nor nonexistence, permanence and impermanence; in which the conceptions of non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) do not operate; which is to be known by the individual wisdom of the noble ones; which is turned away from the specific and general characteristics of the imputed; which enters suchness, the ultimate; whose characteristic is to join the grounds and to become purer and purer, higher and higher; whose characteristic is to follow on into the ground of a Tathāgata; which, through former aspirations, operates like a jewel of various forms; and which ranges spontaneously through the characteristics of limitless domains. Please explain these and the like to me, so that I and the other bodhisattva great beings may come to hold a view turned away from the specific and general characteristics of the imputed nature, may swiftly awaken fully to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, and may bring to perfection all the excellent happiness of all sentient beings."

The Blessed One said: "Excellent, Mahāmati, excellent! Mahāmati, in thinking to ask about this matter you have set out for the benefit of many beings, for the happiness of many beings, out of compassion for the world, for the sake of the multitude of beings, and for the benefit and happiness of gods and humans. Mahāmati, you have done well. Therefore, Mahāmati, listen very well and keep it in mind, and I shall explain it to you."

Saying, "Excellent, Blessed One," the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, listened to the Blessed One.

The Blessed One then spoke to him as follows: "Mahāmati, childish ordinary people do not understand that things are merely the appearance of their own mind, and they cling to the various kinds of external things. Clinging in this way, they cling to conceptuality, the cause of habitual tendencies, whose nature is existence, nonexistence, oneness, difference, both, neither, neither existence nor nonexistence, permanence and impermanence, and so they conceptualize."

Mahāmati, it is like this. Deer tormented by the spring sun take the water of a mirage to be real water and run toward it, wanting to drink. Because they do not understand that the error lies in their own mind's view, they do not even know that there is no water here. In the same way, Mahāmati, childish ordinary beings, too, have intellects imbued from beginningless time with conceptualization of elaborations of many kinds. Their minds are thoroughly tormented by the fire of attachment, aversion and delusion. They are intent on objects of many different forms, they are disposed to view things as arising, perishing and abiding, and they are unskilled regarding inner and outer things. They fall into grasping at oneness, difference, existence and nonexistence.

Mahāmati, it is like this. A city of the gandharvas, the celestial musicians, is not a city, yet those who are unskilled perceive a city there. That likeness of a city appears because, since beginningless time, they have clung to the habitual tendencies that are the seeds of a city. Mahāmati, that city is neither a city nor is it not a city. In the same way, Mahāmati, the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), whose intellects do not understand that all this is mere appearance of their own mind, and who since beginningless time have clung to the habitual tendencies of elaboration, become attached to doctrines of oneness, difference, existence and nonexistence.

Mahāmati, it is like this. A certain person, asleep, dreams that he has gone to a land, a city of jewels, beautified with women, men, elephants, horses, chariots, foot soldiers, villages, towns, cities, cattle, buffaloes, forests, pleasure gardens, mountains, rivers and ponds of many kinds. Then he wakes, and once awake he recalls that land, that city of jewels. Mahāmati, what do you think of that? Is the man who recalls the many unreal things of his dream in this way someone of a wise nature?

Mahāmati replied, "No, Blessed One."

The Blessed One said: "In the same way, Mahāmati, childish ordinary beings who are in the grip of wrong views, whose intellects are those of the non-Buddhists, and who do not understand that the things that appear from their own mind are like a dream, rely on views of oneness, difference, existence and nonexistence.

"Mahāmati, it is like this. A painted surface has no high or low, yet the childish imagine high and low in it. In the same way, Mahāmati, in times to come there will be people whose dispositions carry the habitual tendencies of the wrong views of the non-Buddhists and whose conceptuality has grown very great. They will cling to doctrines of oneness, difference, both and neither, and so ruin themselves. Of those who teach non-arising, who are free from the positions of existence and nonexistence, they will say that they are nihilists. They will say, 'These people deny cause and effect. With their wrong views they have uprooted the cause of the pure white side of virtue. Those who wish for what is good should keep far away from them.' Their dispositions have fallen into views of self, of other and of both. Having imagined existence and nonexistence, their dispositions have fallen into the wrong views of superimposition and denial. Such beings are headed for hell.

"Mahāmati, it is like this. Those with diseased eyes see a net of hairs and say, 'This is wonderful! This is truly wonderful! O friends, look!' Yet that net of hairs, because it is both seen and not seen, is neither truly existent nor nonexistent. In the same way, Mahāmati, those who cling to dispositions of conceptuality rooted in the wrong views of the non-Buddhists, who cling to the positions of existence and nonexistence and to doctrines of oneness, difference, both and neither, and who deny the true Dharma, make both themselves and others fall into error.

"Mahāmati, it is like this. Although a whirling firebrand is not a wheel, the childish imagine its wheel to be a real wheel, but not the wise. In the same way, Mahāmati, those who have fallen into the dispositions of the non-Buddhists with their wrong views imagine every birth into existence as oneness, difference, both or neither.

"Mahāmati, it is like this. When rain falls, bubbles arise from the water that look like crystal gems. The childish cling to them as real crystal gems and run after them. Yet, Mahāmati, because those water bubbles are both graspable and not graspable, they are neither gems nor are they not gems. In the same way, Mahāmati, those imbued with the habitual tendencies of dispositions of conceptual views of the non-Buddhists will come to proclaim that, through conditions, arising takes place from what does not exist, and that perishing takes place from what exists.

"Furthermore, Mahāmati, they will set up the components of the three means of valid cognition one by one, and then, concerning that which is to be known by the noble ones' wisdom, realized individually within oneself, and which is free from the two natures, they will imagine, 'It exists as the intrinsic nature of a thing.' Mahāmati, yogins whose basis of mind, mentation and mental consciousness has been transformed, who have completely abandoned conceptualizations of grasped and grasper, which are appearances of their own mind, and who have attained the ground of the Tathāgata, the wisdom that the noble ones realize individually within themselves, do not have the perception of existence or nonexistence. Mahāmati, if yogins whose domain is of this kind were ever to engage in grasping at existence and nonexistence, that very thing would for them be grasping at a self, grasping at a sustainer, grasping at a cosmic man, and grasping at a person.

"Mahāmati, this teaching of the intrinsic nature of things and of their specific and general characteristics, Mahāmati, is taught by the emanated buddha. It is not taught by the buddha of the nature of reality."

Mahāmati, the teaching arises from views that are present in the dispositions of the childish. It does not speak of the Dharma whose nature is the realization of what is established, the abiding in the bliss of the samādhi of noble wisdom known individually by oneself.

Mahāmati, it is like this. The shadow of a tree appears in water. Because it has the shape of a tree and yet has no shape, it is neither a shadow nor not a shadow. In the same way, Mahāmati, those whose conceptualizations are perfumed by the habitual tendencies of the views of non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), whose minds do not understand that all this is merely the appearance of their own mind, conceive of things as one, as different, as both, as neither, as existent, and as nonexistent.

Mahāmati, it is like this. In a mirror, reflections of all forms appear in accordance with conditions. Those reflections are one's own conceptualization. Because they are seen as form and as not form, they are neither form nor not form. Yet, Mahāmati, to the childish, the conceptualization that is the appearance of their own mind appears as though it were form. In the same way, Mahāmati, the reflection of one's own mind also appears in the aspect of the views of one, different, both, and neither.

Mahāmati, it is like this. An echo arises from the meeting of a person, flowing water, and wind, and it resounds separately. Because it resounds as sound and as not sound, it is neither an entity nor a nonentity. In the same way, Mahāmati, the views of existence, nonexistence, one, different, both, and neither appear as conceptualizations that are the habitual tendencies of one's own mind.

Mahāmati, it is like this. On ground where there is no grass, no thicket, and no spreading creepers, when the sun strikes it, the waves of a mirage shimmer. Because they are craved and not craved, they are neither entities nor nonentities. In the same way, Mahāmati, the conceptualizing consciousness of the childish, perfumed by the habitual tendencies of the dysfunction of proliferation from beginningless time, ripples like the waves of a mirage, in the manner of arising, ceasing, abiding, one, different, both, neither, existent, nonexistent, and even the entity of the noble one's individual wisdom.

Mahāmati, it is like this. A vetāla and a mechanical man, though there is no sentient being in them, perform the activity of moving when they are joined with a spirit and a contrivance. Because they go and do not go, the childish cling to that conceptualization of what does not exist. In the same way, Mahāmati, childish ordinary beings who have fallen into the thinking of non-Buddhists with wrong views cling to assertions of one and different. That is an unreal superimposition.

Therefore, Mahāmati, you should become free of conceptualizations of arising, non-arising, ceasing, abiding, one, different, both, neither, existent, nonexistent, and of an entity to be realized by the noble one individually.

On this it is said:

The aggregates, five with consciousness,
Are like the shadow of a tree in water.
Appearances resemble illusions and dreams;
Do not conceive them through cognition.

These three realms of existence are like a dream,
Like a tangle of hairs, and like an illusion.
The error, like the water of a mirage,
When thoroughly contemplated, one is thoroughly freed.

The mirage that deceives the mind:
Just as, when it shimmers in spring,
Deer take it to be water,
Though no entity is there,

So too the seed of consciousness
Stirs toward the objects of views,
And the childish take it as arising,
Like the floaters seen by one with cataracts.

What the childish, grasping at things,
Have embraced from beginningless saṃsāra,
Just as one nail drives out another,
Draw out that grasping and turn it back.

Seeing beings as like illusions, vetālas, machines,
Dreams, lightning, and clouds,
With the three continua forever cut off,
One is fully liberated.

Here there is no cognition whatsoever;
It is like a mirage in the sky.
If one fully knows phenomena thus,
There is nothing at all to assert.

This cognition is a mere name;
It does not exist by its characteristic.
The aggregates upon which these are imputed
Are like a tangle of hairs.

A painting, a tangle of hairs,
An illusion, a dream, a city of gandharvas,
A firebrand wheel, and mirages,
Though nonexistent, appear vividly to people.

Likewise permanent and impermanent,
One, both, and neither:
Through their bond with beginningless faults,
The deluded childish conceive of these.

In eyes, in mirrors, and in waters,
In vessels and in jewels as well,
Reflections appear in them,
Yet the reflection exists nowhere.

The mind appearing as things is just so:
Like a mirage in various forms,
Just as it appears in the sky,
Like a dream, and like a barren woman's son.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, the Dharma teaching of the Tathāgatas is free from the four. That is, it has abandoned the positions of one, different, both, and neither, and it is free from existence and nonexistence, from superimposition and denial. Mahāmati, the Dharma teaching of the Tathāgatas puts first truth, dependent origination, the path, cessation, and the entry into liberation. Mahāmati, the Dharma teaching of the Tathāgatas is not connected with primal nature (prakṛti), the Lord (Īśvara), causelessness, chance, atoms, time, or essential nature.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, in order to purify the two obscurations, the afflictive and the cognitive, they gradually establish, like a caravan leader, the hundred and eight nonappearances and the characteristics that thoroughly distinguish the vehicles and the limbs of the grounds.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, there are four kinds of meditative concentration. If one asks what the four kinds are, they are these: the meditative concentration practiced by the childish, the meditative concentration that discerns meanings, the meditative concentration that takes suchness as its object, and the meditative concentration of the Tathāgatas. These are the four.

Mahāmati, what is the meditation practiced by the immature? It is this. Yogins on the yoga of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas begin by clinging to the selflessness of the person, and to the characteristics of things: their own and general forms, the skeleton, impermanence, suffering, and unattractiveness. Having seen, "This has such a characteristic and not otherwise," they progress from the very beginning, higher and higher, up to the cessation of perception. This is the meditation practiced by the immature.

Mahāmati, what is the meditation that analyzes meaning? It is this. One sets aside the selflessness of the person and own and general characteristics, and sees that the self, the other, and both, as the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) hold them, do not exist. One then proceeds in order, analyzing the meaning of the characteristics of the grounds of the selflessness of phenomena. This is the meditation that analyzes meaning.

Mahāmati, what is the meditation focused on suchness? It is this. One abides, just as things truly are, in non-conceptualization regarding the two selflessnesses, which are themselves imputed. Because of this, conceptual thought does not arise, and so it is called the meditation focused on suchness.

Mahāmati, what is the meditation of the Tathāgata? It is this. One enters the condition of the ground of the Tathāgata and abides in the bliss of the threefold characteristic of the wisdom that the noble ones realize individually within themselves. Because one thereby accomplishes the inconceivable benefit of sentient beings, it is called the meditation of the Tathāgata.

On this it is said:

The meditation that analyzes meaning,
the meditation practiced by the immature,
the meditation focused on suchness,
and the Tathāgata's virtuous meditation:
when a yogin strives at these,
he sees shapes like the sun and moon,
things like light and lotuses,
like drawings, and like fire in the sky.
These various signs
lead him onto the path of the tīrthikas,
and make him fall as well
into the domain of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas.
When all these are wholly abandoned
and he has become free of appearances,
then the divine hands of the buddhas,
coming forth from every field,
stroke his head:
the sign of following what is real.

Then the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati again spoke to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, people say 'nirvāṇa, nirvāṇa.' Blessed One, of what phenomenon is this word 'nirvāṇa' a designation?"

The Blessed One said: "The transformation of the ālaya, which is the habitual tendency of the intrinsic nature of all consciousnesses, together with the transformation of the habitual tendencies of the views of the mind and the mental consciousness, is the mode of nirvāṇa. It is the domain of the reality that is empty of intrinsic nature. The buddhas and I declare this to be nirvāṇa.

"Further, Mahāmati, nirvāṇa is the domain of the wisdom that the noble ones know individually within themselves. It is free from conceptualizing things as permanent or annihilated, and free from conceptualizing them as existent or nonexistent. How is it not permanent? Because conceptualization in terms of own and general characteristics has been abandoned, it is not permanent. And it is not annihilation, because all the noble ones of the past, future, and present realize it individually within themselves. That is why it is not annihilation.

"Further, Mahāmati, complete nirvāṇa is not destruction, and it is not death. Mahāmati, if complete nirvāṇa were a death, it would still lead to a continuum of rebirth. If it were a destruction, it would fall into the characteristics of the conditioned. Therefore, Mahāmati, complete nirvāṇa is not destruction and not death. Yogins realize a refuge free of dying and transmigrating.

"Further, Mahāmati, nirvāṇa is called nirvāṇa because there is nothing in it to be abandoned and nothing to be attained, and because it is neither annihilation nor permanence, neither one in meaning nor different in meaning.

"Further, Mahāmati, the nirvāṇa of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas is this. They understand own and general characteristics, are free of bustle, and see objects without distortion, and so conceptual thought does not arise. Therefore they come to think of that as nirvāṇa.

"Further, Mahāmati, there are two kinds of characteristic of the two intrinsic natures. If one asks what the two kinds are, they are clinging to the intrinsic nature of expression and clinging to the intrinsic nature of things. Mahāmati, clinging to the intrinsic nature of expression arises from clinging to the habitual tendencies of verbal proliferation since beginningless time. Mahāmati, clinging to the intrinsic nature of things arises from not understanding that it is merely the appearance of one's own mind.

"Further, Mahāmati, bodhisattvas who are blessed by two blessings bow their heads at the feet of the tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly complete buddhas, and ask them their questions. If one asks by what two blessings they are blessed, they are these: the blessing of entering into samādhi, and the blessing of empowerment by the body, the face, and the hands.

"Mahāmati, from the first ground onward, the bodhisattva mahāsattvas are blessed with the blessing of the buddhas and enter the bodhisattva samādhi called Illumination of the Great Vehicle. As soon as the bodhisattva mahāsattvas have entered the bodhisattva samādhi Illumination of the Great Vehicle, the tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly complete buddhas dwelling in the world systems of the ten directions show their faces and fully manifest body, face, and speech. In this way, Mahāmati, they bestow their blessing, just as they did for the bodhisattva mahāsattva Vajragarbha and for the other bodhisattva mahāsattvas endowed with qualities of this kind.

Mahāmati, in this way, on the first ground, bodhisattva great beings obtain the blessing of entering into meditative absorption. Through the roots of virtue they have amassed over a hundred thousand eons, they gradually come to understand the characteristics of what is favorable to the grounds and what is opposed to them. Then, on the bodhisattva ground called Cloud of Dharma, a bodhisattva great being is seated on a seat in a great lotus palace, wearing a crown adorned with every jewel ornament, and surrounded by bodhisattva great beings of the same kind. The hands of the Victors come forth from the worlds of the ten directions, hands that resemble orpiment, gold, the campaka flower, the rays of the moon and the lotus, and they anoint that bodhisattva great being seated on his seat in the great lotus palace on the crown of his head, as one anoints a sovereign, a wheel-turning king, or Indra, king of the gods. Because they confer empowerment with their bodies, faces and all their hands, that bodhisattva and those bodhisattvas are said to be blessed by the blessing of the empowerment of the hand.

Mahāmati, these are the two blessings of bodhisattva great beings. Blessed by these two blessings, bodhisattva great beings behold the faces of all the buddhas. Were it otherwise, they would be unable to see the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly complete buddhas.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, whatever eloquence bodhisattva great beings display in meditative absorption, in miraculous powers and in the forms of teaching, all of it is blessed by the two blessings of the buddhas. Mahāmati, if the eloquence of bodhisattva great beings were to arise without blessing, then, Mahāmati, even foolish ordinary beings would come to have eloquence. Why is that? It is because they are not blessed.

Mahāmati, when even grass, shrubs, creepers, trees, mountains, musical instruments of every kind, towns, houses, dwellings and palaces give forth sound through the blessing of the Tathāgata's coming, Mahāmati, what need is there to speak of those who have minds? Mahāmati, even the mute, the deaf and the blind are freed from their defects. Mahāmati, so great are the special qualities of the Tathāgata's blessing.

Mahāmati again asked, "Blessed One, why do the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly complete buddhas bestow blessing upon bodhisattva great beings at the time when they abide in meditative absorption, and the blessing of empowerment on the distinguished ground?"

The Blessed One said, "So that they will not be disturbed by the works of Māra and by the afflictions; so that they will not fall into the meditative concentration and the ground of the śrāvakas; and in order to increase the Tathāgata ground, which is to be known personally by oneself, the Dharma that is attained, and realization. For these reasons, Mahāmati, the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly complete buddhas bless bodhisattva great beings with their blessing. Mahāmati, bodhisattva great beings who are not blessed fall into the outlook of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), of the śrāvakas and of Māra, and do not fully awaken to unsurpassed, perfectly complete awakening. That is why bodhisattva great beings are taken under the care of the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly complete buddhas."

On this it is said:

From the blessing of the Lord of Men
Come empowerment, absorptions and the rest;
Purified by aspirations,
They arise on the first and on the tenth.
