"Moreover, Mahāmati, a bodhisattva great being who is skilled in word and meaning rightly sees that word and meaning, or meaning and word, are neither one nor different. Mahāmati, if word and meaning were different, the word could not be the cause that makes the meaning clear. Yet the word gives access to that meaning, as a lamp does to one's valuables. Mahāmati, it is like a certain person who takes up a lamp and looks at his valuables, saying, 'Here in this place is my wealth, of such a kind.' Just so, Mahāmati, by the lamp of the word, which is conceptualized in speech, bodhisattva great beings gain access to the object of self-realized meaning, which is free from the conceptuality of words.

"Moreover, Mahāmati, consider non-arising, non-cessation, being by nature completely in nirvāṇa, the three vehicles, the one vehicle, the five dharmas, mind, intrinsic nature and the rest. Some cling to these on the basis of clinging to the words just as they are. Because they cling in this way, they fall into views of superimposition and denial, like the conceptuality that sees various illusions. For they conceptualize in one way what abides in another. Mahāmati, various illusions are seen as other than they are. In the same way the childish conceptualize things as other than they are, but the noble ones do not."

On this it is said:

Having conceived of words just as they are,
They superimpose even upon the nature of reality.
Because they superimpose upon it,
Those beings fall into the abodes of hell.

There is no self in the aggregates,
Nor are the aggregates in the self.
They do not exist as they are conceived,
Yet neither are they nonexistent.

The childish conceive
That all things exist.
If things existed just as they are seen,
Then everyone would see the truth.

Since all phenomena are nonexistent,
There is neither affliction nor purity.
They do not exist as they are seen,
Yet neither are they nonexistent.

"Moreover, Mahāmati, I shall explain to you the characteristics of wisdom and consciousness. By thoroughly understanding their distinct aspects, you and the other bodhisattva great beings will comprehend the characteristics of wisdom and consciousness, and you will quickly and fully awaken to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.

"Now, Mahāmati, wisdom is of three kinds: worldly, supramundane, and highest supramundane. Among these, what arises and then perishes is consciousness. What neither arises nor perishes is wisdom.

"Moreover, Mahāmati, consciousness falls into signs and signlessness. It falls into existence and nonexistence, and it is born from the cause of various characteristics. The characteristic that wholly transcends signs and signlessness is wisdom.

"Moreover, Mahāmati, the characteristic of accumulating is consciousness, and the characteristic of diminishing is wisdom.

"Now, the three kinds are these: the kind that understands particular and general characteristics, the kind that understands arising and perishing, and the kind that understands non-arising and non-cessation.

"Worldly wisdom belongs to all tīrthikas and all childish ordinary beings, who cling to the positions of existence and nonexistence.

"Supramundane wisdom belongs to all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, who cling to an outlook that falls into particular and general characteristics.

"The highest supramundane wisdom is that of the buddhas and bodhisattvas. It thoroughly discerns the dharma that is without appearance. It sees non-arising and non-cessation, and it is free from the positions of existence and nonexistence. It arises because they realize the selflessness of the ground of the Tathāgata.

"Moreover, Mahāmati, the characteristic of non-attachment is wisdom, and the characteristic of attachment to various objects is consciousness.

"Moreover, Mahāmati, the characteristic of arising through the activity in which three things come together is consciousness. The characteristic of an essential nature that does not arise in this way is wisdom.

"Moreover, Mahāmati, the characteristic of non-attainment is wisdom. It is the domain realized by one's own individual noble wisdom, and so it neither enters nor emerges, like the moon in water."

On this it is said:

By the mind, karmas are accumulated;
By gnosis they are analyzed;
And by wisdom the imageless
And powers are fully attained.

The mind is bound up with objects;
Gnosis engages in reasoning;
In the imageless and the distinctive
Wisdom truly and fully engages.

Having abandoned mind, mentation, consciousness,
Perception and conception,
Those who have attained the dharma of conceptuality,
The śrāvakas, are not the Victor's heirs.

In the distinction of fields and patience,
The Tathāgata's gnosis is pure;
It arises for the sake of the distinctive meaning,
Utterly free of all active functioning.

My wisdom is of three kinds:
That by which the noble is made manifest,
That by which characteristics are realized,
And that which is bound up with things.

Not joined to the two vehicles,
Wisdom is free of appearances.
Through clinging to things as existent,
It arises for the śrāvakas.
Because it enters into mind only,
The wisdom of the Sugata is stainless.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, there are nine kinds of views of transformation held by the tīrthikas who teach transformation, namely: transformation of shape, transformation of characteristic, transformation of cause, transformation of reasoning, transformation of view, transformation of birth, transformation of thing, transformation of conditions becoming manifest, and transformation of action becoming manifest. Mahāmati, these are the nine views of transformation. Having reflected on them, all the tīrthikas teach the existence of a self, and transformation as arising from what does not exist.

What, then, is transformation of shape? It is this: because a shape is seen to turn into something else, just as gold is seen to turn into various ornaments. For example, Mahāmati, gold is made into bracelets, rings, pendants, and so forth; when it is transformed it appears to have become various shapes, yet the substance of the gold does not change. In the same way, Mahāmati, some tīrthikas imagine that all things transform, and some imagine it of the cause. Having seized on imputation, these are neither so nor otherwise. Thus all the different transformations should be regarded like curd, milk, liquor, and the ripening of fruit. For example, Mahāmati, curd, milk, liquor, fruit, and the like are each imagined by those tīrthikas to be transformations; but in this case, since external things, which are appearances of one's own mind, have no substance, neither existence nor nonexistence transforms in any way at all. Likewise, Mahāmati, regard this as the operation of the habituated conceptuality of childish ordinary beings. Mahāmati, just as with seeing forms arise in an illusion or a dream, here no dharma whatsoever enters into being or turns back. For example, Mahāmati, just as in a dream one sees the son of a barren woman being born and dying, so too one sees entering and turning back.

On this it is said:

Those who imagine of elements, things and faculties,
Of time, of shape, and of transformation,
And of taking up the intermediate existence,
Are not wise.

The Victors do not imagine
The world as dependently arisen;
Rather, these worlds
Are mere conditions, like a city of gandharvas.

Then again the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati, in order to elucidate the meaning of the connections of all dharmas, made this request to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, please explain to me the characteristics of connection and non-connection of all dharmas, so that, through fully realizing the specific distinctions of the different characteristics of connection and non-connection, I and the other bodhisattva mahāsattvas, skilled in the means regarding all connections and non-connections, will not fall into the connection that clings to meaning according to the literal sound; so that, skilled in the connection and non-connection of all dharmas, and having destroyed with intelligence the discriminations of words and letters, we will move among the assemblies of all buddha fields, sealed with the seal of the powers, the masteries, the superknowledges and the dhāraṇīs; possessing, through the various light rays of emanation, the ten culminating states, and an intellect perfectly joined, equal to the spontaneous mode of operation of the moon, the sun, a jewel, and the great elements; turning our views away from the characteristics of our own conceptualizing about all dharmas, and, by seeing all dharmas as dreams, illusions and the like, entering into the abode of the buddha ground; and so that, teaching the Dharma to all the realms of sentient beings according to their dispositions, we will lead them, having abandoned the positions of existence and nonexistence regarding all dharmas, which are like dreams, illusions and the like, and will establish them in what is free of conceptions of arising and ceasing by means of the state that engages different verbal expressions. May the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, please explain this to me."

The Blessed One said, "Excellent, excellent! Mahāmati, therefore listen very well and keep it in mind, and I shall explain it to you."

Saying, "Excellent, Blessed One," the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati listened again to the Blessed One, and the Blessed One spoke to him thus:

Mahāmati, the linkages of attachment to the words of all phenomena taken literally are immeasurable. There is the linkage of attachment to characteristics, the linkage of attachment to conditions, the linkage of attachment to entity and nonentity, the linkage of attachment to the conceptualization of arising and non-arising, the linkage of discrimination through attachment to cessation and non-cessation, the linkage of attachment to characteristics, the linkage of discrimination through attachment to vehicle and non-vehicle, the linkage of attachment to discriminating the conditioned and the unconditioned, the linkage of attachment to conceptualizing the grounds and the specific characteristics of the grounds, the linkage of realizing one's own conceptualizations, the linkage of discriminating the positions of existence and nonexistence, the ground of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), and the linkage of conceptualizing the realization of the three vehicles and the one vehicle.

Mahāmati, these and others are the linkages by which foolish ordinary beings conceptualize their own domains. Bound fast in these linkages, foolish ordinary beings discriminate, attached to and clinging to the characteristic of linkage with entity and nonentity. Like silkworms, because they wish to be linked through the views of their own conceptualization, they wrap themselves and others all around with the thread of linkage spun from the views of their own conceptualization.

Mahāmati, for one who sees all phenomena as utterly secluded, there is here no linkage at all and no characteristic of linkage whatsoever. Mahāmati, because conceptuality does not operate, the bodhisattva, the great being, views all phenomena as secluded and abides in that.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, one who understands external entity and nonentity as characteristics of what appears to one's own mind, who follows mind-only, which is free of appearances, and who sees the linkages of conceptuality regarding all entities, existent and nonexistent, as utterly secluded, finds no linkage in any phenomenon and no characteristic of linkage either. Mahāmati, here there is no bondage whatsoever and no liberation; otherwise, it is through an intellect fallen into error that one conceives of bondage and liberation. Why is that? Because in all phenomena no linkage to existence or nonexistence is apprehended.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, the linkages of foolish ordinary beings are of three kinds: attachment, aversion, and delusion. Then there is the craving for renewed existence that is accompanied by joy and attachment, and that through which, once linked, the linkages of the destinies arise. Here, the linking of sentient beings into the five destinies is linkage. Mahāmati, once linkage is cut off, there is no longer any knowing of linkage or of the characteristic of linkage.

Furthermore, Mahāmati, because of attachment to the condition of the meeting of the three and to the application of activity, the consciousnesses become linked; and through attachment in the manner of uninterrupted operation, there comes to be linkage to existence. By turning away from the condition of the meeting of the three for the consciousnesses, and by seeing the three gates of liberation, no linkages whatsoever arise.

On this it is said:

Unreal discrimination
Is called the characteristic of linkage;
When it is correctly and fully known,
The net of linkage is cut.

Grasping words as knowledge of entities,
Just like the silkworm,
Fools, unskilled regarding linkage,
Are bound by their own conceptuality.

Then Mahāmati again asked: "The Blessed One has said this: 'Whatever entities are imagined by whatever conceptuality, they are merely imputed; they have no intrinsic nature.' Blessed One, if they are merely imputed and no characteristic of an intrinsic nature of entities is found, then, as the Blessed One has said, since all phenomena would be of an imputed nature, there would be no entities of affliction or of purification either."

The Blessed One said: "Mahāmati, so it is; it is just as you have said. Mahāmati, the intrinsic nature of entities is not such as foolish ordinary beings imagine it to be. That is merely imputed; the intrinsic nature and characteristic of entities are not found. Yet, Mahāmati, the intrinsic nature of entities is just as the noble ones realize it to be through noble wisdom, noble vision, and the eye of noble discernment."

Mahāmati asked: "Blessed One, if the essential nature of entities is just as the noble ones realize it, not through the miraculous eye or the eye of flesh but through noble wisdom, noble vision, and the eye of noble discernment, and if it is not as foolish ordinary beings imagine the intrinsic nature of entities to be, then, Blessed One, how will the conceptuality of foolish ordinary beings turn back, since they do not understand the entity that is the noble nature? Blessed One, they are neither mistaken nor unmistaken. Why is that? Because they do not understand the intrinsic nature of the essence of the noble entity, and because they see a turning away from the characteristics of existence and nonexistence. Blessed One, even the way the noble ones discriminate entities does not hold good either, because it is not a sphere of activity concerned with an object having specific characteristics. Blessed One, their intrinsic nature and characteristic of entities too, being taught as an illusion, appear as nothing but the imputed nature. That is to say, through falling into views about the characteristics of entities, it becomes the domain of others; and, Blessed One, because they do not understand the specific characteristics of entities, those are not as they really are, and so there would be an infinite regress."

"Blessed One, when what arises from the cause of the imputed nature is not the characteristic of the intrinsic nature of things, how is it that things do not come to be just as they are imagined, as they are discriminated by conceptualizing? Blessed One, the characteristic of discrimination is one thing, and the characteristic of the intrinsic nature of things is another. Blessed One, conceptuality and the characteristic of intrinsic nature are dissimilar as causes. So when childish ordinary beings imagine them in terms of one another, how is it that things do not come to be just so? Is it in this way, in order to turn back the conceptuality of beings, that you say that things do not exist just as they are discriminated by discrimination? Blessed One, do you turn back the views of existence and nonexistence held by beings, only to establish them in the view of existence, through clinging to the intrinsic nature of things and clinging to the domain and object of noble wisdom? By teaching the entity that is the essential nature of noble wisdom, do you do away with the teaching of the Dharma of utter solitude?"

The Blessed One declared: "Mahāmati, by teaching the intrinsic nature of noble things I neither do away with the teaching of the Dharma of utter solitude nor establish beings in the view of existence. Rather, Mahāmati, it is in order to remove completely the ground of fear for beings who, since beginningless time, have clung to the characteristic of the intrinsic nature of things, that I teach the Dharma of utter solitude by way of a view that takes as its characteristic clinging to the intrinsic nature of things as known by noble wisdom. Mahāmati, I have not in fact taught an intrinsic nature of things. But, Mahāmati, in this way, having realized for oneself just as it really is, one comes to practise solitude. Seeing that in error there are no signs, one enters into what is merely the appearance of one's own mind, and turns back the views of external appearances as entities and non-entities. Thoroughly sealed by the three liberations with the seal of realizing just as it really is, and having abandoned entities of existence and nonexistence, one comes to abide in direct perception of the intrinsic nature of things, through the intelligence of individual self-knowing.

"Furthermore, Mahāmati, a bodhisattva mahāsattva should not make the proposition, 'All phenomena are unborn.' Why is that? Because the proposition too is included among all things, and because it has the characteristic of proceeding from its own cause, if one makes the proposition and states, 'All things are unborn,' then, Mahāmati, that bodhisattva mahāsattva falls from his proposition. Since the proposition too arises in dependence on that, whoever makes the proposition, 'All things are unborn,' his proposition too is defeated. If, on the other hand, that proposition too is unborn because it is included among all phenomena, then the proposition too is not distinct in characteristic; and because it is unborn, the assertion that all phenomena are unborn is utterly defeated. Owing to the cause, the members of the proposition, the proposition is not born from existence and nonexistence. Mahāmati, that proposition too, being included among all things, has the characteristic of being unborn from existence and nonexistence. Mahāmati, if by means of that unborn proposition one proposes that all things are unborn, even so one falls from one's proposition. The proposition should not be made from the characteristic of things that are not born from existence and nonexistence. Mahāmati, their proposition too has the characteristic of an unborn nature. Therefore, Mahāmati, because it incurs the fault of many errors, you should not make that proposition. And because the members too have the dissimilar characteristic of being causes of one another, and because they are produced, the members should not be put forward as a proposition.

"Mahāmati, it is thus: although a bodhisattva mahāsattva should not make the proposition that all phenomena are unborn, and likewise that all phenomena are empty and without essential nature, Mahāmati, a bodhisattva mahāsattva should teach all things as being like an illusion and a dream. Otherwise, Mahāmati, so that the childish who have fallen into existence and nonexistence will not be frightened and will not become distant from the Great Vehicle, and in order to remove the ground of fear for the childish, one should teach all phenomena as like an illusion and a dream, because of the characteristic of appearing and not appearing, because of views, and because they deceive the intellect."

On this it is said:

"There is no intrinsic nature and no cognition,
there is no entity and no ground of all;
these are imagined by the childish, like corpses,
by those with wrong conceptions.

In order to proclaim, against all non-Buddhists (tīrthikas),
that all phenomena are unborn:
a thing that is endowed with conditions
is not born for anyone, as existent or nonexistent.

To propose that all things
are unborn does not hold,
for its proof depends upon its cause;
their intellect too will be ruined.

Just as tangled hairs are falsely
seen by people with eye disease,
so this conceptualizing of things
is falsely imagined by the childish.

The three realms of existence are mere imagination;
there is no intrinsic nature of things.
The imagined, as the intrinsic nature of things,
is what the conceptualizers conceive.

Having passed beyond signs, the cognition of things,
and the stirrings of the mind,
my sons, having gone beyond them,
practise nonconceptuality.

Just as, in a mirage,
water is grasped where there is no water,
so are the childish toward appearances;
the noble ones are distinguished from them.

The vision of the noble ones is pure,
born of the three liberations,
freed from arising and perishing,
it moves wholly in the appearanceless.

Things are without appearance,
yet for the yogin they are not nonexistent either;
through the sameness of entity and non-entity
the fruit of the noble ones arises.

How do entity and non-entity
come to be made the same?
When the mind does not know
the stirrings outside and within,
then it brings them to destruction,
and sees the sameness of mind as well."

Mahāmati said: "The Blessed One has said this: when the object to be apprehended is not apprehended by knowledge, then one is established in cognition-only; since there is nothing grasped by cognition, the grasper too is without grasping.

"...since there is no grasping of that, the consciousness proclaimed as 'conceptual thought' does not operate either."

"When you say this, Blessed One, is it that knowledge does not perceive because it fails to comprehend the various particular and general characteristics of things as undifferentiated? Or is it that knowledge does not perceive because the essential nature of things with their various particular and general characteristics is overwhelmed? Or is it that knowledge does not perceive the object to be known because it is cut off by walls, fences, ponds, enclosures, earth, water, wind and fire, or because it is very far away or near? Or is it that the object to be known is not perceived because of having the faculties of the blind, of infants, or of the aged?

"Blessed One, if knowledge does not perceive because it fails to comprehend the various particular and general characteristics as undifferentiated, then, Blessed One, it should not even be called knowledge, for that which does not perceive an existing object is not-knowing. If knowledge does not perceive because the essential nature of things is overwhelmed by the various particular and general characteristics, then, Blessed One, that too is not-knowing; it is not knowledge. Blessed One, knowledge arises when there is something knowable, not when there is none; and because it is connected with that, it is called knowledge of the knowable. If knowledge does not perceive because things are not perceived when cut off by walls, fences, ponds, enclosures, earth, water, wind and fire, or because they are very far away or near, or because the faculties are deficient through being an infant, aged or blind, then, Blessed One, an existing object is not perceived because the intellect is deficient, and that is not knowledge but not-knowing."

The Blessed One said: "Mahāmati, in that case it is not not-knowing; it is knowledge. Mahāmati, it was not with this in mind that I said, 'When knowledge does not perceive an object that is not perceived, that is establishing it as mere cognition.' Rather, because one comprehends that it is merely the appearance of one's own mind, and because external things, beyond existence and nonexistence, are without substance, knowledge does not perceive an object. Because it is not perceived, neither the knowable nor knowledge arises. Through realizing the three gates of liberation, knowledge too is not perceived. But those given to conceptual thought, whose intellects have been imbued since beginningless time with the habitual tendencies of elaborating existence and nonexistence, do not know it in this way. Because they do not know it, I teach mind-only: that once it is understood that there are no external things of substance, shape or characteristics, conceptual thought does not arise.

"Those who are attached to and cling to grasping at a self and at the characteristics of a self, not comprehending that it is merely the appearance of their own mind, conceptualize knowledge and the knowable as separate. Because they conceptualize knowledge and the knowable as separate, and find no discernment of the existence and nonexistence of external things, they come to rely on the view of annihilation."

On this it is said:

"If, while the object to be perceived exists,
Knowledge does not come to see it,
That is not knowledge but not-knowing:
Such is the way of those given to conceptual thought.
If, through non-otherness and lack of characteristics,
Through obstruction, nearness or distance,
Knowledge should not see,
That is called wrong knowledge.
In infants, the aged, and those gone blind,
If, while the knowable exists,
Knowledge does not arise,
That too is called wrong knowledge."

"Furthermore, Mahāmati, immature ordinary beings, with the beginningless elaborations and the bodily corruption, dance in the dance of their own discriminations, and are not skilled in the teaching of the mode of their own established tenet. Attached to the appearance of their own mind as the characteristics of external things, they are attached to the verbal expressions taught as skillful means, and do not cultivate the mode of their own established tenet, which is pure of the four extremes."

Mahāmati said: "Blessed One, it is so; it is just as you have said. Blessed One, please explain to me the characteristics of teaching and of the mode of established tenet, through which I and the other bodhisattva great beings in future times, being skilled in teaching and in the mode of established tenet, will not be deceived by non-Buddhists and by followers of the śrāvaka and pratyekabuddha vehicles who hold wrong conceptions."

The Blessed One said: "Mahāmati, therefore listen very well and keep it in mind, and I shall explain it to you."

"Excellent, Blessed One," he replied, and the bodhisattva great being Mahāmati listened to the Blessed One, and the Blessed One spoke to him thus: "Mahāmati, the modes of Dharma of the tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly complete buddhas of the past, future and present are twofold, namely the mode of teaching and the mode of setting forth established tenets individually.

"What, then, is the mode of verbal teaching? Mahāmati, it is the explaining of the various sūtras of the Dharma, teaching sentient beings in accordance with their minds and inclinations.

"Mahāmati, what, then, is the mode of established tenet? It is that by which yogins turn back conceptual thought about what is the appearance of their own mind. That is, what is free from the positions of oneness, difference, both and neither; what transcends mind, mentation and mental consciousness; the domain of the noble ones, known individually by oneself; turned away from the characteristics of cause, view and reasoning; not tasted by non-Buddhists or by followers of the śrāvaka and pratyekabuddha vehicles who hold wrong conceptions and have fallen into the extremes of existence and nonexistence: this I explain as the established tenet. Mahāmati, you and the other bodhisattva great beings should strive in this mode of established tenet and this characteristic of teaching."

On this it is said:

"My mode is of two kinds:
Established tenet and teaching.
To the immature I explain in words;
For yogins there is the established tenet."

Then again the bodhisattva great being Mahāmati addressed the Blessed One thus: "Blessed One, once, on a certain occasion, the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, declared: 'One should not attend upon the Lokāyatikas, who possess mantras and manifold eloquence;

"...nor associate with it, nor honor it, for by relying on it one gathers worldly material things but does not gather the Dharma." Since this is so, why did the Blessed One say that by relying on the Lokāyata, with its mantras and its manifold eloquence, one gathers worldly material things but does not come to gather the Dharma?

"Mahāmati, the Lokāyata is mantras and manifold eloquence. With various reasons, words and phrases it bewilders the childish. It does not do so by being reasonable, and it does not do so by being meaningful. In this way it teaches the talk of the childish, and therefore, Mahāmati, it is called the Lokāyata of mantras and manifold eloquence. Through various pleasing phrases the childish do not enter the true way. Not understanding any phenomena themselves, the Lokāyatikas bewilder the childish with views that fall into the two extremes. Because they are not freed from the confines of the destinies of rebirth, they ruin themselves as well. Not understanding things to be mere appearances of their own minds, and attached to the intrinsic nature of external entities, their conceptuality does not cease.

"Therefore, Mahāmati, the Lokāyata of mantras and manifold eloquence is not freed from birth, old age, sickness, death, sorrow, lamentation, suffering, unhappiness, turmoil and the rest. With words, various phrases, and arguments built from reasons and examples, it bewilders the childish.

"Mahāmati, Indra too had a mind skilled in many treatises, and he taught a treatise on grammar of his own. Yet his disciple, assuming the form and guise of a nāga, issued a challenge in heaven before the assembled retinue of Indra: 'Either I shall break your thousand-spoked chariot, or else my nāga body and my hooded heads shall be cut off one by one.' Having made this challenge, that disciple of the Lokāyata, in the form and guise of a nāga, defeated Śakra (Indra), lord of the gods, who was endowed with the Dharma. He broke the thousand-spoked chariot and then came back to this world.

"Thus, Mahāmati, the Lokāyata is bound up with various reasons and examples. Even one taught by an animal can in this way bewilder the world of gods and asuras with various words and phrases. If it can establish them in attachment to views of accumulation and depletion, what need is there to speak of humans? Therefore, Mahāmati, because the Lokāyata brings about the causes from which suffering arises, it is to be abandoned entirely. One should not resort to it, one should not rely on it, one should not honor it.

"Mahāmati, the Lokāyatikas teach, with various words and phrases, no more than the body, the intellect, and the apprehension of objects. Mahāmati, the Lokāyata, of a hundred thousand, will in later times, in the final five hundred years, have its compendium split apart by teaching views that are the causes of evil conceptions. Through taking in those who are not true disciples, its compendium will split apart. Mahāmati, it is that very compendium of the Lokāyata, bound up with reasons and various words and split apart, that the tīrthikas, attached and clinging to their own reasons, expound; it is not a system of their own. Mahāmati, no tīrthika whatsoever has a system of his own treatise. Rather, they expound that same Lokāyata in many ways, through a hundred thousand gateways of reasoning. Although it is not their own system, out of delusion they do not know that this is the Lokāyata."

Mahāmati said, "Blessed One, if the tīrthikas, attached and clinging to their own reasons, expound that very Lokāyata with various words, phrases, examples and arguments, and it is not a system of their own, then is the Blessed One not also counted among those who set forth the scriptures of all the tīrthikas? For to the gods, humans and asuras who have come and arrived, gathering from every direction, he too teaches, with various words and phrases, that very Lokāyata, and it is not a scripture of his own, is it?"

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, I do not expound the Lokāyata, nor do I teach accumulation and depletion. Mahāmati, I teach the absence of accumulation and depletion. Here, Mahāmati, what is called accumulation is the heap of birth; it arises from coming together. Here, Mahāmati, what is called depletion is destruction. What is called the absence of accumulation and depletion is a synonym for non-arising. Mahāmati, I do not teach anything that belongs among the conceptualizations of all the tīrthikas. Why is that? Because one is not attached to external entities or non-entities, abides in the mere appearance of one's own mind, does not engage conceptuality in the twofold manner, has no domain of signs, and understands things to be mere appearances of one's own mind, one does not engage in conceptualizing what appears from one's own mind. Because conceptuality is not engaged, one enters the three gateways of liberation, signlessness, emptiness and wishlessness, and therefore this is called liberation.

"Mahāmati, I recall that once, when I was staying in a certain place, a Lokāyatika brahmin came to where I was. Without the Blessed One having given him leave, he said this to me: 'O Gautama, everything is made.'

"Mahāmati, I replied to him: 'O brahmin, if everything is made, that is the first Lokāyata.'

"'O Gautama, everything is unmade.'

"'If, brahmin, everything is unmade, that too is the second Lokāyata. Likewise, everything is impermanent, everything is permanent, everything is produced, everything is not produced: these, brahmin, are six Lokāyatas.'

"Again, Mahāmati, the Lokāyatika brahmin said this to me: 'O Gautama, seeing various sound grounds of proof: everything is one; everything is distinct; everything is both; everything is neither; everything depends on causes.'

"'These, brahmin, are eleven Lokāyatas.'

"'Furthermore, O Gautama, everything is declared; everything is undeclared.'"

"A self exists. There is no self. This world exists. This world does not exist. The other world exists. The other world does not exist. The other world both exists and does not exist. Liberation exists. Liberation does not exist. Everything is momentary. Everything is not momentary. Space, nonanalytical cessation, and nirvāṇa, O Gautama, are made. They are unmade. The intermediate state of existence exists. The intermediate state of existence does not exist."

Mahāmati, I said this to him: "O brahmin, if that is so, these too are Lokāyata. O brahmin, this Lokāyata is yours, not mine. O brahmin, I teach that the three realms of existence arise from the cause of the dysfunctional tendencies, the habitual tendencies of conceptual elaboration and conceptual thought from beginningless time. Brahmin, conceptual thought arises because one does not realize that things are merely the appearance of one's own mind. It does not arise from external things, which are not to be found. Thus the meeting of the three, self, sense faculty, and object, as the tīrthikas hold it, is not mine. O brahmin, I am neither one who asserts causes nor one who asserts no cause. Rather, I establish conceptual thought itself as the apprehended and the apprehender, and I teach dependent origination. This is not understood by those like you, or by others whose mental continuums have fallen into grasping at a self."

Mahāmati, nirvāṇa, space, and cessation cannot even be counted as three, so what need is there to speak of their being made?

Further, Mahāmati, a brahmin Lokāyatika said this to me: "O Gautama, have these three realms of existence arisen from the cause of ignorance, craving, and karma, or are they without a cause?"

"O brahmin, both of these are Lokāyata."

"O Gautama, all things fall within particular and general characteristics."

"O brahmin, that too is Lokāyata. Brahmin, as long as there is stirring of the mind, and attachment to and conceptualization of external objects, so long is there Lokāyata."

Again, Mahāmati, a brahmin Lokāyatika said this to me: "O Gautama, since my renown is made known by establishing the various words, letters, reasons, and examples of all the tīrthikas, O Gautama, is there anything at all that is not Lokāyata?"

"O brahmin, there is something not Lokāyata, which is neither established by you nor unestablished, which is not untaught by various words and letters, and which is not devoid of meaning but is indeed endowed with meaning."

"Is not whatever teaches the unestablished itself Lokāyata?"

"O brahmin, there is something not Lokāyata that is not conceived by any of the tīrthikas, who cling to the conceptual elaboration of external things that are unreal, nor by your intellect. That is to say: conceptual thought does not engage with existence and nonexistence. Through realizing that things are merely the appearance of one's own mind, conceptual thought does not engage. Because there is no grasping at external objects, conceptual thought remains in its own abode. Therefore this, which is not Lokāyata, is mine, not yours.

"'Remains in its own abode' means not engaging. The non-arising of conceptual thought is called not engaging. O brahmin, in that case, that which is not Lokāyata is, in brief, O brahmin, where consciousness does not move. Going, death and transmigration, birth, desire, clinging through attachment, fixation, the bases of views, pride, attachment to various characteristics, coming together, the linking of craving, and attachment to causes: these, O brahmin, are your Lokāyata, not mine."

Mahāmati, the brahmin Lokāyatika came and asked me this, and when I had answered him thus, he fell silent and departed.

Then again a nāga king of the dark faction came in the form of a brahmin and said this to the Blessed One: "O Gautama, nonetheless, the other world itself does not exist."

"Then, young man, from where have you come here?"

"Gautama, I have come here from the White Island."

"Brahmin, that itself is the other world."

Then the young man, defeated and left without eloquence, vanished. Without having asked me, he thinks of proclaiming his own separate tenets, thinking: "The son of the Śākyas is outside my system. He speaks of the non-engagement of afflictions, of the absence of characteristics, and of the absence of causes, and he says that through realizing the characteristic of appearing as one's own conceptual thought, conceptual thought does not engage."

"Mahāmati, why do you too ask me in this way, saying that one who attends upon Lokāyatikas with mantras and various kinds of eloquence comes to gather material things, and does not gather the Dharma?"

"Blessed One, what is the meaning of the words 'material things' and 'Dharma'?"

The Blessed One said: "Mahāmati, excellent, excellent, that you, looking to the multitudes of beings of the future, have set out to investigate the meaning of these two words! Therefore, Mahāmati, listen very well and keep it in mind, and I shall explain it to you."

Saying, "Excellent, Blessed One," the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati listened to the Blessed One, and the Blessed One said this to him:

"Here, Mahāmati, what are material things? They are as follows. Material things are contact, mingling, caressing, pride, tasting, attachment to external objects, entering into the two extremes through wrong views, the renewed arising of the aggregates, birth, aging, sickness, and death, sorrow, lamentation, suffering, distress, the arising of turmoil, the craving for renewed existence, and so on. These I and other buddhas call material things. Mahāmati, this is how, by attending upon Lokāyata, one comes to gather material things and does not gather the Dharma, and one attains Lokāyata."

Then, Mahāmati, what is it to gather the Dharma? It is this: to realize the twofold selflessness, that phenomena are one's own mind; to see the characteristic of selflessness in phenomena and in persons, so that conceptuality no longer operates; to know the grounds thoroughly, each higher than the last, and so to turn back from mind, mentation, and mental consciousness; to realize the empowerment into the wisdom of all the buddhas; to take up the endless state; and to have spontaneous mastery over all phenomena. This is called Dharma.

Mahāmati, the talk of the tīrthikas makes the things conceived in the elaborations of all views fall into the two extremes. It is this: fools mostly fall into the two extremes of permanence and annihilation, but the wise do not. Adopting the doctrine of causelessness, one comes to a view of permanence. Holding that when the cause has perished there is no causal thing, one comes to a view of annihilation. Not seeing arising, abiding, and perishing is what is called Dharma.

This, Mahāmati, is the definitive account of Dharma and of material things. You and the other bodhisattva great beings should train in it.

On this it is said:

Through the means of attraction one tames beings;
Through moral discipline one brings them under control;
Through wisdom one destroys their views;
Through liberation one makes them flourish greatly.

All this that the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) teach
Is Lokāyata, and it is false.
Because they view cause and effect as existent,
They have no established tenet of their own.

One like me alone has a tenet of his own,
Which has abandoned cause and effect;
Entirely free of Lokāyata,
I shall teach it to the assembly of disciples.

It is mind only; there is no appearance.
Free of permanence and annihilation,
As the things grasped and the grasper,
Mind appears in two aspects.

As long as mind operates,
So long is there Lokāyata.
When the world sees its own mind,
Conceptuality does not operate.

Accrual is the effect, the aim accomplished;
Depletion is the effect not seen.
When one fully knows accrual and depletion,
Conceptuality does not operate.

Permanent and impermanent, made
And unmade, other and not other:
All these and the like
Are the ways of Lokāyata.

The Noble Descent into Laṅkā, the sixth section.

Then again the bodhisattva great being Mahāmati said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, this which is called nirvāṇa, which all the tīrthikas conceive, calling it 'nirvāṇa, nirvāṇa': of what is it a designation?"

The Blessed One said, "Therefore, Mahāmati, listen very well and keep it in mind, and I shall explain to you how the tīrthikas conceive nirvāṇa, and how nirvāṇa comes about in accordance with their conceptions."

Saying, "Excellent, Blessed One," the bodhisattva great being Mahāmati listened to the Blessed One, and the Blessed One spoke to him thus:

"Mahāmati, some tīrthikas declare that through the cessation of the aggregates, elements, and sense bases, through freedom from attachment to objects, and through seeing that phenomena are not in accord with permanence, the many states of mind and mental factors do not arise; that past, future, and present objects are not recalled; and that, like a lamp, a seed, or the wind, the cause being exhausted, conceptuality no longer operates. Therefore they form the notion of nirvāṇa, but, Mahāmati, because they hold a view of destruction, they do not pass into nirvāṇa.

"Mahāmati, others declare that when conceptuality regarding objects is exhausted, like an arrow whose momentum is spent, going to dwell in another region is liberation. Again, some tīrthikas declare that liberation comes from the destruction of the view of knower and known. Some conceive liberation as coming from the non-operation of conceptuality and from seeing permanence and impermanence. Some think, 'The many conceptualizations of signs bring about the arising of suffering,' and being unskilled regarding what appears as merely their own mind, and frightened and terrified by signs, seeing signs and longing for bliss, they declare that they have formed the notion of nirvāṇa. Again, some realize the specific and general characteristics of all inner and outer phenomena, and conceive nirvāṇa as this: that past, future, and present things do not perish, because they exist. Some conceive nirvāṇa as the non-perishing of the self, the sentient being, the life principle, the nourisher, the person, the individual, and all phenomena.

"Mahāmati, some tīrthikas of unskilled intellect conceive nirvāṇa as coming from seeing the distinction between primal nature (prakṛti) and the person, and from the transformation of the qualities (guṇas) acting as agent. Some conceive nirvāṇa as coming from the complete exhaustion of merit and demerit; some, from knowing the exhaustion of the afflictions; some, from seeing the worlds as made by the Lord (Īśvara) through his own independent power. Some hold, 'This arising of the world comes from one thing entering into another, not from a cause'; while clinging to that cause, out of delusion they do not understand, and they conceive nirvāṇa as coming from understanding that.

"Mahāmati, some tīrthikas conceive nirvāṇa as coming from understanding the truths and the path. Some fully understand qualities and the possessor of qualities, and by seeing them as one, as different, as both, and as neither, they form the notion of nirvāṇa. Some say that things arise from their own nature: seeing the nature of things to be like the variegated colors of the peacock, the many kinds of jewel mines, or the sharpness of thorns, they conceive nirvāṇa. Mahāmati, some conceive nirvāṇa as coming from understanding the twenty-five principles; some, from protecting the people and upholding the teaching of the six qualities. Some, because they see time as the agent, understand that the arising of the world depends on time, and by understanding it thus, they conceive nirvāṇa. Mahāmati, some conceive nirvāṇa through existence; some, through nonexistence; some, through fully knowing existence and nonexistence; some, through seeing existence and nirvāṇa as without distinction."

Mahāmati, some who roar the lion's roar of the Omniscient One conceive of nirvāṇa in this way: as understanding that everything is merely the appearance of one's own mind; as not being attached to external things; as being free from the four extremes and seeing things just as they truly abide; as not falling into the two extremes of conceptuality about what is the appearance of one's own mind; as not apprehending grasped and grasper; as not seeing reality established by any valid cognition; as not conceptualizing reality, because conceptualizing deceives, and so eliminating that; as understanding the noble Dharma that each realizes personally within himself; as realizing the two kinds of selflessness; as turning back the two kinds of affliction; as purifying the two obscurations; and as turning away from mind, mentation and mental consciousness through the samādhis of images such as illusion, rising from ground to ground up to the ground of the Tathāgata.

Even though the mistaken non-Buddhist reasoners teach other ideas of this kind, these do not accord with reason and are rejected by the wise. Mahāmati, all of them conceive of nirvāṇa with an outlook that falls into the two extremes. Mahāmati, although all the tīrthikas fully conceive of nirvāṇa through such conceptualizations, in this there is no entering into anything or turning back from anything. Mahāmati, the nirvāṇa of each of the tīrthikas, devoted to their own treatises and examined in various ways, is mistaken; it does not abide in the way they imagine it. Because the mind moves, comes and goes, there is no nirvāṇa for any of them. Mahāmati, you and the other bodhisattvas should train in this, and you should turn away from the views of nirvāṇa held by all the tīrthikas.

On this it is said:

The non-Buddhists who hold views of nirvāṇa
Conceive of it in differing ways.
Theirs is merely conceptual thought;
There is no means of liberation.

Apart from bond and what is bound,
They have entirely abandoned the means;
Though tīrthikas imagine liberation,
They will never be fit for liberation.

To the minds of the non-Buddhist systems
It appears manifold, diverse, and apart.
Since therefore they have no liberation,
How can the foolish imagine it?

Through bad views of cause and effect
All the non-Buddhists are intoxicated.
They speak for the sides of existence and nonexistence;
Therefore they have no liberation.

Those who delight in the elaborations of childish talk
Do not open their minds wide to the real itself.
Talk is the birthplace of suffering in the three realms;
The real itself is the cause that destroys suffering.

Just as a form in a mirror
Appears, and yet does not exist,
So in the mirror of habitual tendencies
The childish see the mind as twofold.

Through not knowing the appearances of mind,
Twofold conceptuality arises.
When the appearances of mind are fully known,
Conceptuality does not occur.

Mind itself transforms into variety,
Yet it is free of mark and basis of marks.
As the childish imagine it,
The aspect that appears does not appear.

Since the three existences are mere conceptuality,
There are no external objects.
The varied appearances of conceptuality
The childish do not understand.

Through distinctions of name and perception,
Conceptuality is taught in sūtra after sūtra.
Apart from the expressions,
What is expressed cannot be known.

Then the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, may the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, please explain to me the self-awakening of the Buddha, by which I and the other bodhisattva mahāsattvas, skilled in the nature of the Tathāgata, may bring both ourselves and others to understanding."

The Blessed One said: "Therefore, Mahāmati, ask it yourself, and I shall answer you in the way that suits you."

Mahāmati said: "Blessed One, is the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, made or unmade, cause or effect, mark or basis of marks, expression or what is expressed, cognition or what is to be known? Blessed One, is he one with such definitive verbal terms, or different from them?"

The Blessed One said: "Mahāmati, in terms of such definitive verbal terms, the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, is neither made nor unmade, neither cause nor effect. Why is that? Because either alternative would be faulty. Mahāmati, if the Tathāgata were made, he would be impermanent; and because he would be impermanent, all effects would also become tathāgatas. That is not what I and the other tathāgatas intend. If he were unmade, then, since he would have no attained nature, the accomplishment of the accumulations would be pointless, and because he would be unmade he would be just like a hare's horn or a barren woman's son.

"Mahāmati, what is neither cause nor effect is neither existent nor nonexistent. What is neither existent nor nonexistent lies outside the four extremes. Mahāmati, the four extremes are the conduct of the world; whatever lies outside the four extremes becomes mere words, like a barren woman's son. Mahāmati, a barren woman's son is mere words and does not fall into the four extremes. Since it does not fall into them, it is no measure of knowledge for the wise. In this way the wise should understand the meaning of the words of all the tathāgatas.

"Mahāmati, the wise should also understand the meaning of what I have taught: 'All phenomena are selfless.' Mahāmati, the being of selflessness is selflessness itself. Like a horse and an ox, all phenomena exist in their own being and do not exist in the being of another. That is, Mahāmati, the being of an ox is not the nature of a horse, and the being of a horse is not the nature of an ox; yet each exists and is not nonexistent. Those two are not nonexistent in their own characteristics; those two exist in their own characteristics. Likewise, Mahāmati, all phenomena are not nonexistent in characteristics; they do exist.

Therefore it is by grasping at conceptual thought that childish ordinary beings come to understand selflessness, and not by being free of conceptual thought. In the same way, all phenomena should be understood as emptiness, as unarisen, and as without essential nature.

Likewise, the Tathāgata is neither one with the aggregates nor different from them. If he were not distinct from the aggregates, he would be impermanent, because the aggregates are produced. If he were different from them, then, there being two, he would be something other, like the two horns of an ox. Here, because all things are alike, they are not different. Because a distinction in length is seen, they are not different. Mahāmati, the left horn of an ox is other than the right, and the right is other than the left; likewise long and short are other in relation to each other. Likewise various colors are also different from one another. The Tathāgata, too, is not different from the aggregates, the elements, and the sense sources.

Likewise, liberation and the Tathāgata are neither different nor one, for the Tathāgata himself is taught by the word "liberation." If liberation and the Tathāgata were different, he would possess the characteristics of form, and because he possessed the characteristics of form, he would be impermanent. If they were one, there would be no distinction in the characteristics of what yogins attain. But, Mahāmati, yogins do see a distinction, and therefore they are neither one nor different. Likewise, knowledge and the knowable are neither one nor different.

Mahāmati, that which is neither permanent nor impermanent, neither cause nor effect, neither conditioned nor unconditioned, neither intellect nor what is to be known, neither characteristic nor the basis of characteristics, neither the aggregates nor different from the aggregates, neither speaker nor what is spoken, and which has no connection with oneness, difference, both, or neither, is withdrawn from all valid cognition. What is withdrawn from all valid cognition becomes mere words. What is mere words is unborn. What is unborn is unceasing. What is unceasing is like space. Mahāmati, space is neither cause nor effect. What is neither cause nor effect is without any objective support. What is without objective support has transcended all conceptual elaboration. What has transcended all conceptual elaboration is the Tathāgata. That, Mahāmati, is perfect, complete buddhahood. That is buddhahood, turned away from all valid cognition and from all the senses.

On this it is said:

Turned away from valid cognition and the senses,
it is not an effect, nor is it a cause.
It has no intellect and nothing to be known;
it has left behind characteristic and the basis of characteristics.
A buddha depending on the aggregates
has never been seen by anyone anywhere.
What no one anywhere has seen,
how could one contemplate it?

It is not made, nor is it unmade;
it is not an effect, nor is it a cause.
It is not the aggregates, nor joined with the aggregates,
nor joined with anything else, for that would be a mixture.
That which, examined as being some entity,
is not seen to appear,
is not to be conceived as nonexistent either:
that is the true nature of phenomena.

Nonexistence is reckoned as the counterpart of existence,
and existence is reckoned as the counterpart of nonexistence.
Therefore do not understand it as nonexistent,
nor conceive of it as existent.

Those who corrupt the positions of self and selflessness
and engage with mere sounds
sink into the two extremes;
ruined themselves, they ruin the childish.
Whenever one sees my way,
which is free from every fault,
that is seeing correctly,
and such a one does not revile the Guides.

Then again the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, said this to the Blessed One: "In the course of this teaching, the Blessed One has spoken of non-arising and non-cessation, and the Blessed One has also said, 'This non-arising and non-cessation is a byname of the Tathāgata.' When the Blessed One said this, is this non-arising and non-cessation a nonentity, or is it another synonym of the Tathāgata? The Blessed One has explained that all phenomena are unborn and unceasing because one does not see the positions of existence and nonexistence. If all phenomena are said to be unborn, then, Blessed One, since all phenomena are unborn, it would not even be proper to speak of 'phenomena.' But if it is another synonym of some particular phenomenon, then please explain it, Blessed One; please explain it, Sugata."

The Blessed One said, "Therefore, Mahāmati, listen very well and keep it in mind, and I shall explain it to you."

"Excellent, Blessed One," replied the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, and he listened to the Blessed One. The Blessed One then said this to him:

"Mahāmati, the Tathāgata is not a nonentity. Nor is this a statement that all phenomena are without arising and without cessation, nor does it depend on conditions, nor is my speaking of non-arising meaningless. Rather, Mahāmati, this is a byname for the mind-made Dharma body of the Tathāgata. That unborn which is not the domain of any of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), of the śrāvakas, of the pratyekabuddhas, or of the bodhisattvas abiding on the seventh ground, is a byname of the Tathāgata; it is a synonymous term.

"For example, Mahāmati, there are Śakra, Indra, and Puraṃdara, the Destroyer of Cities; hand, doer, and handler; body, frame, and form; earth, ground, and the Bearer of Riches; sky, space, and the passage where things come and go. In this way, many synonymous words are devised for each single thing. Yet although there are many names, one does not conceive of many things, nor do they thereby become without intrinsic nature.

"Likewise, Mahāmati, in this Sahā world system I too resound in the ears of the childish by names numbering three hundred thousand incalculables. Though they speak of me by these, they are known as synonyms for the names of the Tathāgata. Among them, Mahāmati, some know me as the Tathāgata."

Some know me as the Self-Existent (Svayambhū). Likewise, some know me as the Leader, the Guide, the Chief Guide, the Buddha, the Bull, Brahmā, Viṣṇu, Īśvara, the principal (pradhāna), Kapila, Bhūtānta, Ariṣṭanemi, Soma, Bhāskara (the Sun), Rāma, Vyāsa, Śuka, Indra, Bali, and Varuṇa. Still others know me as the Unborn, the Unceasing, emptiness, suchness, truth, reality, the limit of reality, the realm of phenomena (dharmadhātu), nirvāṇa, the Permanent, sameness, nonduality, signlessness, the teaching of the cause by which one becomes a buddha from conditions, liberation, the path, the truths, the Omniscient One, the Victor, and the one whose nature is mind.

Mahāmati, beings in this world system and in others know me by these and other names, countless, fully three hundred thousand, neither more nor less. Like the moon in water, I have neither entered nor emerged, yet the childish grasp me through minds that have fallen into the two extremes. Even so, they honor me, make me foremost, make images of me, and worship me. But because they are not skilled in the meaning of words and in etymology, they do not know their own way: that these are not different names. Attached to the sounds of explanations and to expressions, they imagine that non-arising and non-cessation are nonexistence, and they do not consider them to be other synonyms of the Tathāgata's name, like Indra, Śakra (Indra, lord of the gods), and Purandara. Because they follow the expression of all phenomena as meaning just what the sound says, they have no taste for teachings that set out the proper mode of each.

Mahāmati, those foolish people say this: "The meaning is just as the sound is; the meaning is nothing other than the sound. Why is that? Because the meaning has no body, the meaning is nothing other than the sound; the sound itself is the meaning." Mahāmati, because they do not fully know the nature of sound, those of unskilled intellect do not know that while sound arises and perishes, meaning neither arises nor perishes. Mahāmati, sound falls into letters; meaning does not fall into letters. Because it is free of both entity and non-entity, it is unborn and has no body.

Mahāmati, because the existence and nonexistence of letters are not found, the tathāgatas do not teach a Dharma that has fallen into letters; rather, they teach a Dharma that has not fallen into letters. Mahāmati, because the Dharma has no letters, whoever expounds a Dharma that has fallen into letters is talking nonsense. Therefore, Mahāmati, I and other buddhas and bodhisattvas have taught that, in terms of explanation and expression, the tathāgatas have not spoken a single letter and will not speak one. Why is that? Because all phenomena have no letters. Yet they do not teach what is devoid of meaning either; they teach by taking up conceptual thought.

Mahāmati, if no phenomena were taught at all, the teaching would perish, and when the teaching has perished, there would be no buddhas, pratyekabuddhas, or śrāvakas either. If there were none of them, who would teach what to whom? Therefore, Mahāmati, the bodhisattva mahāsattva should not be attached to the sounds of explanation and expression.

Mahāmati, the expression of explanation is a delusion, since it proceeds according to the dispositions of sentient beings. I and the other tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly complete buddhas teach the Dharma to beings of differing inclinations in order to turn back mind, mentation, and mental consciousness. It is not to turn them back from the realization of the noble wisdom known personally by oneself, which comes when the many kinds of conceptual thought have ceased through understanding that all phenomena are without appearance, that they are merely the appearances of one's own mind.

Mahāmati, the bodhisattva mahāsattva should rely on the meaning, not on the letter. Mahāmati, a son or daughter of good family who follows after the letter ruins their own grasp of the meaning and does not bring others to understanding either. Bad logicians, unskilled in the specific characteristics of all phenomena and of the grounds, and not knowing words and etymology, proclaim their own positions with minds fallen into bad views. But if one is skilled in the specific characteristics of all phenomena and of the grounds, understands and realizes the synonyms of words and their etymology, and becomes skilled in the meaning of things and in reasoning, then one satisfies oneself with the bliss of the true signless state and also establishes others properly in the Great Vehicle.

Mahāmati, when the Great Vehicle is properly upheld, buddhas, bodhisattvas, śrāvakas, and pratyekabuddhas are upheld. When buddhas, bodhisattvas, śrāvakas, and pratyekabuddhas are upheld, sentient beings are upheld. When all sentient beings are upheld, the sublime Dharma is upheld. Mahāmati, when the sublime Dharma is upheld, the lineage of the buddhas is kept from being cut off. Because the lineage of the buddhas is not cut off, one is known to attain special births. Therefore, the bodhisattva mahāsattvas who attain those sublime births take up birth there, and, because they firmly uphold the Great Vehicle, through the ten masteries they come to assume various forms and guises. Having understood and realized the guises of sentient beings and the characteristics of their latent tendencies, they teach the Dharma in accordance with suchness.

Here, what is called reality is suchness, not-otherness, reality, the characteristic of neither taking up nor casting away, and the complete pacification of all conceptual elaborations. Mahāmati, because reality has no letters, a son or daughter of good family should not become skilled in clinging to meaning as being just what the sound says. They should not be like one who looks at the finger.

Mahāmati, it is like this. Suppose someone points something out to another with the tip of a finger, and that person looks only at the fingertip and follows it. In the same way, Mahāmati, the hosts of childish ordinary beings, as though childish by nature, cling and are attached to the fingertip of the literal word. Clinging in this way to the fingertip of the word, they come to the time of death without ever letting go of the literal fingertip and coming to understand the ultimate meaning.

Mahāmati, it is like this. As for the food and drink of the childish, no one eats it without its being cooked. If some were to eat it uncooked, not knowing how to prepare food step by step, they would be regarded as mad. In the same way, Mahāmati, non-arising and non-cessation too will not be beautiful unless they are cultivated. One must certainly cultivate them here, seeing the meaning that the fingertip points to, and not mere words. Therefore, Mahāmati, one should strive for the meaning.

Mahāmati, the meaning is utterly solitary and is the cause of nirvāṇa. The word is bound up with conceptual thought and brings about saṃsāra. Mahāmati, the meaning is found from those of great learning. Mahāmati, what is called great learning is this: being skilled in the meaning, not being skilled in the word. Here, being skilled in the meaning is the view that is unmixed with any of the doctrines of the tīrthikas, by which one neither falls oneself nor causes others to fall. Mahāmati, such great learning in the meaning is truly meaningful. Therefore one who desires the meaning should rely on such people. The opposite of this is attachment to the literal meaning of words, and those who seek reality should avoid such people.

Then Mahāmati, empowered by the blessing of the Buddha, again said this:

"In the view of non-arising and non-cessation, the Blessed One makes no distinction at all. Why is that? Blessed One, the causes of all the tīrthikas are also unborn and unceasing. Blessed One, in your teaching too, space, cessation not brought about by analysis, and the element of nirvāṇa are unborn and unceasing. Blessed One, the tīrthikas also declare that beings arise from a cause consisting of an agent and conditions. The Blessed One, too, giving another name to that very cause and calling it a condition, declares that beings arise from the conditions of ignorance, craving, karma and conceptual thought. And it is the same for you and for them regarding external things arising through external conditions. Therefore, Blessed One, there would be no distinction between this doctrine and the doctrine of the tīrthikas.

"Atoms, the principal (pradhāna), Īśvara, Prajāpati and the rest, together with the nine substances, are unborn and unceasing. Blessed One, in your teaching too, all things are unborn and unceasing, since neither existence nor nonexistence is found in them. Because the elements do not perish, their own characteristics neither arise nor cease. Whatever transformations they undergo, the elements remain elements; they do not abandon the nature of elements. Blessed One, this has been imagined by you and by all the tīrthikas as a transformation of the conceptualization of the elements. Therefore there is no distinction in this doctrine. Please explain the distinction by which what the Tathāgata teaches is distinct and is not the doctrine of all the tīrthikas.

"Blessed One, if your own doctrine has no distinction, then, since non-arising and non-cessation would be the cause, the tīrthikas too would become buddhas. The Blessed One has said that for many tathāgatas to arise in a single world system is impossible and cannot happen. Yet if, as I say, the doctrine has no distinction, then, since existence and nonexistence of the result have been accepted, there would be many tathāgatas."

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, my non-arising and non-cessation is not the same as the tīrthikas' teaching of non-arising and non-cessation, nor is it the same as the teaching of arising and impermanence. Why is that? Mahāmati, for the tīrthikas there is an unarisen intrinsic nature of things, which has attained an unchanging characteristic. Mine does not fall in this way to the side of existence or of nonexistence. Mahāmati, mine is free from the sides of existence and nonexistence. Being free from arising and destruction, it is neither a thing nor a non-thing. Like seeing the various forms of an illusion or a dream, it is not a non-thing either.

"If one asks how it is not a non-thing, it is because the characteristic of the intrinsic nature of form cannot be grasped. Because things appear and do not appear, are grasped and cannot be grasped, all things are therefore neither things nor without an essence. Since, through understanding them to be mere appearances of one's own mind, conceptual thought no longer engages, the world abides in itself and is without activity. Yet the childish conceive it as having activity; the noble ones do not.

"Mahāmati, this is intoxication by conceptualizing unreal objects, like a city of gandharvas or an illusory man. Mahāmati, it is like this. Some who are childish by nature conceive of entering and leaving, thinking, 'These many and various illusory men, beings and travelers have entered the city of the gandharvas; now they have come out.' Yet in truth nothing at all enters or leaves there; their conceptualization is simply mistaken. In the same way, Mahāmati, arising and non-arising are the error of the childish. Just as with the arising of an illusory man, there is nothing at all here that is either conditioned or unconditioned. Since an illusory man does nothing at all by way of existing or not existing, there is no arising or ceasing."

In the same way, all phenomena too are free of arising and perishing. Fools, by contrast, through perceptions that have fallen into error, conceive of arising and ceasing; the noble ones do not. Here, Mahāmati, what is called "error" is this: things are neither as their intrinsic nature is imagined to be, nor otherwise. If one imagines them otherwise, one becomes attached to the essential nature of all things, and so does not see their solitude. When solitude is not seen, conceptuality does not cease. Therefore, Mahāmati, seeing signlessness is wholesome, and seeing signs is not. Signs are the cause of birth, and so they are unwholesome. Mahāmati, signlessness is the non-engagement of conceptuality, and non-arising is explained as nirvāṇa. Here, Mahāmati, nirvāṇa is this: by seeing reality abiding just as it is, the conceptualizing mind and its many mental factors are thoroughly transformed, and on that basis the Tathāgata realizes it through his own noble wisdom, known individually by oneself. That is explained as nirvāṇa.

On this it is said:

To reverse arising,
non-arising is established.
The teaching of the doctrine of no cause
fools do not understand.

All these are unborn,
without entity, and without continuity too.
Like dreams, like illusions, like a city of gandharvas,
there are things that have no cause.

"Tell me how they are unborn,
without intrinsic nature, and empty."

"Apart from aggregations,
the intellect perceives no entity.
Therefore I have explained them as unborn, empty,
and without intrinsic nature.

Likewise, in each aggregation,
since nothing appears, there is no existence.
Nor, as in the view of the tīrthikas,
do aggregations become imperishable.

Like dreams, tangled hairs,
illusions, mirages, a city of gandharvas,
appearing though they have no cause,
so too is the variegated world.

Disputing by means of the doctrine of no cause,
one establishes the unborn.
When non-arising is established,
my way is not destroyed.

When the doctrine of no cause is taught,
the non-Buddhists grow afraid, saying,
'How, from what, by what,
and where does the causeless come to arise?'

When at some time the conditioned is seen
as not from a cause, as causeless,
then the view of those who speak
of arising and perishing is reversed."

"Is non-arising the absence of entities?
Or is it an examination of conditions?
Or is it a name for entities?
Or is it meaningless? Please tell me."

"Non-arising is not the absence of entities,
nor is it an examination of conditions.
Nor is it a name for entities,
nor is it a name without meaning.

That which is not the domain of śrāvakas,
of pratyekabuddhas, of those who have reached the seventh ground,
nor of the tīrthikas:
that is the characteristic of non-arising.

The reversal of causes and conditions,
the refutation of agents,
the establishment as mind only:
this I have explained as non-arising.

Things that do not come from causes,
free of conceiving and the conceived,
liberated from the positions of existence and nonexistence:
this I have explained as non-arising.

Mind fully liberated from appearance,
free as well of the two natures,
the transformation of the bases:
this I have explained as non-arising.

External things are not nonentities,
nor is there any grasping by the mind.
All views completely abandoned:
that is the characteristic of the unborn.

Likewise 'empty,' 'without intrinsic nature,'
and all such words are imputed.
It is never empty merely by emptiness;
it is the emptiness of non-arising.

From the coming together of conditions
things come forth and cease.
Apart from the aggregation
there is no arising and no ceasing.

As one and as different,
just as the non-Buddhists conceive them,
things are not different,
and apart from aggregation they exist nowhere.

Existent and nonexistent things do not arise;
neither nonexistent nor existent is found anywhere.
Therefore it is aggregation, in another sense,
that comes forth and ceases.

This is mere imputation,
a connection of mutual dependence.
Apart from the connection of conditions
there is nothing to be produced.

Since nothing is produced, there is no arising.
Free of the faults of the non-Buddhists,
I have explained it as mere connection;
fools do not understand.

If, apart from connection,
the production of things is said to exist,
that destroys connection,
and should be known as the doctrine of no cause.

If, apart from connection,
an entity existed for anyone,
connection would then illuminate
the kinds of substances, like a lamp.

Whatever phenomenon the unskilled conceive
apart from connections
is unborn, without essential nature;
its nature is like space.

The nature of reality attained by the noble ones
is a different non-arising:
that in which no arising occurs,
that is the forbearance of the unborn.

When all these worlds
are seen as connection itself,
thinking, 'This is mere connection,'
then the mind too rests in equipoise.

Ignorance, craving, karma, and the rest:
these connections are internal.
The wheel, water, wood, clay, and the rest,
seeds, elements, and so forth, are external.

These are not mere connection.
If some hold that things
arise from other conditions,
that does not stand in reasoning or scripture.

If there is no entity to be produced,
from what condition does the intellect arise?
These produce one another mutually;
therefore know them as conditions.

Heat, moisture, solidity, and motion:
fools imagine these phenomena.
This aggregation is not a phenomenon;
therefore it is without essential nature.

Physicians, according to the patient,
prescribe different treatments,
yet their treatise is not different;
the distinctions are made by the illness.

In the same way, I too, knowing the strength of the faculties
of beings' mental streams,
stained by the faults and failings of the afflictions,
explain the way to the childish.

Though afflictions and faculties differ,
my teaching is not divided.
The peaceful path endowed with eight limbs
is the one vehicle."

Then again the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, all the tīrthikas conceive of impermanence, saying 'impermanence, impermanence,' and you too, in all your teachings and statements, say:

'Alas, formations are impermanent;
they are of the nature to arise and perish.'

"When you say this, Blessed One (the Buddha), is it true, or is it false? Blessed One, how many kinds of impermanence are there?"

The Blessed One said: "Mahāmati, all the tīrthikas imagine it in eight ways, but I do not. What are the eight? Here, Mahāmati, some say that impermanence is the ceasing of undertaking. Mahāmati, what is called 'undertaking' is arising, and not arising is impermanence. Some say that impermanence is the ceasing of shape. Some say that form itself is impermanent. Some say that impermanence is the further, distinct alteration of form. They say that all phenomena, without interruption, continually perish and dissolve of their own accord, and that just as milk turns into curd, which is a distinct alteration, so in all things the impermanence of vanishing from sight and of destruction is at work. Some imagine the entity to be impermanent. Some imagine impermanence as the absence of entities in entities. Some say it is the impermanence that is the non-arising of all phenomena, because impermanence too is included within that.

"Here, Mahāmati, what is called the impermanence that is the absence of entities in entities is this: the own-characteristics of the elements and of what is derived from the elements are destroyed and are not apprehended, and the essential nature of the elements does not come into play.

"Here, what is called the impermanence of non-arising is this: permanent impermanence; not arising from existence or from nonexistence; and the not seeing of any phenomenon at all. When one examines down to the atoms, this not seeing is a term for non-arising, not for arising. Mahāmati, that is the characteristic of the impermanence that is non-arising. Because they do not understand this, all the tīrthikas fall into proclaiming the impermanence of arising.

"Furthermore, Mahāmati, those who hold that the entity is impermanent have imagined it with their own minds. Entities are neither permanent nor impermanent. Why is that? It is like this, Mahāmati: impermanence itself does not cease. Here, Mahāmati, the absence of entities in all entities is the effect of impermanence. Without impermanence, the essencelessness of all entities is not apprehended. Just as with something to be broken by a stick, a stone or a hammer, whichever it may be, and the breaking of it, they are seen to appear without any distinction between them. Therefore impermanence is the cause, and the essencelessness of all entities is the effect. Since there is no distinction of cause and effect such that one could say 'this is impermanence' and 'this is the effect,' cause and effect are not distinct. Because entities have no cause, all entities are permanent.

"Mahāmati, the essencelessness of all entities is causeless, and this the childish ordinary people do not understand. A cause does not produce a dissimilar effect. If it did produce one, then all those entities would be dissimilar effects of impermanence; and if cause and effect came to be without distinction, a distinction between cause and effect would still be seen.

"If their impermanence were an entity, it would fall into being an action, the nature of a cause, and a characteristic. It would also be complete in a single entity or in all entities. Because it falls into being the cause of an action and the characteristic of an entity, impermanence itself would become impermanent. Since impermanence would itself be impermanent, all entities would also become permanent; they would become permanence itself.

"If impermanence were present within all entities, it would therefore fall into the three times. In that case, past form would have perished together with it. The future too would be unarisen, because form does not arise. In the present, too, form and characteristic are not distinct. Form is a particular arrangement of the great elements.
