The nature of the elements and of what is derived from the elements does not perish. For the tīrthikas, because it is free of identity and difference, the nature of the elements and of what is derived from the elements does not perish. Because, for all the tīrthikas, none of the elements perish, all three realms of existence are the elements and what is derived from the elements, and arising, abiding and change are designated in them. What other impermanence is there, distinct from the elements and what is derived from them, that the tīrthikas could imagine as impermanence? Since the characteristic of the elements' nature does not perish, there is neither coming into play nor ceasing.

"Here, what is called the impermanence that is the ceasing of undertaking means that the elements do not undertake, that is, originate, other elements. Because their own-characteristics are mutually incompatible, they do not originate anything distinct. Because they possess two aspects, the absence of any distinction of theirs and their again not originating, the notion of impermanence arises with regard to not originating.

"Here, what is called the impermanence that is the ceasing of shape is this: the elements and what is derived from the elements do not change until their destruction. Mahāmati, what is called 'destruction' is analyzing and examining them down to the atoms. Destruction means that long and short are not apprehended, because the shape of the elements and of what is derived from them is seen to be otherwise. It is not because the elements are destroyed; it does not apply to the atomic elements. By viewing it as the ceasing of shape, they fall into the doctrine of the Sāṃkhyas.

"Here, what is called the impermanence of shape is this: for whoever holds that form itself is impermanent, it is the shape itself that is impermanent, not the elements. If the elements were impermanent, there would be no worldly conventions. If there were no worldly conventions, all things would be mere words; and because own-characteristics too are seen to arise, one would fall into the view of the Lokāyatas.

"Here, what is called the impermanence of alteration is this: form is seen to change into something else, but not the elements. It is like seeing the shape of gold and its ornaments change. The gold does not perish into anything other than the substance of gold, yet the shape of the ornament perishes. In this way and in other such ways, falling into transformation, the tīrthikas conceive views of impermanence."

Even when the elements are burned by fire, one does not burn another, because each is its own characteristic. Mahāmati, if their own characteristic were to cease to exist, the elements and the things derived from the elements would be annihilated.

Mahāmati, my teaching is neither permanent nor impermanent. Why is that? Because I do not accept external things, because I teach the three existences as mind only, and because I do not teach diverse characteristics. The distinctions of the bases of the great elements neither come into operation nor cease. The elements and what is derived from the elements do not enter into a twofold conceptuality. This is because there is no characteristic of grasped and grasper, because the two modes of conceptual activity are fully understood, because one is free from views of external things and of the nonexistence of things, and because one realizes that it is merely one's own mind. Conceptuality operates through the forming activity of conceptual thought, not through what is unformed.

Because one is free from mind, from conceptuality, from things and from the absence of things, and because one realizes that they are merely appearances of one's own mind, all phenomena, worldly, supramundane and supremely supramundane, are neither permanent nor impermanent. Because they do not understand their own conceptuality, the tīrthikas, with minds fallen into the bad views of the two extremes, conceive as impermanent everything, even the person of their discourse, which was never established in the first place.

Mahāmati, the characteristics of all phenomena that arise from conceptuality in the words of all the tīrthikas, and in the worldly, the supramundane and the supremely supramundane, are of three kinds; childish ordinary beings do not understand them.

On this it is said:

The cessation of commencement,
the change of shape and form into something other,
form and things as impermanent:
so the non-Buddhists, intoxicated, conceive.

Since the elements abide as elements themselves,
there is no destruction of things.
Because they are sunk in diverse views,
non-Buddhists conceive impermanence.

For the non-Buddhists, in nothing at all
is there destruction, nor is there arising.
If the things that are elements and derived from them are permanent,
what is it they conceive as impermanent?

All of these are mind only.
As the things that are grasped and grasping,
the mind operates in two modes;
there is no self, nor anything belonging to a self, at all.

From Brahmā and the rest up to the abodes,
I have explained all as mind only.
Without realizing mind alone,
Brahmā and the rest are not established.

This is the third chapter, on impermanence, from the Descent into Laṅkā.

Then once again the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, please explain to me the characteristic of the sequence and continuity of cessation of all bodhisattvas, śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, so that, being skilled in the characteristic of the sequence and continuity of cessation, I and the other bodhisattva great beings will not be confused at the gateway to entering absorption in the bliss of cessation, and will not fall into the confusion of the śrāvakas, the pratyekabuddhas and the tīrthikas."

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, listen very well and keep it in mind, and I shall explain it to you."

Saying, "Excellent, Blessed One," the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, listened to the Blessed One, and the Blessed One spoke to him thus:

"Mahāmati, from the sixth ground onward, bodhisattva great beings, śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas enter absorption in cessation. On the seventh ground, moment of mind by moment of mind, bodhisattva great beings enter absorption by removing the characteristic of the essential nature of all things; but śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas do not. The absorption in cessation of those śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas falls into the characteristic of grasped and grasper, accompanied by forming activity. Therefore, since it would not be fitting for them to attain the characteristic of non-differentiation of all phenomena and the absence of diverse characteristics, on the seventh ground absorption is entered moment of mind by moment of mind. Their absorption is one in which the specific characteristics of all phenomena, wholesome and unwholesome, are not understood. Therefore, on the seventh ground they have no skill in absorption moment of mind by moment of mind, by which absorption would be entered.

"Mahāmati, on the eighth ground the conceptuality and perception of the mind, of mentation and of mental consciousness of bodhisattvas, śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas cease. On the first ground and on the sixth, they directly perceive that the three realms are merely mind, mentation and mental consciousness, devoid of self and what belongs to a self, arising from one's own conceptuality, and not fallen into the diverse characteristics of external things. Otherwise, the very mind of the childish turns into two modes, as things grasped and grasping; those imbued since beginningless time with the habitual tendencies of the negative tendencies of conceptual elaboration do not understand this, because of their ignorance.

"Mahāmati, on the eighth ground is the nirvāṇa of śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas and bodhisattvas. The bodhisattvas, however, are held back from that bliss of samādhi by the buddhas of samādhi, so that they do not pass fully into nirvāṇa. If they were not held back, then, because the ground of the Tathāgata would not be fulfilled, the work for sentient beings would be cut off, and the lineage of the tathāgata family would be cut off as well. Therefore those buddhas, the blessed ones, also reveal the inconceivable greatness of buddhahood, and for that reason the bodhisattvas do not pass fully into nirvāṇa. Śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, however, are carried away by the bliss of samādhi, and therefore they come to hold the notion that it is nirvāṇa."

"Mahāmati, I have set forth the following for the bodhisattva mahāsattvas on the seventh ground, for it would not be right that, not knowing the specific and general characteristics and being unskilled in the sequence of the grounds and in their connections, they should fall into the views and paths of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas). I have set forth skill in becoming familiar with the characteristics of mind, mentation, and mental consciousness; familiarity with the grasping at self and at what belongs to self, with the selflessness of phenomena and of persons, with engaging and turning back, and with specific and general characteristics; skill in the analytical knowledges, free of doubt; the bliss of delight in the masteries; entry into the sequence of the grounds; and the analysis of the factors conducive to awakening. For that reason I have established the arrangement of the sequence of the grounds. Yet, Mahāmati, in this there is no engaging or turning back whatsoever.

"Rather, it is like this: this sequence of the grounds and their connection, the triple realm designated in its variety, is merely the appearance of one's own mind, and the childish do not comprehend it. Because the childish do not comprehend it, I and those buddhas have established this triple realm designated in its variety as the teaching on the connection of the sequence of the grounds.

"Furthermore, Mahāmati, on the eighth bodhisattva ground the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas are intoxicated with the intoxication of the bliss of the attainment of cessation. Being unskilled in the mere appearance of their own mind, they fall into the obscuring habitual tendencies of specific and general characteristics and into the view that grasps at the selflessness of persons and of phenomena, and so with regard to that conceptuality they come to have the mind that takes it to be nirvāṇa. They do not have the mind devoted to the Dharma of utter solitude.

"Mahāmati, the bodhisattvas, having seen the door of the bliss of the meditative absorption of cessation, are endowed with their former aspirations, with love, and with compassion, and they know the analysis of what it is to enter the final state; therefore they do not pass into complete nirvāṇa. Yet because conceptuality does not operate in them, they are also in nirvāṇa. Their conceptuality of apprehended and apprehender has ceased. Because they comprehend all phenomena as merely their own mind, conceptuality does not operate in them; and yet those who are free from the conceptuality that clings to the essential nature and the characteristics of mind, mentation, mental consciousness, and external things are not ones who fail to engage for the sake of the qualities of a buddha. For the sake of the personal realization of the Tathāgata's own state, with wisdom going before, they engage, like a person crossing a river in a dream.

"It is like this, Mahāmati. Someone asleep, in a dream, is crossing a great river with effort and great exertion, and before he has crossed it he wakes up. Having woken, he wonders, 'Is this true, or is it false?' And he reflects: 'This is neither true nor false. Rather, I fell into the forms and shapes of the various habitual tendencies of conceptuality arising from what was seen, heard, discerned, cognized, and experienced, and with a mental consciousness that had abandoned the conceptualizing of existence and nonexistence I saw appearances in the dream I experienced.'

"In the same way, Mahāmati, the bodhisattva mahāsattvas too, on the eighth bodhisattva ground, see that conceptuality does not operate; they see the passage from the first ground up to the seventh and the realization of all phenomena; they see their exertion regarding all phenomena as being like illusions and so forth; and they see the movement of the conceptuality of mind and mental factors that is free from the conceptuality of apprehended and apprehender. Having seen this, they apply themselves thoroughly to the qualities of a buddha. Mahāmati, this is the practice of bodhisattvas for the sake of nirvāṇa, by which what was not realized comes to be realized; it is not destruction. Because mind, mentation, mental consciousness, conceptuality, and perception have become absent, they attain acceptance of the nonarising of phenomena. Mahāmati, since this teaches the Dharma that is free of appearances and isolated from conceptuality, in the ultimate there is no sequence and no connection of the sequence."

On this it is said:

Mind only and the imageless,
The grounds of the buddhas and the abodes:
These have been taught by the buddhas,
Are taught, and will be taught.

The seven grounds are mind;
Here the imageless is the eighth;
Here two grounds are abodes;
The remaining ground is my own nature.

Selfhood and personal self-awareness:
This ground is my own nature.
Supreme among the abodes of Maheśvara,
Akaniṣṭha shines in utter splendor.

Just as from a fire,
Its many-colored rays
Stream forth, pleasing and peaceful;
They emanate the three existences.

Some of the three existences they emanate now,
Some were emanated before;
There the vehicles are taught;
That is the ground of my own nature.

The tenth is the first;
The first is the eighth;
The ninth is the seventh;
The seventh is the eighth;
The second is the third;
The fourth is the fifth;
The third is the sixth:
In the imageless, what order is there?

This is the fourth chapter, on Realization.

Then once again the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, is the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, permanent, or is he impermanent?"

The Blessed One said: "Mahāmati, the Tathāgata is neither permanent nor impermanent. Why is that? Because in both cases a fault would follow. Mahāmati, the fault that follows in both cases is this: if he were permanent, he would become a cause. Mahāmati, the causes of all the tīrthikas are unmade and permanent; therefore, since the unmade is permanent, the Tathāgata is not permanent. If he were impermanent, he would be made and impermanent. Since the aggregates, the characteristic, and the basis of the characteristic would be absent, with the destruction of the aggregates he would be annihilated; but the Tathāgata is not annihilated."

Mahāmati, everything that is made is impermanent. Like a pot, cloth, a mat, grass, wood, and brick, all of these become impermanent. Since he would then be something made, the accumulations for omniscient wisdom would also become pointless. And since there would be no distinguishing reason, all childish beings would also become tathāgatas. Therefore, Mahāmati, the Tathāgata is neither permanent nor impermanent.

Moreover, Mahāmati, the Tathāgata is not permanent. Why is that? Because, as with space, the accumulations would become pointless. For example, Mahāmati, space is neither permanent nor impermanent. Since it is free of permanence and impermanence, it cannot be described in terms of the faults of oneness, otherness, both, neither, permanence, or impermanence.

Moreover, Mahāmati, if he were permanent by being unborn, he would be equal to the horns of a hare, a horse, a donkey, a camel, a frog, a snake, a bird, or a fish. Because what is unborn would then be permanent, the Tathāgata is not permanent either.

Yet, Mahāmati, there is also a sense in which the Tathāgata is permanent. Why is that? Because the wisdom that has fully comprehended realization is permanent, the Tathāgata is permanent. Mahāmati, the wisdom by which the tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly complete buddhas, have fully comprehended realization is permanent. Whether tathāgatas appear or do not appear, this nature of reality, this immutability of phenomena, this abiding of phenomena, remains in all the realizations of śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas, and tīrthikas. Though, as with space, the abiding of phenomena does not change, childish ordinary beings do not understand it.

Mahāmati, the wisdom comprehended by the tathāgatas is brought forth by discernment and primordial wisdom. Mahāmati, the Tathāgata is not brought forth by mind, mentation, mental consciousness, the aggregates, the elements, the sense bases, or the habitual tendencies of ignorance. Mahāmati, the whole of the three realms of existence arises from unreal conceptualization, but the tathāgatas do not arise from unreal imagining.

Mahāmati, where there is duality, there are permanence and impermanence, but not where there is nonduality. Mahāmati, since all phenomena are nondual and have the characteristic of being unborn, they are void of duality. For this reason, Mahāmati, the tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly complete buddhas, are neither permanent nor impermanent. Mahāmati, as long as verbal conceptuality operates, so long does the fault of permanence and impermanence arise. Mahāmati, by the exhaustion of the conceptualizing mind, the view of childish beings concerning permanence and impermanence is turned back, but not through the exhaustion of the mind of seclusion.

On this it is said:

Free from permanence and impermanence,
Yet brought forth as permanent and impermanent:
Those who always see the buddhas thus
Do not fall under the sway of views.

With permanence and impermanence,
The perfect accumulation would be pointless.
Through the absence of the conceptualizing mind,
Impermanence and permanence are turned back.

As long as there is any assertion,
So long is everything confused.
Look upon it as one's own mind only,
And do not embark on disputes.

This is the fifth chapter, called "Whether the Tathāgata Is Permanent or Impermanent."

Then again the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, made a request to the Blessed One: "Please, Blessed One, explain to me the coming into operation and the ceasing of the aggregates, the elements, and the sense bases. Please, Sugata, explain it to me. If there is no self, what is it that comes into operation or ceases? Childish beings, abiding in coming into operation and ceasing, do not understand the exhaustion of suffering, and therefore do not know nirvāṇa."

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, then listen very well and hold it in mind, and I shall explain it to you."

"Excellent, Blessed One," Mahāmati the bodhisattva, the great being, replied, and he listened to the Blessed One. The Blessed One then spoke to him as follows:

"Mahāmati, the tathāgatagarbha, which is the cause of virtue and non-virtue, which produces all births and destinies, and which is free of self and what belongs to a self, enters perilous destinies like a dancer. Because this is not understood, when it operates together with the activity of conditions, the conjunction of the three, the non-Buddhists, who are attached and cling to causes, do not understand it.

"Perfumed by the many kinds of habitual tendencies of the dysfunction of conceptual elaboration from beginningless time, and called the all-base consciousness, it operates together with the seven consciousnesses born of karma through the ground of ignorance, like the waves of the great ocean: constantly, its body unbroken in continuity, free from the fault of impermanence, turned away from the doctrine of a self, utterly pure in its intrinsic nature.

"The other consciousnesses, which arise and perish, the seven beginning with mentation and mental consciousness, are momentary. Born from the cause of unreal imagining, they take as their object the activity and particulars of shapes; they are attached and cling to names and signs; they do not comprehend that forms and characteristics are appearances of their own mind; they do not experience pleasure and pain; they are not a cause of liberation; and they take as their object the cause of what is stirred up and produced by names, signs, and the desire that fully arises.

"When those called the appropriated faculties are completely exhausted and have ceased, then, because they do not arise again immediately afterward and others also do not come into operation, the yogins who have entered the meditative attainment of the cessation of perception and feeling, in which the pleasure and pain conceived by their own minds are not experienced, and who are skilled in the four dhyānas, the truths, and the liberations, come to take this for liberation.

If the all-base consciousness, which is proclaimed as the tathāgatagarbha, has not been transformed, the seven active consciousnesses do not cease. Why is that? Because the consciousnesses proceed with it as their cause and with it as their object, and because it is not the domain of any of the yogins in the yoga of the śrāvakas, the pratyekabuddhas and the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas). Through realizing the selflessness of persons, and through grasping the specific and general characteristics of the aggregates, the elements and the sense sources, the tathāgatagarbha proceeds. Through seeing the five dharmas, the intrinsic natures and the selflessness of phenomena, it turns back once it is transformed through the successive progression of the grounds, and it cannot be shaken by the views of the other, non-Buddhist paths.

Therefore one who is firmly established on the bodhisattva ground called Immovable attains the ten paths that are gateways of the truth of samādhi. Being upheld by the buddhas of samādhi, and therefore contemplating the inconceivable qualities of a buddha and his own aspirations, he turns the gateway of samādhi back from the limit of reality. Then, by the path of yoga that is to be known through the noble individual self-awareness and is not shared with any of the śrāvakas, the pratyekabuddhas or the tīrthikas, he attains the ten paths of the noble lineage, wisdom, the mind-made body, and a samādhi free of deliberate effort.

Therefore, Mahāmati, bodhisattva mahāsattvas who seek distinction should purify the tathāgatagarbha that is proclaimed as the all-base consciousness. Mahāmati, if the tathāgatagarbha proclaimed as the all-base consciousness did not exist, then, Mahāmati, since there would be no tathāgatagarbha proclaimed as the all-base consciousness, there would be neither proceeding nor turning back. Yet, Mahāmati, for childish beings and for noble ones there is both proceeding and turning back, and yogins who do not give up their diligence abide in happiness in this very life through their own individual self-awareness.

Mahāmati, this sphere of the tathāgatagarbha, the all-base consciousness, is hard to comprehend for śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas and non-Buddhists who hold speculative views. Although it is completely pure by nature, it appears to them as though impure, because it is defiled by adventitious afflictions. It is not so for the tathāgatas. Mahāmati, for the tathāgatas it becomes a domain of direct perception, like a myrobalan fruit in the palm of the hand.

Mahāmati, this very point was set forth in teaching for the sake of Queen Śrīmālā, and for the sake of other bodhisattvas who are learned and whose intellect is pure, subtle and vast: the tathāgatagarbha proclaimed as the all-base consciousness, together with the seven consciousnesses. In order to teach the selflessness of phenomena to the śrāvakas who are attached to the proceeding of consciousness, the domain of the Tathāgata was explained for the sake of Queen Śrīmālā. It is not a domain to be realized by the śrāvakas, the pratyekabuddhas or the other tīrthikas.

Otherwise, Mahāmati, the domain of the tathāgatagarbha, the all-base consciousness, is the domain of the tathāgatas. It is also the domain of bodhisattva mahāsattvas like you, whose inclinations are subtle and learned, who discern with their intellect, and who rely on the meaning. It is not that of the other non-Buddhists, nor of any of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, who are attached to the teachings just as they sound. Therefore, Mahāmati, you and the other bodhisattva mahāsattvas should apply yourselves to this tathāgatagarbha, the all-base consciousness, the domain of all the tathāgatas, which is to be fully understood. You should not be satisfied with merely hearing of it.

On this it is said:

The tathāgatagarbha,
Even when joined with the seven consciousnesses,
Proceeds by way of the twofold grasping,
And through full understanding it turns back.

Mind appears like a reflection,
Imprinted from beginningless time;
When one examines it exactly as it is,
Though it resembles objects, there are no objects.

Just as a child grasps at the fingertip
And does not look at the moon,
So those attached to letters
Do not know my true reality.

Mind abides like a dancer,
Mentation is like a jester;
Consciousness, together with the five,
Imagines appearances like colors.

The Noble Descent into Laṅkā, the seventh fascicle.

Then again the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati made this request of the Blessed One: "Blessed One, please explain to me the characteristic of realizing the distinctions of the five dharmas, the intrinsic natures, the consciousnesses and the two selflessnesses. It is the characteristic of realizing the distinctions of the two selflessnesses through which I and all the other bodhisattva mahāsattvas will cultivate these dharmas in their successive stages, through which by every means we will enter into all the qualities of a buddha, and through which, having entered into all the qualities of a buddha, we will enter as far as the Tathāgata's own ground of individual self-awareness. Sugata, please explain it to me."

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, then listen very well and keep it in mind, and I shall explain it to you."

Saying, "Excellent, Blessed One," the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati listened to the Blessed One, and the Blessed One spoke to him thus:

"Mahāmati, I shall explain to you the characteristic of realizing the distinctions of the five dharmas, the intrinsic natures, the consciousnesses and the two selflessnesses. They are name, sign, conceptuality, correct wisdom and suchness. This is the entry into the Tathāgata's noble individual self-awareness. It abandons the views of permanence and annihilation, of existence and nonexistence. It is entry into absorption as the gateway to happiness in this very life, an abiding in happiness that becomes directly evident to yogins."

"Here, Mahāmati, it is because they do not understand the five dharmas, the natures, the consciousnesses, the two kinds of selflessness, their own mind, external things, and the absence of things that the conceptuality of childish beings operates. That of the noble ones does not."

Mahāmati asked, "Blessed One, how is it, then, that the conceptuality of childish beings operates and that of the noble ones does not?"

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, because they are attached to names, perceptions and conventional signs, childish beings follow after the mind. Following after it, they hold that there are various characteristics. Their thinking has fallen into the view of a self and of what belongs to a self, and so they become strongly attached to the rich profusion of colors. Once attached, and obscured by ignorance, they become intensely passionate. Being intensely passionate, they create karma born of attachment, aversion and delusion. Having created it, again and again, like silkworms, their minds become wrapped in their own conceptuality. They fall into the ocean and the wilderness of the destinies, and like a waterwheel they never pass beyond them.

Because of delusion, they do not know the following. All phenomena are like illusions, mirages and the moon in water. They are devoid of a self and of what belongs to a self. They arise from unreal conceptuality. They are free of any basis for characteristics and free of signs. They are free from the occurrence of arising, abiding and destruction. They arise from the conceptuality of one's own mind. They do not arise from Īśvara, from time, from atoms, or from the principal (pradhāna).

Mahāmati, because they are distracted by names and signs, childish beings conform to signs and so pursue signs.

Here, Mahāmati, a sign is whatever appears to visual consciousness, which is called form. In the same way, for the ear, nose, tongue, body and mental consciousnesses there are what are called sound, smell, taste, touch and mental phenomena. This is what is explained as sign.

Here, Mahāmati, conceptuality is that by which one expresses things in names and says, 'This is an elephant, a horse, a chariot, a foot soldier, a man, a woman, and so forth. It is just so, and not otherwise.' Whatever makes signs distinct in this way is conceptuality.

Mahāmati, correct wisdom is that by which names and signs are not apprehended. Because they are mutually adventitious, consciousness does not operate. Because it is free of permanence and free of annihilation, it does not let one fall to the level of any tīrthikas, śrāvakas or pratyekabuddhas. For this reason it is called correct wisdom.

Moreover, Mahāmati, by that correct wisdom the bodhisattva mahāsattva does not take names to be things. Nor does he take signs to be the absence of things. Such a knowing has abandoned the wrong views of the two extremes of superimposition and denial, and knows that the meaning of names and signs does not operate. That, it is explained, is this suchness.

Mahāmati, the bodhisattva mahāsattva who abides in suchness reaches the domain of a realization free of appearances, and so attains the bodhisattva ground called the Joyous ground. Once he has attained the bodhisattva ground called the Joyous ground, he has turned away from all non-Buddhists and from every destiny of the lower realms. He has truly entered into the realization of the transcendent dharmas. He is free of curiosity about the views of the logicians, and because he is familiar with the characteristics, he cultivates the approach to the Dharma that is led by the understanding that things are like illusions and the like. Step by step, up to the Cloud of Dharma ground, he attains the characteristic of the dharma that is his own noble, individually realized wisdom.

After the Cloud of Dharma ground, the flowers of the samādhis, powers, masteries and superknowledges come into full bloom, and he attains everything up to the ground of the Tathāgata. Having attained this, he is beautifully adorned with the many rays of light of his emanations, which he sends out in order to bring sentient beings to maturity. Like the moon in water, his nature wholly bound up with the limitless state, he teaches the Dharma to sentient beings of differing inclinations.

Mahāmati, because bodhisattva mahāsattvas have entered suchness, they also attain a body free of mind and cognition."

Again Mahāmati asked, "Blessed One, are the three natures included within the five dharmas, or are they established by their own characteristics?"

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, within these very five are included the three natures, the eight consciousnesses and the two kinds of selflessness. Here, name and sign should be known as the imputed nature.

Mahāmati, there is the conceptuality that rests on these and operates through them. It is proclaimed as mind and mental factors, and these arise at one and the same time, like the sun together with its rays. Its nature is one's own conceptuality, which grasps the various characteristics. That, Mahāmati, is called the dependent.

Mahāmati, correct wisdom and suchness are indestructible, and so they should be known as the perfected nature.

Moreover, Mahāmati, through attachment to what appears from one's own mind, conceptuality is divided into eight, because one imputes the characteristic of unreal signs. When the two kinds of grasping, at a self and at what belongs to a self, are pacified, the two kinds of selflessness arise.

Mahāmati, within these five dharmas are included all the qualities of a buddha and the connections that distinguish the grounds. Included too are the ways in which śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas, bodhisattvas and tathāgatas enter into the realization of their own noble, individually realized wisdom.

Moreover, Mahāmati, the five dharmas are sign, name, conceptuality, suchness and correct wisdom. Here, Mahāmati, what is sign? Whatever appears with the characteristics of shape, aspect, distinguishing feature, appearance, form and so forth, that is sign.

That very sign, when it is designated as "pot" and so forth, with the thought, "This is thus and not otherwise," is name. Mahāmati, that by which one utters that name, saying "pleasant" or "armor," and makes the sign clear, and which is proclaimed as mind and mental factors, is conceptual thought. Because these phenomena are not experienced by one another and are not imputed either, the utter non-apprehension of name and sign, up to the complete dissolution of the intellect, is suchness. It is the real, the perfectly ascertained, the ultimate, the intrinsic nature, the essential nature, the unapprehended. As the characteristic of suchness, it has been realized by me and by the other tathāgatas as well, and it has been explained just as it is, designated, analyzed, and made clear. That by which one comprehends and truly realizes, which does not enter into conceptual thought because it is neither annihilation nor permanence, which accords with the noble wisdom realized by oneself, and which is not the domain of those on the opposing side, the tīrthikas, nor of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas: that is right wisdom. Mahāmati, these are the five dharmas. Within these very ones are included the three natures, the eight consciousnesses, the two kinds of selflessness, and all the qualities of a buddha. Mahāmati, you should therefore become skilled in this through your own intelligence. You should also lead others to do the same, and you should not depend on others.

On this it is said:

The five dharmas, the natures,
The eight consciousnesses themselves,
And the two kinds of selflessness:
In these the whole Great Vehicle is contained.
Name, sign, and imputation
Are the characteristic of two natures;
Right wisdom and suchness
Are the characteristic of the perfected.

Then once again the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, said this to the Blessed One: "In the discourses the Blessed One has given, he says that the tathāgatas of the past, the future, and the present are as numerous as the sands of the river Ganges. Blessed One, should this be taken in its literal meaning, or is there some other particular meaning? Blessed One, please explain this."

The Blessed One said: "Mahāmati, it should not be taken in its literal meaning. Mahāmati, the measure of the sands of the river Ganges does not even come to the measure of the buddhas of a single one of the three times. Why is that? Mahāmati, it is like this: since they are both similar and dissimilar to what transcends the highest in the world, the example is no example. Mahāmati, the tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly complete buddhas do not present an example that is dissimilar as though it were similar to what transcends the highest in the world. Mahāmati, it is otherwise. It is in order to make weary the childish ordinary people who are attached to permanence and impermanence, who are ruined by the evil views of the tīrthikas, and who follow the wheel of saṃsāric existence, so that these beings of existence may grow weary of that frightful wheel of existence and, longing for something higher, set out toward what is higher; and it is to show that attaining buddhahood is easy, so that they will take up diligence, thinking, 'The birth of tathāgatas is like the udumbara flower.' Therefore this saying, 'tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly complete buddhas as numerous as the sands of the river Ganges,' is set forth merely as an example.

"In my discourses, having looked upon the beings to be trained, I did say, 'The appearance of tathāgatas is as hard to find as the udumbara flower.' But, Mahāmati, the udumbara flower has never been seen by anyone before, nor will it be seen, whereas, Mahāmati, the tathāgatas are seen in the world and appear even now. When speaking in the context of a talk that establishes my own principle on its own terms, I did not say that the appearance of tathāgatas is as hard to find as the udumbara flower. Mahāmati, when one gives a talk establishing one's own principle on its own terms, one does not use fitting examples for what transcends the highest in the world, for since that would not be credible, childish ordinary people would not come to have faith.

"Because reality transcends mind, mentation, and mental consciousness, examples do not apply to the domain of the noble wisdom realized by oneself. Reality is the tathāgatas, and for that reason examples should not be set up for them. Nevertheless, Mahāmati, this is given merely as an example: the tathāgatas are equal to the sands of the river Ganges. Since they neither think nor conceptualize, they are equal, without any inequality.

"Mahāmati, it is like this. The sands of the river Ganges, even when they are stirred up by fish, turtles, crocodiles, humans, buffalo, lions, elephants, horses, and so forth, do not think or conceptualize, 'We are stirred up,' or, 'We are not stirred up.' They are clear and free of stains, without conceptual thought. In the same way, Mahāmati, the noble wisdom realized by oneself of the tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly complete buddhas is the river Ganges, and its sands of powers, masteries, and superknowledges, even when stirred up by all the fish of the childish tīrthikas and opposing disputants, do not think or conceptualize, because of the aspirations the tathāgatas made in the past. Because they have perfected all the blissful meditative absorptions, they do not think or conceptualize about sentient beings. Therefore those tathāgatas, being without affection or anger, are equal to the sands of the river Ganges; there is no difference.

"Mahāmati, it is like this. The sands of the river Ganges have the nature of the characteristic of earth, so even when they are burned at the burning of the eon, they do not abandon the nature of earth. Mahāmati, earth does not burn, because it is bound up with the element of fire. Childish ordinary people, with minds fallen into error, imagine otherwise, that it burns; but since it has come about from the cause of fire, it does not burn. In the same way, Mahāmati, the dharma body of the tathāgatas is also like the sands of the river Ganges: it is indestructible.

For example, Mahāmati, the sands of the river Ganges are measureless. Likewise, Mahāmati, the measureless rays of light of the tathāgatas are sent forth by the tathāgatas into the maṇḍalas of the assemblies of all the buddhas, in order to urge sentient beings toward full maturation.

For example, Mahāmati, the sands of the river Ganges do not turn into some other nature; sands remain in the very state of sand. Likewise, Mahāmati, for the tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly complete buddhas, the cause of entering any state of existence has been cut off. Therefore there is no entering into saṃsāra for them, and no turning back from it either.

For example, Mahāmati, when sand is taken from the river Ganges, no decrease is apparent, and when sand is added, no increase is apparent. Likewise, Mahāmati, because the Dharma has no body, the wisdom of the Tathāgata neither diminishes nor grows through the work of bringing sentient beings to maturity. Mahāmati, the Dharma has no body. What has a body is subject to destruction, but what has no body is not.

For example, Mahāmati, though one who wants butter and oil may press the sands of the river Ganges hard, there is no butter or oil in them. Likewise, Mahāmati, though the tathāgatas are sorely afflicted by the sufferings of sentient beings, because they possess great compassion, the tathāgatas do not give up the bliss of their aspiration, which rests in the very nature of the dharmadhātu, until all sentient beings have been brought to nirvāṇa.

For example, Mahāmati, the sands of the river Ganges flow in keeping with the current that carries them; they do not flow where there is no water. Likewise, Mahāmati, all the teachings of the Dharma given by the tathāgatas proceed in keeping with the current that flows to nirvāṇa. For this reason the tathāgatas are said to be equal to the sands of the river Ganges.

Mahāmati, this meaning of going does not apply to the tathāgatas. Mahāmati, to go forth is the meaning of going; and, Mahāmati, the first limit of saṃsāra is not known. If it is not known, how can one speak in terms of going? Mahāmati, while the meaning of going is annihilation, childish ordinary beings do not understand this.

Mahāmati asked, "Blessed One, if the first limit of saṃsāra is not known for sentient beings, how then is the liberation of living beings known?"

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, through fully understanding external objects as the appearance of one's own mind, the cause, namely the habitual tendencies of conceptuality that grasp at the negative states, the elaborations of beginningless time, is reversed, and one's own basis is thoroughly transformed. That is liberation, Mahāmati; it is not destruction. Therefore, Mahāmati, talk of endlessness serves no purpose at all. Mahāmati, this so-called end of the endless is a synonym for conceptuality. When one examines it thoroughly with intelligence, there is no other being, outer or inner, apart from conceptuality. Mahāmati, all dharmas are void of knower and known. Otherwise, because conceptuality is not fully understood as the appearance of one's own mind, conceptuality goes on operating; when it is realized, it comes to an end."

On this it is said:

Those who truly see the Guide
as equal to the Ganges' sand,
perfected in the way of the imperishable,
they are the ones who see the Guide.

Just as the sands of the Ganges
are free of every fault,
flow along the current and are constant,
so are the buddhas, buddhahood itself.

Then again the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, Sugata, please explain to me the destruction of all dharmas through their momentary change. Tathāgata, Arhat, Perfectly Complete Buddha, please explain it to me. Blessed One, in what way are all dharmas momentary?"

The Blessed One said, "Then, Mahāmati, listen very well and keep it in mind, and I shall explain it to you."

Saying, "Excellent, Blessed One," the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati listened to the Blessed One, and the Blessed One spoke to him thus:

"Mahāmati, what are called all dharmas, all dharmas, are these: the virtuous and the nonvirtuous, the conditioned and the unconditioned, the mundane and the supramundane, the blameworthy and the blameless, the defiled and the undefiled, the appropriated and the unappropriated. Mahāmati, in brief, the five aggregates of grasping, which arise from the cause, the habitual tendencies of mind, mentation, and mental consciousness, are imagined as virtuous and nonvirtuous by childish ordinary beings in whom the habitual tendencies of mind, mentation, and mental consciousness have increased. Mahāmati, the bliss of samādhi and the meditative attainments, being the basis for dwelling happily in this very life, are called the undefiled virtue of the noble ones.

"Further, Mahāmati, the virtuous and the nonvirtuous are the eight consciousnesses. If one asks what the eight are, they are: what is proclaimed as the tathāgatagarbha, the all-base consciousness; mentation; mental consciousness; and the five groups of consciousness spoken of by the tīrthikas. Mahāmati, among these, the five groups of consciousness together with mental consciousness, distinguished as virtuous and nonvirtuous by their division into one moment after another, arise through the continuity of their stream as an undivided body. Having arisen, they perish. Because the appearance of one's own mind is not realized, immediately upon cessation another consciousness arises: the mental consciousness that grasps the particulars of shape and form arises. It arises together with the five groups of consciousness. What does not abide even for the duration of a moment, that I call momentary."

"Mahāmati, the all-base consciousness, which is proclaimed as the tathāgatagarbha, together with the mind, is momentary by virtue of the habitual tendencies of the engaging consciousnesses. By virtue of the uncontaminated habitual tendencies, however, it is not momentary. Childish ordinary beings who are attached to the doctrine of momentariness do not understand this momentariness and non-momentariness of all phenomena. Because they do not understand it, their nihilistic view destroys even the unconditioned phenomena.

"Mahāmati, the five groups of consciousness are not what transmigrates in saṃsāra. They do not experience pleasure and pain, and they are not the cause of nirvāṇa. Mahāmati, the tathāgatagarbha does not itself experience pleasure and pain, yet it is accompanied by their cause. Being bound up with the four habitual tendencies, it proceeds and turns back. Childish beings whose intellects are imbued with the conceptual thought of the view of momentariness do not understand this.

"Furthermore, Mahāmati, gold, the vajra, the relics of the Victor, attainment and the special qualities are indestructible. Mahāmati, if the attainment of realization were momentary, the noble ones would become ignoble. But the noble ones do not become ignoble. Mahāmati, gold and the vajra remain just as they are even when measured over eons, neither diminishing nor increasing. How, then, can childish beings who are unskilled in the intended meaning of the teaching regard all outer and inner phenomena as momentary?"

Again Mahāmati asked, "The Blessed One has said, 'If one brings the six perfections to completion, one will attain buddhahood.' What are these perfections, and how are they brought to completion?"

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, there are three kinds of perfection. What are the three? They are the worldly, the supramundane, and the supreme supramundane.

"Mahāmati, among these, as for the worldly perfections: those who are attached to and fixated on grasping at self and what is mine, who grasp at the two extremes, and who desire objects such as forms for the sake of various places of birth in existence, bring the perfection of generosity to completion. In the same way, Mahāmati, childish beings bring to completion the perfections of moral discipline, patience, diligence, meditative concentration and wisdom, and they also accomplish the superknowledges in order to be born as Brahmā.

"As for the supramundane perfections: just as childish beings desire nirvāṇa as their own happiness, śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, whose intentions have fallen into the desire for nirvāṇa, apply themselves to generosity and the rest.

"Mahāmati, as for the supreme supramundane perfections: for the bodhisattva mahāsattvas, yogins in the sublime yoga, the perfection of generosity arises for the benefit and happiness of all sentient beings through their apprehending things as merely the appearance of their own mind, understanding duality as their own mind, conceptual thought not engaging, having no grasping at appropriation, and not being attached to the characteristics of their own mind and of form.

"Meditating so that conceptual thought does not engage with whatever is taken as an object: that is moral discipline, and that is also its perfection.

"The patience by which, through fully understanding the grasped and the grasper, that very conceptual thought does not engage: that is the perfection of patience.

"The diligence by which one strives in the first and last parts of the night, and through which, by seeing in accordance with yoga, conceptual thought ceases: that is the perfection of diligence.

"That by which, because conceptual thought has been turned back, one does not fall into grasping at the nirvāṇa of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas): that is the perfection of meditative concentration.

"As for the perfection of wisdom: when there is no conceptual thought of one's own mind, and when one analyzes all the way to intellectual discernment, one does not fall into the two extremes; and through the transformation of the basis that goes before being indestructible, one applies oneself to attaining one's own noble, individually self-aware knowledge. That is the perfection of wisdom.

"Mahāmati, these are the perfections, and this is the meaning of perfection."

On this it is said:

"Empty, impermanent and momentary:
So the conditioned is imagined as produced.
By the examples of a river, a lamp and a seed,
It is imagined to mean momentariness.

Being without an agent, being momentary,
Being isolated, free from activity,
And the non-arising of phenomena:
These I have taught as the meaning of momentary.

That things perish as soon as they arise
I do not teach even to the childish.
The conceptual thought of entities
Moves without interruption through the realms of rebirth.

The cause that brings that mind into being
Is ignorance.
As long as forms are not known,
Does it remain for just that long?

As soon as it has perished,
Other minds arise.
If form does not endure through time,
Taking what as its object would they arise?

The mind that moves from this to that
Arises from a mistaken cause.
If that is not truly established,
How can it be known to perish in an instant?

The meditative absorptions of yogins,
Gold and the relics of the Victor,
The celestial palace of the Ābhāsvara abode:
These are not destroyed by worldly causes.

The things that endure, the Dharma that is heard,
The perfection of the buddhas' wisdom,
Monkhood, and those who have attained knowledge:
How can these be seen as momentary?

How are illusions, cities of the gandharvas and the like,
And forms momentary?
Forms that are not made of the elements,
And those elements: where have they gone?"

This concludes the sixth chapter, on momentariness.

Then again the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati said this to the Blessed One:

"Why did the Blessed One (the Buddha) prophesy that arhats will attain unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, and also that beings whose nature is never to attain final nirvāṇa will become tathāgatas? From the night on which the Tathāgata awoke fully into unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening until the night on which he passed into final nirvāṇa, the Blessed One did not speak or teach a single letter. The tathāgatas always remain in meditative equipoise, and without conceptualizing or analyzing they send forth emanations, and these perform the deeds of a tathāgata. The consciousnesses, too, are explained as having characteristics that differ from one instant to the next. Vajrapāṇi always follows close around him as his attendant. The beginning limit is not posited, yet nirvāṇa is posited. There are Māra and the deeds of Māra, the causes of karma, and karmic obscurations such as the brahmin girl Ciñcā, Sundarī the wandering ascetic, the alms bowl that came back as though washed clean, and so forth. If these are so, Blessed One, how did the Blessed One attain omniscience without having abandoned these faults?"

The Blessed One said, "Mahāmati, therefore listen very well and keep it in mind, and I shall explain it to you."

"Excellent, Blessed One," said the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, and he listened to the Blessed One. The Blessed One then spoke to him as follows:

"It was with the realm of nirvāṇa without any remainder of the aggregates in mind, and in order to encourage those who practice the conduct of a bodhisattva. Mahāmati, in this buddha field and in others there are those practicing bodhisattva conduct who wish to attain final nirvāṇa by the vehicle of the śrāvakas. In order to turn them back from their wish for the śrāvaka vehicle and to inspire them toward the Great Vehicle, emanation bodies prophesied to emanated śrāvakas; it was not done by the buddhas of dharmatā. Mahāmati, it was with this in mind that śrāvakas were prophesied.

"Mahāmati, because liberation has a single taste, there is no difference as regards the abandonment of the afflictive obscuration by śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas; but this is not so as regards the abandonment of the cognitive obscuration. Mahāmati, the cognitive obscuration is purified through the distinctive seeing of the selflessness of phenomena. The afflictive obscuration is purified through the distinctive seeing of the selflessness of persons. The afflictive obscuration is abandoned, because mental consciousness is turned back, by first meditating on the seeing of the selflessness of persons. Liberation from the obscuration regarding phenomena is purified because the habitual tendencies of the all-base consciousness are turned back.

"It is with the ancient way of Dharma in mind: because, being without earlier and later, it does not newly arise, the Tathāgata teaches the Dharma without conceptualizing or analyzing, by means of those very letters that were spoken before. Because he acts with full knowledge, has no lapse of mindfulness, has abandoned the ground of habitual tendencies, is free of the two, death and transmigration, and has abandoned the two obscurations, the afflictive and the cognitive, he does not conceptualize or analyze.

"Mahāmati, the seven, namely the mind, the mental consciousness, the eye consciousness and so forth, are devoid of the side of the virtuous and undefiled and do not transmigrate. Because they arise from the cause of habitual tendencies, they are momentary. Mahāmati, buddha-nature is the cause of transmigration and of nirvāṇa, of happiness and of suffering. Childish ordinary beings, whose minds are distracted by emptiness, do not know its very purity.

"Further, Mahāmati, Vajrapāṇi stays in the presence of the tathāgatas emanated by magical emanation, but not in the presence of the actual tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and completely awakened buddhas. Mahāmati, the actual Tathāgata is beyond the epistemic instruments and the faculties of all childish ordinary beings, śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas and non-Buddhists (tīrthikas). Those who dwell in bliss in this very life realize it through the forbearance that knows the dharmas realized as directly seen. Therefore Vajrapāṇi does not follow it as its attendant.

"All the emanated buddhas do not arise from karma. There is no Tathāgata in them, and there is no Tathāgata apart from them either. Through a combination like that of a potter with his materials and so forth, they perform all activities and also display the major marks; yet they do not teach the discourse that sets forth their own way individually, the domain of noble wisdom realized individually for oneself.

"Further, Mahāmati, childish ordinary beings rely on the view of annihilation because the six groups of consciousness cease. Because they do not realize the all-base, they come to hold the view of permanence. Mahāmati, the beginning limit of the conceptuality of one's own mind cannot be known. Through turning back the very conceptuality of one's own mind, liberation too is known. Through abandoning the four habitual tendencies, all faults come to be abandoned."

On this it is said:

The three vehicles and no vehicle,
the buddhas having no nirvāṇa,
the prophecy that all will be buddhas,
the teaching that they are free from faults,
the wisdom at the culmination of realization,
and likewise the realizations without grasping:
in order to encourage the faint-hearted,
these were taught with an intention in mind.

The buddhas bring forth wisdom,
And they themselves also teach the path.
By that one goes, and not by anything else;
Therefore they do not pass into that nirvāṇa.

Through existence, desire, form and views,
Habitual tendencies become fourfold.
Arising from the mental consciousness,
They abide in the ālaya and in the mind.

The mental consciousness, the eye and the rest,
Being impermanent, are taught as annihilation.
Because they are beginningless, there comes the view of permanence,
And a view that is devoted to nirvāṇa.

Then the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati, having questioned the Blessed One in verses, again made a request to the Blessed One: "In times to come and in the present, there are sentient beings imbued with the habitual tendencies of meat-eating and the like, beings attached to the food of flesh. So that I and the other bodhisattva mahāsattvas may rid them of their craving for its taste; so that those beings, their craving for taste removed and devoted instead to the food of the taste of the Dharma, may hold all sentient beings dear as an only child and gain loving-kindness for one another; and so that, having gained it, they may be trained through the bodhisattva grounds and swiftly awaken fully to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, or else, having rested on the grounds of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, may come to attain the unsurpassed ground of the Tathāgata: for this, may the Blessed One, the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly and completely awakened Buddha, please explain to me the Dharma that shows meat-eating to be a fault.

"Blessed One, even the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), whose teachings are badly expounded, who cling to the views of the Lokāyatas, who take sides on existence and nonexistence, and who proclaim eternalism and annihilation, forbid the eating of meat and do not eat it themselves. How then could it be that in your teaching, Protector of the world, which through compassion has a single taste, and which was spoken by the perfectly and completely awakened Buddha, people eat meat themselves and do not turn others back from eating it? So that I and the other bodhisattva mahāsattvas may teach the Dharma to sentient beings as it truly is, may the Blessed One, who looks upon the whole world with love in every way as on an only child, the one possessed of great compassion, out of that love, please explain well the faults of eating meat."

The Blessed One said, "Therefore, Mahāmati, listen very well and keep it in mind, and I shall explain it to you."

Saying, "Excellent, Blessed One," the bodhisattva mahāsattva Mahāmati listened to the Blessed One, and the Blessed One spoke to him thus:

"Mahāmati, a bodhisattva whose very nature is compassion should not eat any meat, for there are countless reasons. I shall explain just a portion of them.

"Mahāmati, among living beings who have wandered in saṃsāra for a very long time, there is no way to find easily any sentient being who has not been one's father, mother, brother, sister, son, daughter, friend or relative, or someone like a relative. Those who were relatives or like relatives have changed bodies in other lives and been born as wild animals, livestock or birds, in some birthplace or another. How, then, could a bodhisattva mahāsattva who longs for the qualities of a buddha, and who wishes all beings to be as himself, eat flesh that comes from any of the creatures that have become living beings?

"Mahāmati, if even rākṣasas, on hearing this excellent nature of reality of the Dharma of the tathāgatas, turn away from eating meat, shed the nature of rākṣasas and become compassionate, what need is there to speak of people who long for the Dharma? Thus, Mahāmati, because throughout one life after another all sentient beings have been friends and relatives, and in order to cultivate, with that perception, the perception of each sentient being as an only child, a bodhisattva whose very nature is compassion does not eat any meat.

"Mahāmati, a bodhisattva endowed with right conduct does not eat any meat, both because meat is unfit to eat and because of deception. Mahāmati, although the meat of donkeys, camels, dogs, oxen, human beings and the like is not eaten by the world, butchers declare it fit to eat for the sake of money and sell it in the middle of the streets. For that reason too, Mahāmati, a bodhisattva does not eat any meat.

"Mahāmati, because he desires purity, too, a bodhisattva does not eat meat, for meat comes from semen and blood.

"Mahāmati, because it terrifies living creatures, too, a bodhisattva yogin who longs for loving-kindness should not eat any meat. For example, Mahāmati, when dogs see beings who eat dog meat, such as ḍombas, caṇḍālas and fishermen, even from far away, they are seized with terror, and some, thinking, 'They are coming to kill us too,' are brought to the point of death. In the same way, Mahāmati, the other very tiny creatures that live in the sky, on land and in water, as soon as they see meat-eaters from afar, catch their scent with their keen sense of smell and flee swiftly, as people flee from a rākṣasa, and some even fear for their lives. Therefore, Mahāmati, because it causes terror, a bodhisattva who abides in loving-kindness does not eat meat.

"Mahāmati, because meat is resorted to by ignoble people, smells foul, brings disrepute, and is shunned by the noble ones, a bodhisattva does not eat meat. Mahāmati, noble people eat the food of sages and do not eat blood and flesh; for that reason too a bodhisattva does not eat meat.

"Mahāmati, in order to protect the minds of many people, a bodhisattva whose very nature is compassion, wishing to prevent disparagement of the teaching, does not eat meat. It is like this, Mahāmati: in the world there are those who speak in disparagement of the teaching: 'Alas, what sort of ascetic life do these people have? Where is their holy life? Having abandoned the food of the sages of old, they fill their bellies with the food of flesh like beasts of prey, and they roam about this world terrifying the tiny creatures that live in the sky, on land and in water. Their ascetic life has degenerated; their renunciation of evil has been destroyed.

"These people have neither Dharma nor Vinaya!" Speaking this way, those who harbor hostile minds disparage the teaching in many ways. Therefore, Mahāmati, in order to protect the minds of many people, the bodhisattva of compassionate nature who wishes to prevent disparagement of the teaching should not eat any meat.

Mahāmati, because meat is like a corpse, foul-smelling and unwholesome, the bodhisattva should not eat meat. Mahāmati, when the flesh of a dead human is burned, and when the flesh of some other creature is burned, there is no difference whatever in the smell; both are equally foul. For this reason too, Mahāmati, the bodhisattva who is a yogin and desires purity should not eat any meat.

Mahāmati, there are the charnel-ground dwellers; the yogins who stay in remote lodgings in the wilderness and in forests where nonhumans roam; those who practice yoga and abide in loving-kindness; the holders of vidyā-mantras; and those who wish to accomplish vidyā-mantras. Meat hinders their accomplishment of vidyā-mantras, and because it obstructs liberation, it hinders all the yogic accomplishments of the sons and daughters of noble family who have entered the Great Vehicle. Seeing this, the bodhisattva who wishes to benefit the bodies of himself and others should not eat any meat.

Because consciousness that takes visible form as its object becomes the condition that gives rise to attachment, the bodhisattva of compassionate nature, for whom all living creatures have become as his own self, should not eat any meat. Mahāmati, because even the gods shun one who eats it, the bodhisattva of compassionate nature should not eat any meat. Mahāmati, because even in this very life such a person's mouth becomes extremely foul-smelling, the bodhisattva should not eat any meat.

Such a person sleeps in misery and wakes in misery. He sees evil dreams that make his hair stand on end. When he stays in an empty house or dwells alone in solitude, nonhumans who steal vitality steal his vitality. He is startled again and again, and suddenly becomes frightened and alarmed. He does not know moderation in food, and what he eats, drinks, chews and tastes does not become well-digested nourishment. Worms arise in his belly, he becomes the ground for many skin diseases, and he is made prone to many illnesses. He does not even gain the perception of food as repulsive, whereas I have taught that food is to be regarded like the flesh of one's own child, and like medicine.

Mahāmati, meat and blood are food resorted to by ignoble people and wholly shunned by noble ones, food that brings many faults and is devoid of many virtues, food unfit for sages, unwholesome and improper. How could I permit them to my disciples?

Mahāmati, I do permit, saying that they are suitable and well examined, the foods not shunned by noble ones, which bring many virtues and avoid many faults, the wholesome food of the sages of old: rice, barley, wheat, mung beans, black gram, lentils and the like; ghee, sesame oil, honey, treacle, jaggery, sugar candy, sugar and the like. Even so, Mahāmati, in time to come, foolish people who make conceptual distinctions about the Vinaya, who say many things, who are imbued with the habitual tendencies of a meat-eating lineage and are deeply attached to the craving for taste, some of them, will hold that meat is wholesome food. But, Mahāmati, it is taught that the bodhisattva mahāsattvas hold those foods to be proper: those who have rendered service to the Victors of the past and generated roots of virtue; who are faithful and free of conceptual thought; the sons of the Śākya lineage, the sons and daughters of human family, who are unattached to body, life and possessions, who do not cling to tastes and are not greedy for tastes, who are compassionate, who wish to cherish all living creatures as themselves, and who look upon all sentient beings with love, as on an only child.

Mahāmati, in times long past there was a king named Siṃha Saudāsa. He ate meat as his food, and by indulging in it improperly he craved its taste, grew attached and longed for it, so that he came to eat even human flesh. Because of this he was abandoned even by his friends, ministers, relatives and kin, to say nothing of the townspeople and the people of the land, and since he had lost his kingdom and his land, he was greatly afflicted.

Mahāmati, Indra too, having formerly attained lordship over the gods, through the fault of the habitual tendency of eating meat took on the form of a hawk, while Viśvakarman, taking the shape and guise of a pigeon, was cruelly tormented. Indra even placed King Śibi, who was faultless and compassionate toward living creatures, on the scales and brought him great suffering. Mahāmati, if, through habituation over many lives, even one who had become Śakra, lord of the gods, was thus led to bring harm upon himself and others, what need is there to speak of anyone else?

Mahāmati, other men who had become lords of men were carried off by a powerful horse and wandered in a wilderness. To sustain their lives they mated with a lioness, and children were born to them. Because of that union with a lion, the prince Kalmāṣapāda and others, through the habitual tendencies of the fault of eating meat in former lives, came to eat human flesh even though they had become lords of men.

Mahāmati, even in this very life, if someone in a village of seven households greatly craves meat and indulges in it improperly, he will be reborn among the savage ḍākas and ḍākinīs who eat human flesh. Mahāmati, after that life, through that same attachment to the taste of meat, such beings also fall into rebirth as lions, tigers, leopards, wolves, hyenas, cats, foxes and owls, births of creatures that eat much meat, and into extremely fierce births such as those of the rākṣasas who eat much meat. For those who have fallen there, even a human birth is hard to obtain, let alone nirvāṇa. Mahāmati, such are the faults of eating meat, and since these and others befall even those who merely indulge in it, what need is there to speak of those who indulge heavily? But by turning away from it, the virtues are very many. Mahāmati, childish ordinary beings do not understand these virtues and faults, nor others besides.

Mahāmati, because they see these and other benefits and faults, it is taught that bodhisattvas, whose very nature is compassion, should eat no meat at all. Mahāmati, if no one ate meat in any way at all, then living beings would not be killed for it either. Mahāmati, innocent living beings are killed mostly for the price they fetch, and only rarely for any other reason.

Mahāmati, the craving for taste is utterly dreadful. Indulging it to excess, people have even come to eat human flesh, so what need is there to speak of the flesh of wild animals, livestock, birds, and other creatures? Mahāmati, those tormented by craving for the taste of meat set up nets and traps of every kind, and with them fowlers, butchers, fishermen, and others kill, in many different ways, innocent creatures that fly in the sky, walk on the land, and swim in the water.

Mahāmati, those who have become hunters, fierce of mind and as pitiless as rākṣasas, kill and eat living beings while knowing full well that they are living beings, and still compassion never arises in them.

Mahāmati, there is no meat that is permissible, not even the meat called "not done for one, not caused to be done, and not suspected," which one might cite as something I allowed to the śrāvakas. Yet, Mahāmati, in times to come there will be people who have gone forth in my teaching and who profess to be sons of the Śākya, wearing the saffron banner: foolish persons whose minds are corrupted by false reasoning, who dream up many interpretations of the Vinaya, whose view of the transitory collection has grown strong in their disputation, and who are attached to the craving for taste. They will put forward all kinds of semblances of reasons for eating meat, and they will think to slander me falsely. Examining the various incidents that occasioned the rules, they will say, "On the occasion of this incident the Blessed One allowed meat as food, so is it not permissible? He also spoke of it among the delicious foods, and even the Tathāgata himself ate it."

Mahāmati, there is no sūtra in which it is allowed to be eaten, and it is not taught as permissible among the delicious foods either. Mahāmati, if I had wished to allow it, or if it were something my śrāvakas could permissibly eat, would I also have forbidden all eating of meat to the yogins who dwell in loving-kindness and frequent the charnel grounds, and to the sons and daughters of good family who have truly entered the Great Vehicle, so that they might cultivate the perception of all sentient beings as their only child? Mahāmati, I have also forbidden all meat to the sons and daughters of good family who long for the Dharma and have entered any of the vehicles, and to the charnel-ground dwellers who abide in loving-kindness, the forest dwellers, and those who practice yoga, so that they might accomplish every yoga and cultivate the perception of all sentient beings as their children.

In the teachings I have given on various occasions, the bases of training were laid down step by step, like fitting the rungs of a ladder. Having laid down the threefold rule, I forbade also what was prepared for that purpose; therefore the meat of the ten that die naturally was not forbidden. But here it is forbidden entirely, altogether, in every respect, with no exception whatsoever. Thus, Mahāmati, I have not allowed meat as food to anyone, I do not allow it, and I will not allow it. Mahāmati, I teach that meat as food is not permissible for those who have gone forth.

Mahāmati, whoever thinks to slander me by saying, "Even the Tathāgata ate it," Mahāmati, those foolish persons, abiding in the obscuration of the faults of their own karma, will bring upon themselves what is fruitless, harmful, and painful for a long time.

Mahāmati, my noble śrāvakas do not eat even ordinary food, so what need is there to speak of the impermissible food of flesh and blood? Mahāmati, my śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas, and bodhisattvas eat the food of the Dharma, not material food, so what need is there to speak of the tathāgatas? Mahāmati, the tathāgatas are bodies of Dharma, sustained by the food of the Dharma. They are not material bodies, and they are not sustained by any material food at all. They have abandoned all the habitual tendencies of seeking through craving for existence and for the requisites of existence; they are free of all the habitual tendencies of the faults of the afflictions; their mind and wisdom are utterly liberated; they are omniscient and all-seeing; they regard all sentient beings equally, as an only child; and they possess great compassion.

Thus, Mahāmati, since I hold the perception of all sentient beings as my only child, how could I allow the śrāvakas to eat the flesh of my own child, let alone eat it myself? That I allowed it to the śrāvakas and ate it myself, Mahāmati, is impossible.

On this it is said:

That great-minded bodhisattvas
Should neither eat nor drink
Alcohol, meat, garlic, or onions,
Supreme Victor, please explain.

Its foul smell is relished by the ignoble,
And it brings disrepute as well.
Meat is the food of beasts of prey;
The great Sage has said it must not be eaten.

The faults that come from eating it,
The benefits of not eating it,
And whatever befalls the eater of meat,
Mahāmati, understand these well.

Because of delusion, and because of kin and friends,
Because it arises from semen and blood,
And because creatures are terrified,
The yogin does not eat meat.

Meats, garlic and onions,
Alcohol of every kind,
Leeks, and wild garlic likewise,
The yogin should always shun.
Anointing with sesame oil should also be shunned.
Since the creatures that dwell in beds
Are crushed within their crevices,
One should not lie down on a bed.

From food arises arrogance;
From arrogance, thoughts arise;
Thought gives birth to desire;
Therefore one should not eat it.
From thought, desire is born;
Desire stupefies the mind;
Through stupefaction one is drawn to union,
And one is born, and is not liberated.

For profit, sentient beings are killed;
For meat, wealth is handed over.
Both of these are doers of evil,
Boiled in Raurava, the Howling Hell, and the rest.

Whoever transgresses the words of the Sage
And with evil intent eats meat,
To ruin both this world and the next,
Has taken up vows in the Śākya's teaching.
Into the most unbearable hells
Those doers of evil go;
In dreadful Raurava and the others
Those eaters of meat are boiled.

Meat that is pure in three respects,
Unconceived, unrequested,
And unurged, does not exist;
Therefore one should not eat meat.
The yogin does not eat meat:
So I and the buddhas have taught.

Beings who eat one another
Are born in the families of carnivores.
Foul-smelling and despised,
And mad, they will be born.
In the families of caṇḍālas and ḍombas,
As dyers, they are born again and again.
In the wombs of the ḍākinī kind
And in the race of piśācas they will be born.
Those lowest of humans
Are born in the wombs of rākṣasīs and of cats.

In the Hastikakṣya and the Mahāmegha,
In the Nirvāṇa and the Aṅgulimālīya,
And also in the Descent into Laṅkā Sūtra,
I have censured meat.
What the buddhas, the bodhisattvas,
And the śrāvakas have censured,
Those who eat it without shame
Will always be born insane.

Because they have given up meat and the like,
Into brahmin families, or as yogins,
As people wise and wealthy,
Those ones will be born.

Seen, heard, or suspected,
All meat is censured.
Logicians born in the families of carnivores
Do not understand this.

Just as desire
Becomes an obstacle to liberation,
So meat, alcohol and the like
Become obstacles.

In times to come,
Meat-eaters speaking out of delusion
Will say, "Meat is blameless and permissible;
So the Buddha taught."

Food that is like medicine,
That is like the flesh of one's own son,
In due measure and as something repugnant,
The yogin takes as food.

Keeping company with lions,
Tigers, foxes and the like,
For one who dwells in loving-kindness,
I have always censured in every way.

Because it contradicts the Dharma of liberation,
Meat, which terrifies people,
Should therefore not be eaten;
This is the banner of the noble ones.

This is the seventh chapter, on not eating meat, from the Descent into Laṅkā, the heart of the teachings of all the buddhas.

Then the Blessed One said to the bodhisattva Mahāmati: "The mantra words of the Descent into Laṅkā, which the buddhas, the blessed ones of the past, the future and the present, have taught, are teaching, and will teach, and which I too now teach in order to protect those who proclaim the Dharma, you should hold. They are as follows: tudṭe tudṭe budṭe budṭe padṭe padṭe kadṭe kadṭe amale amale bimale bimale nime nime hime hime bame bame kale kale kala kale adṭe madṭe badṭe tudṭe rñadṭe spudṭe kadṭe ladṭe padṭe hime hime dime dime cale cale pace pace bhande bhande anche mance ṭuḍāre dhuḍāre paḍāre agke agke magke magke cagkre cagkre dime dime hime hime ṭu ṭu ṭu ṭu ṭhu ṭhu ṭhu ṭhu ḍu ḍu ḍu ḍu ṭṭu ṭṭu ṭṭu ṭṭu svāhā

"Mahāmati, these are the mantra words of the Descent into Laṅkā Sūtra. Whatever son of good family or daughter of good family holds, bears, reads and masters these mantra words, no one will find an opening against them. Whether god or goddess, nāga or nāginī, yakṣa or yakṣiṇī, asura or asura woman, garuḍa or garuḍa woman, kinnara or kinnarī, mahoraga or mahoraga woman, gandharva or gandharva woman, bhūta or bhūta woman, kumbhāṇḍa or kumbhāṇḍa woman, piśāca or piśācī, ostāraka or ostārakī, apasmāra or apasmāra woman, rākṣasa or rākṣasī, ḍāka or ḍākinī, ojohāra or ojohārī, pūtana or pūtanī, human or nonhuman, none of them at all will find an opening against that person. Should a fierce graha arise, by reciting the mantra over it one hundred and eight times, it will go away weeping and wailing, staring off in one direction.

"Mahāmati, I shall also teach other mantra words. They are as follows: padme padma debe hene hina hine cule culā cule hule hula hule yule yula yule pale pala pale munce chindhe bhindhe bhanche marde pramarde dinakare svāhā

"Mahāmati, if any son of good family or daughter of good family holds, bears, reads and masters these mantra words, then god or goddess, nāga or nāginī, yakṣa or yakṣiṇī, asura or asura woman, garuḍa or garuḍa woman, kinnara or kinnarī, mahoraga or mahoraga woman, gandharva or gandharva woman, bhūta or bhūta woman, kumbhāṇḍa or kumbhāṇḍa woman, piśāca or piśācī, ostāraka or ostārakī, apasmāra or apasmāra woman, rākṣasa or rākṣasī, ḍāka or ḍākinī, ojohāra or ojohārī, pūtana or pūtanī, human or nonhuman, none of them at all will find an opening. Whoever reads these mantra words will have read the entire Descent into Laṅkā Sūtra."

These mantra words were spoken by the Blessed One in order to repel the rākṣasas.

The Noble Descent into Laṅkā, the eighth fascicle.

Then the bodhisattva, the great being Mahāmati, said this to the Blessed One:

"This world, free of arising and perishing,
Is just like a flower in the sky;
Through your wisdom and compassion,
You do not perceive it as existent or nonexistent.

The world, beyond permanence and annihilation,
Is ever like a dream;
Through your wisdom and compassion,
You do not perceive it as existent or nonexistent.

All phenomena are like illusions,
Free of mind and of consciousness;
Through your wisdom and compassion,
