You do not apprehend existence or nonexistence.

The selflessness of phenomena and of persons,
And your obscurations of affliction and of the knowable,
Through your wisdom and compassion
And through signlessness, are always pure.

You neither pass into nirvāṇa nor fail to pass;
Nirvāṇa does not abide in you.
You are free of understanding and of what is to be known,
And you have left behind the positions of existence and nonexistence.

Those who see the peaceful Sage
Who is thus free of arising and perishing
Will, in this life and in the next,
Be without attachment and without grasping.

Just as, when the mirage that deceives the mind
Shimmers in the spring,
The deer take it for water,
Though there is no real thing there,

So too the seed of consciousness
Stirs within the domain of views,
And the childish take it to be arising,
Like the floaters seen by one with impaired sight.

Meditation, the object of meditation, the meditator,
Abandonment, and the seeing of the truth:
Whoever realizes that these
Are mere conceptions is liberated.

These phenomena have no core;
They arise from the presuming mind.
The presumption by which one presumes,
"They are empty," is itself empty.

The aggregates, with consciousness the fifth,
Are like the reflection of a tree in water.
Appearances resemble illusions and dreams;
Do not conceive of them through cognition.

When a person sees things as like an illusion, a vetāla,
A mechanical device,
A dream, lightning, or clouds,
Ever cutting the threefold continuum, that person is freed.

Through improper conceptualization,
Consciousness operates in manifold ways,
Eightfold or ninefold,
Just like the waves of the ocean.

Ever produced by habitual tendencies,
Its roots sunk deep and firmly established,
The mind wanders among its objects
Like iron drawn by a magnet.

Abiding in all sentient beings,
The ground of the lineage is free of conceptualization;
When wisdom and the knowable are relinquished
And it is freed from activity, it turns back.

The illusion-like samādhi
That arises from the ten grounds,
And the king of mind, free of perception and consciousness,
Should be beheld.

When the mind has been transformed,
And a lotus-like celestial palace,
An illusory domain, comes forth,
Then one abides there forever.

Having come to abide there,
Through spontaneously accomplished conduct and knowledge,
Like a jewel of many forms,
One acts for the benefit of all sentient beings.

Apart from conceptualization
There is nothing conditioned or unconditioned;
These are like a barren woman's son in a dream.
The childish cling to corrupted positions.

Absence of intrinsic nature, nonarising,
The person, and the continuum of aggregates,
Conditions, elements, the knowable,
Emptiness, existence, and nonexistence:
These I have taught as skillful means.
Though I do not teach characteristics,
The childish grasp at things as real,
As bases of characterization and as characteristics.

Though knowing all, he is not the Omniscient One;
Even in the midst of all, there is no "all."
Childish worldlings imagine, "This is the Buddha,"
Yet he neither knows nor teaches.

This designation is merely a name;
It does not exist by any characteristic.
The aggregates on which it is imagined
Are like a tangle of hairs.

Nothing whatsoever arises from nonexistence,
And nothing is destroyed by conditions.
When one sees the conditioned thus,
As like a flower in the sky or a barren woman's son,
Then, seeing grasped and grasper
To be an error, one turns away from them.

I do not pass into nirvāṇa
As a thing with characteristics and activity;
When conceptualizing consciousness is reversed,
Then I have passed into nirvāṇa.

Just as, when a great river has run dry,
Waves no longer arise,
So too, when it has ceased,
The manifold consciousnesses do not arise.

Empty, without essential nature,
Unborn, like illusions,
Neither existent nor nonexistent:
These things are like a dream.

I teach a single nature,
Free of the signs and reasonings of logicians,
The domain of the noble gods,
Free of the two natures.

There is no grasper and nothing grasped,
No liberation and no bondage;
It is like an illusion and a mirage,
Just like a dream or floaters in the eyes.

When one who seeks the true meaning
Sees without conceptualization or attachment,
That one has entered into yoga
And, without doubt, sees me as well.

There is no cognition there at all,
Just like a mirage in the sky.
When phenomena are known in this way,
Nothing whatsoever is known.

Because the elements are thrown into turmoil,
Things like fireflies,
Though not there, appear in variety to one intoxicated;
Such is the nature of the world.

Though an illusion conjured from grass,
Wood, and pebbles may be beautiful,
That illusion does not exist;
Such is the nature of phenomena.

Phenomena do not arise
From conditions, whether existent or nonexistent;
It is because of error in the three realms
That variety appears.

The world is like a dream in its essential nature;
In it, too, various forms appear.
Contact, enjoyment, the body's going to its end,
The Principal of the world, and its makers are all alike.

This mind has become the birthplace of the three realms;
Deluded mind appears as this world and the beyond.
Because the world does not exist, one should not conceive of it.
Having realized that such is the nature of the world,
One reaches the end of the impure abodes.

Because of delusion, the childish
See arising and perishing;
Those endowed with wisdom
Do not see arising or perishing.

The palace of the gods is Akaniṣṭha.

Wholly rid of every evil,
ever endowed with nonconceptuality,
having abandoned mind and mental factors,
having gained the powers, the superknowledges and mastery,
they, having found mastery in meditative absorption,
awaken there as perfect buddhas.
Their emanations awaken here.
Emanations of the buddhas
arise in countless millions.
From them all the childish
hear the inconceivable Dharma, which is like an echo.

Free of beginning, middle and end,
having abandoned existence and nonexistence,
pervasive, unmoving and pure:
from what is not manifold the manifold arises.
Veiled by the family of cognition,
it abides in all embodied beings.
Though illusion and error are there,
it is not the cause of illusion and error.
Whatever slight thing there is in the worlds
that deceives the mind
is bound up with the two natures
and is conjured by the all-base consciousness.

In the view of mere cognition,
and in dealing with persons and phenomena,
when one has thus meditated on the world
and at some point it is fully transformed,
then he is my son,
who teaches the perfect dharmas.

As heat, moisture, solidity and motion
the childish imagine phenomena.
It is an unreal superimposition,
without characteristic and without basis of characteristic.
In this body made of continua
this is form composed of eight substances;
as the shape of the body and as the faculties
the deluded childish conceive it.
From the coming together of causes and conditions
the childish imagine that things arise.
Not understanding these principles,
they wander in the abode of the three existences.

All things are without intrinsic nature; conceptuality is but the word "fire," and that too does not exist.
Having examined existence as like a dream, like an emanation,
one should not take up saṃsāra, nor take up nirvāṇa.

The mind called "the various seeds"
appears within the domain of mind;
once it has appeared, through the two conceptions
the childish imagine arising.
Ignorance, craving and karma
abide as mind and mental factors;
because it arises from them,
it is held to be dependent.
To conceive them as things
is error, the domain of mind.
It is not established through conception;
it is falsely, erroneously imagined.
From connection with conditions
the minds of embodied beings arise.
When, freed from conditions
and having abandoned its own characteristic,
it no longer abides in a body,
then it is not within my domain.

Just as a king or a merchant
coaxes his children with various animal likenesses,
sets them playing at home,
and shows them meeting the animals of the forest,
so I, with various
mere reflections of phenomena,
explain to my sons
the limit of reality, known personally within.

Just as the waves of the ocean,
arising through the condition of wind,
are set in motion as if dancing
and their stream is never cut off,
so the river of the all-base
arises ever through the condition of objects,
and with the various waves of consciousness
bows and sways as if dancing.
Into things grasped and grasping
the minds of embodied beings enter.
As the childish imagine,
the appearance has no such characteristic.

The supreme all-base consciousness
is cognition; and the all-base,
because it is free of grasped and grasper,
I explain as suchness.

In the aggregates there is no self,
no life-principle and no person.
Consciousness arises,
and consciousness ceases.
Just as a painting appears
high and low, though it is not so,
likewise in things
thingness appears, though it does not exist.
Like a city of gandharvas,
like mirages:
though one always sees appearances thus,
for wisdom they do not exist.

Valid cognition and the faculties are reversed;
there is no result, and no cause either;
it is free of intellect and of what is to be cognized,
and has abandoned characteristic and basis of characteristic.
One who is a buddha by relying on the aggregates
no one anywhere has seen;
what no one anywhere has seen,
how could one meditate on it?

With conditions, reasons and examples,
propositions and causes,
with examples such as dreams, the gandharva's wheel,
mirages, the sun and the moon,
sprouts, light and so forth,
I have refuted these arisings.

Beings are like dreams and illusions,
empty and imputed;
they do not abide in the three worlds,
and so it is too within and without.
By seeing existence as unborn
the forbearance of nonarising arises.
The illusion-like meditative absorption,
and the body too is illusion-like;
the superknowledges, the masteries and the powers
take many forms in his mind.

Those things that are unborn,
empty, utterly without intrinsic nature,
in them, through conditions,
error arises and ceases.
Through what has form and appears external,
it is mind, and mind alone, that appears.
Apart from what the childish imagine,
there is nothing else to be seen.

Connections and the forms of the buddhas,
the elements and their destruction:
by thoroughly trained cognition
the various beings are empowered.
Body, abode and enjoyments
are the three grasped cognitions;
mind, the grasping cognition
and conceptualization are the three graspers.

Conception and examination:
as long as one is in the domain of letters,
because the reasoner is misled by reasoning,
for so long one does not see reality.
When through wisdom
one knows things to be without intrinsic nature,
then the yogin
abides in signlessness and rests from weariness.

When a bird is daubed with ink,
the ignorant take it for a bird of another kind;
so it is for the ignorant childish
with regard to the three vehicles as well.
Here there are no śrāvakas at all,
and likewise no pratyekabuddhas.
Whatever forms of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas
appear here
are emanations displayed
by compassionate bodhisattvas.

The three existences are mere cognition;
the two natures are imputed.
Because phenomena and persons are transformed,
that transformation is suchness.

The light of sun, moon and lamp,
likewise the elements and jewels,
arise utterly without conceptualization;
so too is the buddhahood of the buddhas.
Just as those with diseased eyes
see a net of hairs wrongly tangled,
so this conceiving of things
is falsely imagined by the childish.
Things of affliction and purification
are like a tangle of hairs:
free of abiding, destruction and arising,
they have abandoned permanence and impermanence.

Just as someone who has eaten datura
sees the world as if made of gold,
and though there is no gold there,
even the ground appears as gold,

so fools are corrupted
by beginningless mind and mental factors,
and existence, arisen like an illusion or a mirage,
they grasp as something real.

The single seed and the seedless,
the even, and the predominant, which has seeds,
and likewise the all-seed:
look at the mind that has become diverse.

When it is pure, it is the single seed;
when fully transformed, it is seedless;
because it does not conceptualize, it is the even;
because it is predominant, lifetimes are mingled.

Because it plants diverse seeds,
it is called the all-seed.
Here nothing arises through conditions,
and nothing whatever ceases.

It is only imagined conditions
that arise and cease.
The three realms of existence are mere designation;
by their own nature there are no things.

Designations as the nature of things
are what logicians imagine.
Those who wish to know the intrinsic nature of things,
being mistaken, do not realize it.

When one sees in this way
that the intrinsic nature of things is unarisen, one is liberated.
Because they share the qualities of an illusion and of nonexistence,
things are spoken of as existing in that same way.

Because they turn away as swiftly as lightning,
therefore know them to be like an illusion.
Nothing is produced and nothing is born;
and conditions too, anywhere at all,

do not exist in the least,
yet some speak of them as functioning.
The connection of arising and perishing through conditions
is not something to be refuted;

what is without intrinsic nature and without cognition,
which fools conceive of in terms of conditions,
that is what is refuted.
Though there is no intrinsic nature and no cognition,

no storehouse (ālaya) and no thing,
foolish and inferior logicians,
like corpses, imagine these.
When the Victor's children

see the world as mind only,
then they attain a body
belonging among the classes of beings,
free of the formations of fools,

and endowed with the powers,
the superknowledges and the masteries.
When there arises the mind
to which all appears as form, the realization

"Here there is no mind and no form;
this is beginningless mistaken mind,"
then yogins, through knowledge,
see beings as without appearance.

Having passed beyond signs, things and cognition,
and the stirring of whatever is cognized,
my children
engage in nonconceptuality.

Illusions, cities of the gandharvas,
tangled hairs and mirages,
though nonexistent, appear to exist;
so it is with things being things.

All things are unborn;
it is mere error that appears.
Fools, delighting in dual conception,
and from error, imagine them to be born.

The mind that belongs to the share of birth,
arising from diverse habitual tendencies,
surges forth like waves on a river;
once that is cut off, it no longer arises.

Just as the mind that apprehends various aspects
enters into the various,
why then does it not likewise
enter into space or a wall?

If the mind were to enter
by apprehending some sign,
then mind too would be produced by conditions,
and mind only would not hold.

It is by mind that mind is grasped;
nothing at all comes with a cause.
The true nature of mind is pure;
in space there are no habitual tendencies.

Through clinging to one's own mind,
mind arises in abundance.
There is no external appearance;
therefore it is mind only.

Mind is the storehouse consciousness;
that which thinks "I" is mentation;
that which grasps objects
is called consciousness.

Mind is always indeterminate;
mentation moves in both directions;
and the consciousness that arises
is virtuous and nonvirtuous.

Body, abode and enjoyments
are the threefold cognition of the grasped;
mentation, the cognition of the grasper,
and conceptual thought are the threefold grasper.

The abandoning of both cognitions
is the gateway to the ultimate.
When one abides in nonappearance,
how could the three vehicles be set out?

Mind only and nonappearance,
the stages and abodes of the buddhas:
these the buddhas have taught,
now teach, and will uphold.

The seven stages are mind;
the eighth is nonappearance.
Here two stages are abodes;
the remaining stage is my own nature.

Pure, and individually self-aware,
this stage is my own nature:
the supreme abode of Maheśvara,
Akaniṣṭha, surpassingly beautiful.

Just as from a fire,
its various rays
stream forth, lovely and peaceful,
and these emanate the three realms.

Some emanate the three realms of existence;
some were emanated before;
there the vehicles are taught:
that is the stage of my own nature.

For passing to the fields and for realization,
there is no time for the stages;
having passed beyond even mind only,
it is the fruit of abiding in nonappearance.

Various aspects, existent
and nonexistent, appear;
fools are corrupted by grasping;
the variety is an inversion.

When there is nonconceptual wisdom,
things cannot exist.
Because mind is not form,
therefore it is nonconceptual.

The faculties are like illusions,
and objects are like dreams;
agent, deed and action
do not exist in any way.

The meditative concentrations, the immeasurables,
the formless attainments, the samādhis,
and the cessation of perception, all without exception,
do not exist in mind only.

The fruit of stream entry,
the fruit of once-returning,
the fruit of non-returning,
and arhatship itself are errors of the mind.

Fools imagine conditioned things
to be impermanent, momentary and empty;
by the examples of a seed, a stream and a lamp
they imagine the meaning of momentariness.

Without agent, momentary,
isolated and free of activity,
and the non-arising of phenomena:
this I have taught as the meaning of momentariness.

Arising from existence and from nonexistence
is what the Vaiśeṣikas and Sāṃkhyas hold,
and all the unanswered questions
were set forth by those very ones.

Answers are of four kinds:
the categorical, by counter-question,
by distinguishing, and by setting aside;
these refute the assertions of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas).

Conventionally, everything exists;
ultimately, it does not exist.
If the absence of intrinsic nature in phenomena
is held to be the ultimate,

then, for the apprehended object that lacks intrinsic nature,
the conventional is therefore taught.
If things that arise from the cause of verbal expression
had intrinsic nature,

there could be no talk of
"things arising from words being nonexistent."
In this expression there is no thing;
it does not exist even conventionally.
Because inversion does not exist,
apprehension does not exist.

If error existed,
the absence of essential nature would not exist.
Since error is fourfold,
whatever is apprehended
is without intrinsic nature;
it does not exist in any way at all.

This that appears in such variety
is mind imbued with dysfunctional tendencies,
grasping at what appears as form.
External things are the mind's delusion.

Through conceiving and not conceiving,
conceptual thought is abandoned.
On the ground where conceptuality does not conceive,
emptiness is truly seen.

Like many illusory elephants,
like leaves taken for gold,
because the mind is imbued with ignorance,
appearances show themselves to people in this way.

The noble ones do not see error,
yet the real is not found in that place either,
for if the real were found there,
error itself would become the real.

Even when all errors have been abandoned,
if signs still go on arising,
that itself is their error,
like a cataract not fully cleared.

Just as those with diseased eyes,
because of the defect, see hairs,
so too the grasping of childish beings
is deeply engaged with objects.

These three realms of existence are like those hairs,
an error like the water of a mirage.
If one meditates on them as like a dream or an illusion,
one will be liberated.

Conceptualizing, what is conceptualized,
the engagement of conceptual thought,
binding, what is to be bound, and being bound:
these six are the causes of liberation.

There are no grounds, there are no truths,
there are no fields, there are no emanations;
buddhas and pratyekabuddhas
and śrāvakas too are imagined.

Persons, causes and aggregates,
likewise conditions and atoms,
the principal (pradhāna), Īśvara and creators
are conceptualized within mind only.

Mind, in every way and everywhere,
enters into all bodies;
in six ways it grasps the manifold.
The very kind of mind is without defining marks.

The aggregates are without self,
and no self is found in the aggregates.
They do not exist as they are imagined,
yet neither are they nonexistent.

If all things existed
just as the childish imagine them,
if they existed just as they are seen,
then everyone would see the truth.

Because all things are nonexistent,
there is neither defilement nor purification.
They do not exist as they are seen,
yet neither are they nonexistent.

The signs of erroneous conceptualization
are the dependent characteristic.
The name imputed upon that characteristic
is the imputed characteristic.

When the signs of erroneous conceptualization,
arising from conditions and from the conventions of names,
no longer arise there,
that is the perfected characteristic.

Buddhas of maturation,
emanated victors,
bodhisattvas and sentient beings,
the fields in every direction,
illusion-like phenomena and magical displays,
and whatever victors are emanated:
all of these come forth
from Sukhāvatī of Amitābha.

Whatever is taught by emanations,
and whatever is taught by those born of maturation,
the way of the extensive sūtras:
understand their intent.

Whatever the Victor's children teach,
and whatever the guides teach,
is what the emanations teach,
not the Victor of maturation.

These phenomena are unborn,
yet neither are they nonexistent.
They are like a city of gandharvas, an illusion,
dreams and emanations.

The manifold is the activity of mind;
it is mind that is liberated;
what ceases, too, is mind itself;
mind itself arises, and nothing else.

People's mind, imagining the manifold,
shows itself as appearing as objects.
This is mind only, and there are no objects.
Through nonconceptuality one is liberated.

Through proliferation since beginningless time
dysfunctional tendencies have been amassed,
and because conceptuality is imbued with them,
false appearances arise.

What appears as objects is consciousness;
wisdom has the real as its domain.
What has been fully transformed is without appearance;
that is the domain of the noble ones.

The meditative concentration that discerns meaning,
the meditative concentration practiced by the childish,
the meditative concentration focused on suchness,
and the excellent meditative concentration of the Tathāgata.

In terms of the imputed nature,
all phenomena are unborn.
Abiding in the dependent,
people's conceptual thoughts wander.

When the dependent
is free of conceptual thought,
the transformation is suchness,
abiding in the abandonment of thought.

Conceptual thought does not truly exist,
so do not conceive with conceptual thought.
When one conceives those errors
as having the character of grasped and grasper,
the imagining that sees external objects
is the imputed nature.

When, having abandoned all of these,
there is no longer any appearance,
then, on that ground, the divine hands of the buddhas,
coming from fields on every side,
stroke that one's head,
a sign of following suchness.

Because, when examined by the intellect,
intrinsic natures are not apprehended,
therefore they are inexpressible,
and are taught to be without essential nature.

In what is produced by conditions
there is neither existence nor nonexistence.
Those who conceive existence and nonexistence
in things that belong to conditions,
holders of non-Buddhist (tīrthika) views,
know them to have strayed far from the teaching.

In speaking of all things,
whenever they pass on to other lives,
through their mutual conceptual thoughts
they become habituated, and habituated again.

If names were not spoken,
the whole world would be bewildered.
Therefore, to dispel bewilderment,
designations are made with names.

Through three kinds of conceptual thought,
that of names, that of error,
and that produced by conditions,
the childish conceive of things.

Unceasing and unborn,
by nature like space,
whatever is the nature of the absence of things,
that is the imputed characteristic.

Like an optical illusion, like a magical illusion,
a mirage, a dream, a whirling firebrand,
like a city of gandharvas or an echo,
that phenomenon does not arise from conditions.
Nondual with suchness,
emptiness, dharmatā, the limit of reality,
that which is taught as nonconceptual:
that is the perfected characteristic.

The domain of body and speech is false;
nonconceptual wisdom is true.
Because mind falls into the two extremes,
therefore wisdom is nonconceptual.

As long as mind has a domain,
there are the two extremes of existence and nonexistence.
When the domain has been abandoned,
then mind too truly ceases.

Whatever thought conceives
regarding the nature born of conditions,
seeing external objects, is false.
There are no objects; there is only mind.

When one analyzes with reasoning,
grasped and grasper cease.
External objects do not exist
in the way the childish imagine them.

The mind stirred up by habitual tendencies
arises, vividly appearing as objects.
When the two kinds of conceptuality cease,
the true domain is wisdom;
the domain of the noble ones is inconceivable,
and it comes about without appearances.

Name, sign, and imagination
are the characteristics of the two natures.
Right wisdom and suchness
are the characteristic of the perfected nature.

Because father and mother have come together,
the foundation consciousness (ālaya), together with the mind,
like a mouse in a jar of butter,
is born together with the semen.
As the fetal mass, the lump and the swelling, all unlovely,
it is shaped by karma into various forms.
By the wind of karma and the great elements
it develops like a fruit.
With the five, the five, and the five,
and the nine openings,
covered with teeth, nails and hair,
it stirs and is fully born.
Like a worm born in filth,
like people waking from sleep,
the eye opens out toward forms,
and conceptions too increase.
From the two lips and the palate,
having discerned through conceptual thought,
the conceptual construction of human speech
comes forth like a parrot's.

The doctrines of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) are clung to;
the Great Vehicle does not stay fixed.
It proceeds in accord with the intentions of sentient beings,
and is no abode for wrong views.
The vehicle of personal self-awareness
is not the domain of logicians.

"After the Protector has passed away,
who will uphold it? Please tell me."

"After the Sugata has passed into nirvāṇa,
one who will uphold the ways
will appear when time has passed;
Mahāmati, you should know this.
In the land of Veda in the south
a monk, glorious and greatly renowned,
whose name is called Nāga,
destroyer of the positions of existence and nonexistence,
having fully taught my vehicle in the world,
the unsurpassed Great Vehicle,
will attain the Joyous ground
and go to Sukhāvatī.

Having wandered through all destinies,
with minds weary of existence,
although they have gone the peaceful path of calm,
they strive at various yogas.
When yogis make such effort,
they see what resembles the shape of sun and moon,
what is like light and like lotuses,
like drawings, like fire in the sky.
These various signs
lead them onto the path of the tīrthikas,
and make them fall as well into the domain
of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas.

Because there is no grasping at objects,
it is not that there is no cessation;
as in the domain of the noble ones,
the reality of suchness exists.
As it appears to the wise,
it does not appear to the childish:
how all phenomena are unborn
and appear without characteristics.
Just as the childish imagine as gold
a heap of brass that looks like gold but is not gold,
so bad logicians imagine phenomena.

That through conditions, from existence and nonexistence,
something that had not been arises,
and having arisen perishes: such a claim
does not stand in my teaching.
Transformation, time and shape,
the elemental entities and the faculties,
and the taking up of the intermediate state:
those who imagine these are not wise.

The Victors do not proclaim
the world to be dependently originated;
in this world there are only conditions,
like a city of the gandharvas.
'When this exists, this comes to be':
this is a conventional expression of the Dharma;
what is other than convention
is unborn and does not cease.

In eyes, in water, in mirrors,
in vessels and in jewels,
reflections appear in these,
yet the reflection exists nowhere at all.
Just as a mirage in the sky
appears as a thing, so too the mind;
like a dream, like a barren woman's son,
it appears in forms of many kinds.

The Great Vehicle, my vehicle, is not a vehicle;
it has no letters and no sound;
it is not truth, it is not liberation,
it is not the domain of the imageless.
Yet the Great Vehicle is a vehicle
that gives mastery over samādhi,
the mind-made body of various kinds,
adorned with the flowers of the masteries.

Entities and conditions
are neither one nor different;
arisings are taught in brief,
cessations in detail.
Unborn emptiness is one,
and the emptiness of what is born is also one;
unborn emptiness is supreme,
the emptiness of what is born will perish.
Emptiness, suchness and the ultimate limit,
nirvāṇa and the dharmadhātu,
the mind-made body of various kinds:
these I have taught as synonyms."

Whoever conceives of purity through the Sūtras,
The Vinaya and the Abhidharma,
By their words and not by their meaning,
Does not abide in selflessness.

Neither I, nor the buddhas,
Nor the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), nor anyone at all,
Has ever established existence through conditions;
How, then, could anything come to exist?
And if someone established existence through conditions,
What nonexistence would they have established?
Those who proclaim arising, through their wrong views,
Conceive of existence and nonexistence.

For one for whom nothing arises
And nothing comes to cease,
Since he sees the world as void,
Existence and nonexistence do not approach.

Appearances are like a rabbit's horn;
What exists is people's conceptual thought.
Like deer chasing a mirage,
Those who conceive are mistaken.
Through clinging to conceptual thought,
Conceptuality arises in abundance.
Freed from arising and destruction,
One moves within the imageless.

Profound, vast and extensive wisdom
And perfect buddha fields
Are taught to the children of the Victor.
To the śrāvakas, nonconceptuality:
The three realms of existence impermanent and empty,
Free of self and of what belongs to self.
Conceptual thought that has no cause
Cannot be called conceptual thought.

Just as, in a mirage,
Where there is no water, one takes it for water,
So do the childish toward appearances.
The noble ones are distinct:
The view of the noble ones is pure,
Arisen from the three gateways of liberation.
Specific and general characteristics
Are taught to the śrāvakas.
Nonattachment to all phenomena,
Living alone in solitude,
And the fruit that is not reached by reflection
Are taught to the heirs who are pratyekabuddhas.

The external is the imagined nature;
The dependent arises from its own ground.
Not seeing the delusion that arises from oneself,
Minds therefore run on and on.

The tenth is the first,
The first is the eighth,
The ninth is the seventh,
And the seventh is the eighth;
The second is the third,
The fourth is the fifth,
And the third is the sixth:
In the imageless, what sequence is there?

When things do not appear,
For the yogin there is no nonbeing either.
Through the sameness of being and nonbeing,
The fruit arises for the noble ones.

How are things without thingness?
How is sameness brought about?
When the mind does not cognize
The agitation of outer and inner,
Then it brings about cessation,
And the sameness of mind becomes even.

In saṃsāra, which is without beginning,
The childish are shrouded by grasping at things.
Just as one peg drives out another,
By enticing them, their grasping is turned back.

Having it as cause, it takes it as its object;
It is the support on which mentation (manas) operates;
It also gives forth the cause of mind,
And consciousness too abides in it.

Born of ripening, granted by blessing,
Arising from class and from destiny,
And what is gained in dreams:
The superknowledges are of four kinds.
Whatever is gained in dreams,
Whatever the buddhas grant,
Whatever comes from class, destiny or lineage,
These are not superknowledges born of ripening.

Mind, imbued with habitual tendencies,
Runs on, appearing as things.
When the childish do not understand this,
Then arising is to be taught.

As long as one conceives the external
As things possessed of characteristics,
And does not see one's own error,
So long does mind proliferate.

For whatever reason arising is spoken of,
For that reason appearance is spoken of.
Appearances are not appearances:
Whose, then, of what, and what is said?

Mind is by nature clear;
It is mentation that makes it turbid.
Mentation, together with the consciousnesses,
Constantly plants habitual tendencies.
The storehouse (ālaya) casts the body off;
Mentation longs for a destiny;
Consciousness, seeing the error
That appears as objects, becomes attached.

It is my mind that appears,
And there is no external object.
Meditate thus upon the error,
And keep suchness, too, in mind.

The domain of meditators, karma,
And the greatness of the buddhas:
These three are inconceivable;
The sphere of consciousness is inconceivable.

The future and the past,
Space, the person and nirvāṇa:
These are taught conventionally;
In the ultimate sense there are no letters.

Those within the schools and the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas)
Abide in one and the same view;
Because they are confused about mind-only,
They conceive of external things.

If in a dream one sees consecration,
Pratyekabuddhas and buddhas,
And arhat buddhas,
The seed of awakening grows.

Where are they, and of what kind?
From what, and for what reason?
And the pacifying of the aspects of the illusory mind,
And the positions of existence and nonexistence: please tell me of these.

To those confused about mind-only,
Existence and nonexistence are taught as like an illusion,
As possessed of arising and destruction,
And known as characteristic and what is characterized.

What is called mentation, which conceptualizes,
Together with the five consciousnesses,
Like reflections, like the water of a river,
Arises from the seeds of mind.
When mind, mentation
And consciousness no longer operate,
At that time one attains the mind-made body
And the ground of buddhahood as well.

Conditions, elements and aggregates,
The intrinsic nature of phenomena,
Designations, minds and persons
Are like a dream, like tangled hairs before the eyes.
Seeing the world as a dream, as an illusion,
Abide in what is genuine.
Suchness is free of characteristics,
Beyond reasoning and logical proof;
The personal self-awareness of the noble ones:
Always keep its practice in mind.

Set the world, confused about reasoning and causes,
Within suchness.
Because all conceptual elaborations are pacified,
Wisdom does not enter into error.
As long as one conceives in error,
So long does mind operate through delusion.

Absence of intrinsic nature, and things,
Emptiness, permanence and impermanence:
These are the views of those who proclaim arising,
Not of those who proclaim non-arising.

The world as one, as different, or as both,
Is imagined through Īśvara, through chance,
Through time, the Principal (pradhāna), atoms,
And conditions.

Consciousness is the seed of saṃsāra;
When there is a seed, it arises,
Like a painting when there is a wall;
Once fully understood, it ceases.

Just as people, like illusory men,
Die and are born,
So, because the childish are deluded,
Bondage and liberation appear.

There are two kinds, outer and inner:
Phenomena and conditions.
Meditating thoroughly in that yoga,
One should abide in the imageless.

Mind is differentiated by habitual tendencies,
Yet mind and tendencies are not distinct;
Mind is not distinct in its characteristic,
Yet it is wrapped all around by tendencies.
Regard the tendencies arising
From mental consciousness as stains.
Mind is like a white cloth;
It is made unlovely by the tendencies.

Just as I have taught that space
Is neither an entity nor a non-entity,
So too the universal ground (ālaya) in the body
Is free of both entity and non-entity.

Mind itself, freed of turbidity,
Is the reversal of mental consciousness.
Because it knows all phenomena,
I declare that mind is the Buddha.

The three continua are explained:
Free of existence and nonexistence,
Fully liberated from the four extremes,
Becoming is always like an illusion.

The seven grounds that arise from mind
Have an essential nature that is twofold.
The other grounds and the buddha ground
Are the fully consummate.

The form realm and the formless realm,
The desire realm and nirvāṇa,
And all the domains of mind as well,
Are taught to be within this very body.

As long as there is apprehending,
So long does error come into play.
When one realizes error to be one's own mind,
There is no engaging and no turning back.

There is no birth, and no cause either.
It is through entities that one clings to saṃsāra.
When one sees them as like illusions and the rest,
One does not imagine characteristics.

For the childish, those of feeble intellect,
I myself teach one vehicle, three vehicles,
And no vehicle at all.
For the noble ones there is solitude itself.

Characteristics and conception:
These are my twofold birth.
The teaching of the method is fourfold:
The teaching of reasoning and the established tenet.

Through the distinctions of shape and feature,
Seeing error, they conceive.
Once freed from name and shape,
Intrinsic nature is the pure domain of the noble ones.

As long as conceptuality conceives,
So long is there the imagined characteristic.
Because there is neither imagined nor imagining,
Intrinsic nature is the domain of the noble ones.

Permanent, everlasting, reality itself,
The essential nature of lineage and of entities:
Suchness is free from mind,
And conceptions too have been abandoned.

If there were no entity, there would be no purification,
And no one at all would have afflictions.
Since the mind is pure,
And yet total affliction appears,

Therefore the wholly pure domain of the noble ones,
Reality itself, becomes an entity.
Mind is produced by conditions
And multiplied by conceptions.

When one sees it as like dreams, illusions and so on,
One becomes fully awakened.
The various habitual tendencies, the states of dysfunction,
Are thoroughly joined together with the mind.

To people external things appear,
But that is not the true nature of mind.
The true nature of mind is pure;
Mind arises from error.

Error is the state of dysfunction,
And for that reason mind is not seen.
Suchness is in mere error;
Suchness does not exist apart.

It is not in the formations, nor is it apart from them,
And yet it is through seeing the formations.
When one sees that conditioned things
Are free of characteristic and of what is characterized,

By seeing beings as one's own mind,
For that reason they are abandoned.
Abiding in mind-only,
One should not imagine external objects.

Abiding in the true object,
One should go beyond mind-only.
Having gone beyond mind-only,
One should go beyond the nonappearing.

When the yogin abides in nonappearance,
He does not see the Great Vehicle.
Spontaneous, realized, peaceful,
Fully purified by aspirations,

It is the supreme wisdom that is selfless;
When there is no appearance, it is not seen.
One should look at the domain of mind,
And look as well at the domain of wisdom.

When one sees the domain of discernment,
One will not be confused about characteristics.
The truth of suffering belongs to mind;
The origin is the domain of wisdom.

The two truths and the buddha ground:
Therein discernment fully engages.
Attaining the fruit and nirvāṇa,
And likewise the eightfold path:

Because all phenomena are realized,
The wisdom of the Buddha becomes wholly pure.
Eye and form and light,
Attention and likewise space:

Through these, for people,
Consciousness arises from the universal ground.
The grasped, the grasping, and the grasper,
Name, non-entity, and nonexistence,

And conceptuality without a cause:
Those who think in this way are not wise.
Name has not arisen from the object,
Nor likewise the object from the name.

Do not conceive of conceptions
As arising from a cause or without a cause.
All entities lack intrinsic nature,
And words likewise do not exist.

Not seeing emptiness and the meaning of emptiness,
The childish run astray.
Seeing, with conceit, the positing of truth,
This is an account of what is merely designated.

Since the single one is established as fivefold,
This so-called true one is abandoned.
Elaboration is seized by Māra;
One should pass beyond existence and nonexistence.

Seeing wrong perception as selfless,
One should not yearn for becoming.
Where there is the act of examining,
Mere dispute comes into play.

The supreme truth cannot be spoken;
When this has ceased, the Dharma is seen.
Relying on the universal ground,
Mentation fully comes into operation.

Relying on mind and mentation,
Consciousness comes into operation.
The superimposed and the superimposing,
The true nature of mind, suchness:

When the yogin meditates on this,
He will attain the knowledge of mind-only.
Regarding mentation, characteristics and entities,
There is no conceit of permanent or impermanent.

In the yoga of arising and non-arising,
The yogin has no conceit.
The consciousness that arises from the universal ground
Conceives of two objects.

The single object arising from it
Is not known by two minds.
There is no speaking and there are no words;
Because mind is seen, it is not empty.

Because one's own mind is not seen,
The net of views comes into play.
Conditions do not come;
There are no entities whatsoever.

There are no elements and no aggregates,
No attachment and nothing conditioned.
There is no karma and no fire, nor any before;
Nothing done and nothing conditioned.

There is no extreme and no clinging,
No liberation and no bondage.
There is nothing indeterminate and no entity,
No dharma and no non-dharma.

There is no time and no nirvāṇa,
And even the nature of reality does not exist.
There are no buddhas and no truths,
No fruit and no causes.

There is no distortion and no nirvāṇa,
No arising and no destruction.
The twelve links do not exist,
Nor is there end or endlessness.

In order to abandon all views,
I have taught mind-only.

Body, afflictions, karma, and path,
the agent and the results,
are like a dream and a mirage,
like a city of the gandharvas.

By establishing everything as mind only,
one turns away from the marks of entities.
Because one abides in mind only,
one comes to see permanence and annihilation.

In nirvāṇa there are no aggregates,
there is no self, and there are no characteristics.
Through entering into mind only,
one turns back from grasping at liberation.

What has surely arisen from the cause that makes earth appear
appears to people as something external.
Mind arises from what has no appearance;
therefore the mind does not appear.

To people, habitual tendencies
appear as body, possessions, and abode.
Mind is neither an entity nor a non-entity;
through habitual tendencies it does not shine.

On white, a stain appears;
on a stain, the white does not appear.
Like clouds in the sky,
so too the mind does not appear.

Mind accumulates karma;
wisdom clears it away.
Through discernment one attains
the imageless, and powers as well.

Mind is bound up with objects;
cognition engages in reasonings.
In the imageless and the distinctive,
discernment fully engages.

Those who have abandoned mind, mentation, consciousness,
perception, and conceptualization,
and so attained the nonconceptual Dharma,
are śrāvakas, not the Victor's children.

In the peaceful, distinctive forbearance
lies the virtuous wisdom of the Tathāgata.
Free of all activity,
it arises by virtue of that distinction.

The imagined nature exists;
the dependent does not exist.
The deluded grasp at the imagined;
the dependent is impermanent.

All form derived from the elements does not exist;
form not derived from the elements exists.
A city of gandharvas, a dream,
an illusion, a mirage, are not derived from the elements.

My discernment is threefold:
that by which the noble ones are set apart,
that by which true characteristics are examined,
and that by which entities are analyzed.

The discernment of śrāvakas, and that which, rid of appearances,
is free of the two vehicles:
the first, because it clings to entities as existent,
comes fully into operation;
the other is entry into mind only,
the stainless discernment of the Sugata.

If things were produced by conditions
out of existence and nonexistence,
they would without doubt remain
in views of oneness and difference.

Just as an illusion produced
from many components is not established,
so too the various signs,
when examined, are not established.

The bondage of signs and of dysfunctional tendencies
arises from the mind.
Through not understanding the imagined,
one conceives of it as the dependent.

This entity that is imagined
is itself also the dependent.
The various appearances of the imagined
are conceived by way of the dependent.

The conventional and the ultimate:
there is no third arisen from causes.
The imagined is called the conventional;
its cutting off is the domain of the noble ones.

Like the object of a yogin's meditation,
variety shines forth as one.
In it there are no various aspects;
so too with the mark of the imagined.

Just as those with cataracts,
seeing forms, conceive of variety,
while the floaters are neither form nor formless,
so is the dependent for the wise.

Just as gold that has been purified,
water rid of its turbidity,
and the sky without clouds,
so is the purification of conceptuality.

My śrāvakas are threefold:
the emanated, those born of aspiration,
and śrāvakas arisen from the Dharma,
who have abandoned attachment and aversion.

Bodhisattvas too are threefold.
Buddhas have no characteristics;
in mind after mind of sentient beings
the form of the Buddha appears.

The imagined has no entity;
the dependent exists.
Superimposition and denial,
when examined, are destroyed.

If, while the imagined is no entity,
the dependent were an entity,
what is no entity would become an entity,
and an entity would arise from a non-entity.

Taking the imagined as its basis,
the dependent is apprehended.
From the connection of name and sign
the imagined arises.

When the imagined is utterly unestablished,
it has not arisen from another.
Then the pure essential nature
of the ultimate is known.

Imagination is tenfold;
the dependent is sixfold.
What is individually self-realized is suchness;
therefore there is no distinction.

The five dharmas are suchness,
and so too are the three natures.
When a yogin meditates on this,
he does not pass beyond suchness.

Like stars and clouds,
like the sun and the moon,
like appearances arising from habitual tendencies,
so do the minds of people appear.

These elements have no body;
they lack characteristics and lack a basis for characteristics.
If they are elements of elements,
and form is derived from the elements,
then the great elements are not real,
and no element is derived from elements.

The great elements are the cause;
the results are earth, water, and so on.
Form that is substantial and form that is imputed,
likewise that made by illusion,
the forms of dreams and of gandharva cities,
and the mirage: these are five.

Those given to desire are of five kinds,
and the lineages likewise are five.
There are five vehicles and the no-vehicle;
nirvāṇa is sixfold.

The distinct aggregates are twenty-four;
form, too, is eightfold.
The buddhas are twenty-four;
the Victor's children are of two kinds.

The approaches are one hundred and eight;
the śrāvakas likewise are of three kinds.
The field of the buddhas is one,
and likewise the Buddha is one.

Liberation likewise is threefold;
the mental continuum is fourfold.
My selflessness is sixfold;
the knowable, too, is fourfold.

Free of causes,
rid of the faults of views,
individually self-realized and unmoving,
is the unsurpassed Great Vehicle.

Arising and non-arising
are eightfold and ninefold.
Realization is at once and gradual;
the tenet, too, is one alone.

The formless realm is of eight kinds;
the dhyānas are distinct, of six kinds;
for pratyekabuddhas and the heirs,
emergence comes to be of seven kinds.

The three times, too, do not exist;
there is neither permanence nor impermanence.
Agent, action and result
are like the deeds done in a dream.

Buddhas, śrāvakas, heirs of the Victor,
have no arising at the first or at the last.
They are free from the appearances of mind,
always like phenomena that are illusions.

The womb, the wheel, and likewise birth,
the coming forth and the abode of Tuṣita,
and the going to all the fields as well:
though these appear, there is no birth from a womb.

Transmigration, cause and sentient beings,
and likewise teaching and nirvāṇa,
the truths and the realization of fields,
are projected by conditions.

The world, the forests and the continents,
non-Buddhists, transmigration and selflessness,
dhyāna, the vehicles, the abodes and attainments,
and the domain of the fruits: these are inconceivable.

The lineages of the moon and of the stars,
the royal lineages and those of the gods,
the lineages of yakṣas and of gandharvas,
are born of karma and arise from existence.

When there is inconceivable transformation,
death and transmigration bear habitual tendencies.
When the appearance of death and transmigration is cut off,
the net of afflictions ceases.

Cattle, sheep and servants,
and likewise elephants, horses and the like,
wealth, grain and gold,
and fields and property: these are conceptually imagined.

One should not lie down on beds,
nor should one smear the ground.
Alms bowls of gold, of silver, of bronze,
or of copper, one should not keep.

Cloth dyed blue,
cow dung, ochre,
earth, fruit and leaves:
with these the yogin should always change what is white.

A bowl made of iron, of stone, of clay,
of conch or of crystal,
of the measure of one Magadhan measure,
is what the yogin should keep.

The curved knife for cutting cloth
should be made four finger-widths in size.
The yogin devoted to yoga
should not train in the arts of craft.

The yogin who has entered yoga
should not buy and sell.
Let a lay attendant of the monastery do that;
this I have taught as the Dharma.

With senses restrained and knowing the meaning,
and likewise in sūtra and vinaya,
not mingling with householders:
this I have taught for the yogin.

In an empty house or a charnel ground,
at the foot of a tree or in caves,
in the open air or on a heap of straw,
the yogin should make his dwelling.

In a charnel ground or anywhere else,
one should always wear the three robes.
Whatever is given gladly
should be sewn together to make robes.

Intent on food gained by begging,
one should look ahead a yoke's length.
As a bee goes among the flowers,
so should one go on the alms round.

Mingling with great crowds,
and whatever mingling there is with nuns,
a livelihood that has become mingled in this way
is not fitting for the yogin.

The yogin devoted to yoga
should not attend upon kings and princes,
nor likewise upon ministers and merchant chiefs,
for the sake of food.

Food from houses where there has been a death or a birth,
food from those bound to one by affection and friendship,
and mingling with monks and nuns:
these are not fitting for the yogin.

A monastery from which smoke is rising,
where cooking is constantly done by the regular rite,
and whatever is prepared for one's own sake:
these are not fitting for the yogin.

Seeing the world, with its bases of characteristics and its characterized,
as freed from arising and perishing,
and abandoning the sides of existence and nonexistence,
the yogin should meditate on yoga.

If one does not conceive of arising,
then with samādhi and the powers,
the superknowledges and mastery,
one swiftly becomes a yogin.

One should not imagine the world as coming from atoms, from time,
from the principal or from a creator.
The yogin should not imagine
the world as arising from causes and conditions.

The world is imagined by one's own conceptuality;
it arises from various habitual tendencies.
The yogin should always see
existence as like an illusion or a dream.

Always viewing things as free
from superimposition and denial,
having gone well upon the path of peace,
one undertakes the various yogas.

Do not conceive the three realms of existence
as resembling body, enjoyments and abode.
Having finished the task of eating,
straightening the body, free of thoughts,

bowing again and again
to the buddhas and to awakening,
the one who knows yoga, having fully gathered
sūtra, vinaya and reasoning,

should meditate on the five dharmas, on one's own mind,
and on selflessness.
Personal self-awareness, the pure nature of reality,
the grounds and the buddha ground:

if the yogin meditates on these,
he will be consecrated upon a great lotus.
Having wandered through all the destinies,
with a mind weary of existence,

if the yogin exerts himself,
he sees what resembles the shape of the sun and moon,
what is like light and a lotus,
what resembles patterns, the sky and fire.

Those various signs
lead one onto the path of the non-Buddhists;
they also make one fall
into the domain of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas.

When, having rejected all of these,
one has become free of appearances,
then the divine hands of the buddhas,
coming forth from all the fields,

stroke his head:
the sign of correctly following.
Saying that things exist from no cause,
free of eternalism and annihilation,

free of the sides of existence and nonexistence,
they imagine this to be the Middle Way.
Those who speak of causelessness
conceive the view of causeless annihilation.

Because they do not understand external things,
they bring about the destruction of the Middle Way.
Thinking, "It would become the view of annihilation,"
they do not give up grasping at things,

and so, by denial and superimposition,
they come to teach the Middle Way.
Through realizing mind-only
and eliminating external things,

conceptuality is turned back;
that very path is the Middle Way.
It is mind-only; there is no appearance.
Because there is no appearance, there is no arising.

This path of the Middle Way
has been taught by me and by others too.
Arising and non-arising,
things and non-things, emptiness,

and the absence of intrinsic nature in things:
one should not conceive these as twofold.
Because conceptuality does not engage,
the childish imagine that to be liberation.

Because they do not understand the engagement of mind,
they do not abandon the two kinds of grasping.
Through understanding appearances as one's own mind,
the two kinds of grasping are abandoned.

Abandoning and grasping
do not destroy conceptuality.
Through fully knowing the appearances of mind,
conceptuality does not engage.

The non-engagement of conceptuality
is suchness, the abandonment of mind.

Free of the faults of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas),
If activity should appear,
Since what has ceased does not come into play again,
The wise have held to this.
So that it may be realized,
I and the other buddhas have taught it as buddhahood.
If one conceives of it in any other way,
It becomes the doctrine of the non-Buddhists.

In the unborn there are many births;
Without dying and passing on, there is dying and passing on.
In millions of fields all at once
It appears like the moon in water.
Becoming one and becoming many,
It blazes as fire and pours down as rain.
Becoming mind within the minds of beings,
They teach that all is mind only.
In minds there is mind only,
And the mindless too arises from mind.
Various forms, figures and shapes:
Know them to be understood as mind only.
In the form of the Lord of Sages and the form of śrāvakas,
In the likeness of pratyekabuddhas,
And in other forms of many kinds,
They teach that these are mind only.
For the formless they take a formless form;
For hell beings, the form of hell.
The causes of mind only as well
They show to sentient beings.
When these have been thoroughly transformed,
The illusion-like concentration,
And the mind-made body likewise,
The masteries and the ten grounds are attained.

By their own inverted conceptuality,
By proliferation and agitation,
By what is heard, seen, known and understood,
And by perceptions, the childish are bound.
The dependent nature is the sign;
Whatever is name is the imputed;
Therefore conceptualizing and signs
Arise wholly from the dependent.
When the intellect has taken them fully apart,
There is no imputed and no dependent.
If there is no perfected entity,
How could the intellect conceive of it?
There is a perfected entity
That is free of existence and nonexistence.
What is liberated from the existence and nonexistence of entities,
How could it become two natures?
In the imputed nature
Two natures abide:
The imputed appears in various ways;
The pure is the domain of the noble ones.
The imputed appearing in variety
Is conceived of as "the dependent."
If one conceives of it in any other way,
One comes to abide in the doctrine of the non-Buddhists.
What is imagined by conceptuality is called the imputed;
Because it is seen, it has arisen from causes.
Freedom from these two conceptions
Is the perfected itself.

The Noble Descent into Laṅkā, the ninth and final fascicle.

Fields and emanated buddhas,
The one vehicle and likewise three,
Nirvāṇa and the absence of self:
All are empty and free of arising.
The thirty-six distinctions of buddhas,
The ten distinctions,
And the individual mind-streams of sentient beings:
These are the vessels of the fields.
Just as an imputed thing
Appears to the eye as various,
Yet variety itself is not in it,
So too are beings and the Dharma buddha.
The Dharma buddha is the buddha;
The rest are its emanations.
Sentient beings, through the continuum of their own seeds,
See the buddha appearing.
From connection with signs
Erroneous conceptuality proceeds.
Suchness is not other than conceptuality,
Nor is conceptuality other than signs.
Whatever buddhas there are, of the intrinsic nature, of complete enjoyment,
Of magical display, and of emanation,
The many buddhas, thirty-six,
Are the buddha of the intrinsic nature.

Blue and red, salt,
Conch, milk and sugar,
Astringent fruits, flowers and the like,
Just as the rays from the sun,
And the ocean with its waves,
Are neither one nor different,
So it is with the seven consciousnesses
Together with the mind.
The transformation of the ocean
Is those various waves;
Likewise from the ālaya arise
The various things called consciousness.
Mind, mentation and consciousness
Are designated for the sake of characteristics.
The eight are not distinct in character;
There is no basis of characterization and no characteristic.
Just as the ocean and its waves
Are without difference,
So in the mind the transformation
Into consciousnesses is not found.

Mind accumulates karma;
Mentation, too, gathers it fully;
Consciousness cognizes;
The five imagine what appears.

"In the aspects of blue and red
People's consciousness appears.
The likeness of the waves to mind,
Great Sage, please explain how it holds."

"The aspects of blue and red
Do not exist in the waves.
For the sake of characteristics, to the childish,
The functioning of mind is taught.
In it there is no functioning;
One's own mind is free of anything grasped.
If there were something grasped, the grasper too
Would be established as like the waves.
As body, enjoyments and abode
People's consciousness appears;
Therefore its functioning
Appears to be like the waves."

"If the ocean, in the form of waves,
Is perceived as dancing,
Why is the functioning of the ālaya likewise
Not cognized by the intellect?"

"Because the childish lack understanding,
The ālaya is likened to the ocean;
Because it parallels the arising of waves,
The example is applied."

"Just as the sun rises
On people good, bad and middling,
So you, lamp of the world,
Teach the truth to the childish.
Having fully entered into the Dharma,
Why do you not speak of suchness?"

"If suchness were to be spoken of,
There is no suchness in the mind.
Just as the waves of the ocean,
Or as in a mirror or a dream,
Appear all at the same time,
So too the mind in its own domain.
Since it is not joined with objects,
It arises by gradual functioning.
Consciousness cognizes;
Mentation, too, imagines;
Appearance is manifest to the five;
In meditative equipoise there is no sequence.
Just as some master painter
Or a painter's apprentice
Lays out colors for the sake of a painting,
So is my teaching.
The painting is not in the colors,
Nor in the brush, nor in the bowl.
In order to guide sentient beings
A painting in colors is devised.
Reality is free of letters;
Explanations are deceptive."

Establishing a footing in the dharmas,
the truth is taught to yogins.
The truth itself, realized by individual self-awareness,
free of imagining and of what is imagined,
is what is taught to the Victor's heirs;
to the childish it is explained in other ways.

Just as the various illusions
appear and yet do not exist,
so too the various teachings, though illusory,
are taught in just that way.
What is taught to one
is not what is explained to another.
Just as a physician gives his medicines
to each patient according to the patient,
so the buddhas teach sentient beings,
and teach them also as mind-only.

From the seeds of habitual tendencies toward externals,
conceptual thought arises.
That by which one grasps is the dependent;
what is grasped is the imagined.
Taking an external referent as what is grasped,
and relying on mind as well, it comes to arise.
Error arises in two ways;
there is no cause for a third.
That from which error arises,
and that on which it depends in arising:
for that reason I have taught
the twelve, the eighteen and the six with regard to mind.

From the seeds of self bound up with what is grasped,
clinging to a self arises.
When mind engages in examination,
grasping at phenomena is given up.
The consciousnesses that arise
from the all-base consciousness
are the inner sense sources;
whatever appears is outer.

Just as the unskilled
grasp at hairs and the forms of dreams,
so they imagine the conditioned and the unconditioned
to be permanent, yet these do not exist.
Just as a city of gandharvas,
an illusion, and the water of a mirage
appear though they do not exist,
so too is the dependent.

Designations of a self and of sense faculties
I do not teach with regard to mind.
Mind, mentation and consciousness
are free of characteristics of their own.
Mind, mentation and consciousness,
likewise the two kinds of selflessness,
the five dharmas and the natures:
these are the domain of the buddhas.

By their characteristics they become three,
arising from the single cause of habitual tendencies,
just as a single pigment
appears in various ways upon a wall.
Mind and the two selflessnesses,
mentation and consciousness,
the natures and the five dharmas:
these do not exist in my family.
Free of the characteristics of mind,
having abandoned consciousness and mentation,
the absence of intrinsic nature in phenomena:
this is the family of the Tathāgata.

With body, speech and mind
one should act virtuously in it.
The family of the Tathāgata is pure,
free of all wandering.
Pure through the superknowledges and the masteries,
adorned with meditative absorptions and powers,
with various mind-made bodies:
this is the virtuous family of the Tathāgata.
Stainless individual self-awareness,
free of the characteristic of cause,
the eighth ground and the buddha ground:
this is the family of the Tathāgata.

Excellent Intelligence (Sādhumatī), Gone Afar (Dūraṃgamā),
Cloud of Dharma (Dharmameghā), and the ground of the Tathāgata:
these are the family of the buddhas;
the rest attain the two vehicles.
Because of the distinctions in beings' mindstreams
and because of their characteristics, for the childish
the buddhas taught the seven grounds
that come under the sway of mind.

The dysfunctional tendencies of speech, body and mind
do not occur on the seventh ground;
on the eighth ground their basis
is like a dream or a flowing river.
On the eighth ground and the fifth,
the Victor's heirs know
the crafts, the sciences and the arts,
and in the abodes of existence they even act as kings.

Arising and not arising, empty and not empty:
the wise should not imagine these.
Intrinsic nature and absence of intrinsic nature
do not exist in mind-only.
"This is true, this is true,
this is false": such imagining
is taught to pratyekabuddhas and śrāvakas,
not to the heirs of the Victor.

In mind-only there is no existence;
existence and nonexistence do not come from existence.
There is no momentariness and no characteristic;
there is neither the imputed nor substance.
Conventionally, things exist;
ultimately, they have no intrinsic nature.
Error with regard to what has no intrinsic nature:
that is conventional truth.

Upon all phenomena, which do not exist,
I have made designations.
The verbal conduct of the childish
is utterly devoid of the true.
Things that arise from expression
have a domain of objects;
things that arise from expression
do not exist when looked at.

Like a painting without a wall,
or a shadow apart from a tree stump and the like,
so the pure all-base
is not marred by waves.
Mind abides like a dancer,
mentation is like a jester;
consciousness, together with the five,
imagines appearances like colors.

The teaching of Dharma that accords with the cause,
and whatever is emanated by what accords with the cause:
these are the buddhas of essence;
the rest are emanation bodies.
There are no appearances of various kinds;
mind is deluded by appearance.
As though it were body, enjoyments and abode,
the all-base appears to people.

Mind, mentation and consciousness,
the natures and the five dharmas,
and the two selflessnesses, purified:
these the Guide explains.
The domain of individual self-awareness
that the perfect Buddha explains
is not the domain of logicians,
nor is it that of the śrāvakas.

Long and short and so forth are related;
each comes about through the other.
What proves existence is nonexistence;
what proves nonexistence is existence.
When substance is analyzed into atoms,
one does not conceive of form.
The establishment of mind-only
those of bad views do not believe.

"Empty," "mere conceptual thought,"
or "not empty": such statements
imagine that very thing as existent or nonexistent;
there is no object to be examined.
Through the aggregation of qualities, atoms and substances,
the childish imagine form.
Not a single atom exists;
for that reason too there are no objects.

One's own mind, the shape of appearance,
appears to people as external.
There is no external appearance;
for that reason too there are no objects.
Various tangled hair-nets, illusions,
dreams, cities of gandharvas,
firebrand circles and mirages,
though nonexistent, appear vividly to the eye.

Permanent and impermanent, likewise one,
two and not two, likewise:
bound by faults since beginningless time,
the deluded childish imagine these.
There is no establishing of vehicles;
I have taught a single vehicle.
In order to guide the childish,
I have taught different vehicles.

Likewise the three liberations,
the selflessness of phenomena,
the wisdom of sameness, and freedom from affliction:
these are forgone through that liberation.

Just as a log upon the ocean
is tossed about by the waves,
so too the deluded śrāvaka
is tossed about by characteristics.

For him there is no final consummation,
yet he does not turn back either;
having gained the body of samādhi,
he does not awaken for eons.

Bound to the habitual tendencies of affliction,
yet free of their active outbursts,
they are drunk with the intoxication of samādhi
and dwell in the undefiled realm.

Just as a drunken man
becomes sober once the liquor is gone,
so at that time they will attain
my Dharma body of a buddha.

Just as an elephant sunk in the mire
cannot run this way or that,
so the śrāvakas remain,
sunk in the mire of samādhi.

Through the blessing of the Lord of Men,
empowerment, samādhis and the rest,
purified by aspirations,
arise from the first stage through the tenth.

The horns of a hare, the sky,
and a barren woman's son as well:
just as these are spoken of in words though they do not exist,
so too is conceptualizing about things.

The world arises from the cause of habitual tendencies;
it exists nowhere at all, being neither existent nor nonexistent.
Those skilled in the selflessness of phenomena,
when they see this, become liberated.

Name is the imputed nature;
what arises from another is born of dependence;
suchness is the perfected nature:
this I have always taught in sūtra after sūtra.

Words and letters in great number,
and names in all their particulars:
foolish children of poor understanding cling to them
like an elephant stuck in the mire.

The vehicle of the gods, the vehicle of Brahmā,
and likewise that of the śrāvakas,
of the pratyekabuddhas, and of the tathāgatas:
these vehicles I have taught.

So long as the mind still engages,
there is no end to the vehicles;
when the mind has been fully transformed,
there is no vehicle and no one who goes.

Mind, conceptuality, cognition,
mentation, and consciousness itself,
the ālaya, the stirrings of the three realms of existence:
these are synonyms of mind.

Life, warmth, consciousness,
the ālaya, the life faculty,
mentation, and mental consciousness:
these are distinctions of conceptual thought.

Mind takes hold of the body;
mentation constantly conceives a self;
consciousness, together with the consciousnesses,
discriminates the objects of mind.

Craving is taught to be the mother,
and ignorance is known as the father;
because consciousness cognizes objects,
I have taught it to be the Buddha.

The latent tendencies are the arhat;
the gathering of the five aggregates is the Saṅgha.
Because these are severed without interval,
that is a deed of immediate retribution.

The two selflessnesses and the afflictions,
likewise the two obscurations,
and inconceivable death and transmigration:
through attaining these, one is a Tathāgata.

The established tenets and their ways,
and the teaching as well, individually self-known:
whoever sees and knows their distinction
does not come under the sway of conceptuality.

If, just as fools imagine,
things have no truth,
why do the logicians not accept
that the absence of things is no extreme?

Those who see conditioned things
as bound up with arising and perishing
foster the two views;
they do not understand conditions.

Nirvāṇa, the abandonment of mentation,
that alone is the truth.
The imagined world should be regarded
as an illusion, like a plantain trunk.

Attachment, aversion,
and delusion exist, but there is no person.
The aggregates arise from existence
and are like dreams.

From the night on which I awakened
to the night I passed into complete nirvāṇa,
in all that time between
I have not taught even the slightest thing.

With the way of the individually self-known Dharma in mind
I have fully taught;
between me and those buddhas
there is not the slightest difference.

The self exists as though a substance;
the aggregates are free of its characteristics;
the aggregates exist as entities,
but in them there is no self.

Beset by afflictions and their latent tendencies,
by meditating with discernment,
when beings see their own mind
they are freed from all suffering.

Those who say that the world comes forth
through causes and conditions
and through the reasoning of the four extremes
are not skilled in my way.

The world does not arise as existent or nonexistent;
neither nonexistent nor existent, it is nowhere at all.
How then do fools imagine it
by way of causes and conditions?

When one sees the worlds
as neither existent, nor nonexistent, nor both existent and nonexistent,
then the mind turns back,
and selflessness too is realized.

Because they arise from conditions,
all things are unborn;
all conditions are results,
and craving does not arise toward a result.

Because from a result there would be duality,
a result does not arise from a result;
because of the fault of duality,
no entity is found arising from a result.

When conditioned things
are free of the observed and the observing,
and one sees mind-only, without mind,
that I declare to be mind-only.

Mind is the very form of essential nature,
free of conditions and of entities;
the ultimate essence, the supreme brahman:
of this I speak as "only."

The self is a truth by imputation;
it does not exist as a substance.
The aggregates and aggregate-hood likewise
exist by imputation, not as substances.

Sameness is of four kinds:
of characteristics, of cause, of what arises from entities,
and the sameness of selflessness,
the fourth, for those engaged in yoga.

Turning away all views,
free of what is imagined and of imagining,
without object and unborn:
this I explain as mind-only.

Neither an entity nor a non-entity,
suchness, free of entity and non-entity,
free of mind:
this I explain as mind-only.

Emptiness, the ultimate limit, and suchness,
nirvāṇa and the dharmadhātu,
the manifold mind-made body:
these I explain as mind-only.

Bound up with the habitual tendencies of conceptuality,
the manifold that arises from mind
and appears to people as external:
that is the mind-only of the world.

There is no external appearance;
as body, enjoyments and abode,
mind appears in manifold forms:
this I explain as mind-only.

The wisdom of exhaustion of the śrāvakas,
The birth and arising of the buddhas,
And that of the pratyekabuddhas
Come forth fully through the absence of afflictions.

There is no external form;
It is one's own mind that appears as external.
Because they do not comprehend the mind,
The childish conceive of the conditioned.

Not knowing that external objects
Are one's own mind appearing in variety,
The foolish set out to refute this
With reasons framed to fit a thesis.

There is no reason and no thesis,
Nor are there examples or the members of proof.
One's own mind is the stream of objects;
Whoever knows this is wise.

Whatever is imagined by conceptual thought
Has the imagined characteristic.
With the imagined as its basis,
Conceptual thought sets in motion.

Mutually unseparated and connected,
They arise from the single cause, habitual tendencies.
Because the two arise adventitiously,
The mind of people does not arise.

Mind and mental factors that dwell
In the three realms of existence are conceptual thought.
Whatever engages as the appearance of objects
Is the imagined nature.

Because they possess the seeds of appearance,
The sense sources are twelve.
Because they have a basis and an object,
I have explained them as a mere account.

As a mirror holds a reflection,
And one with blurred vision sees tangled hairs,
So mind veiled by habitual tendencies
Is what the childish see.

Toward objects imagined by their own thought
Conceptuality sets in motion.
External objects do not exist
In the way the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) imagine.

Just as the childish, not knowing a rope
For what it is, take hold of it as a snake,
So, not knowing objects to be their own mind,
They conceive of them as external objects.

Thus the rope, being simply rope,
Is free of being one or different;
But that which conceives of it as a rope
Is the fault of one's own mind.

Where, examining anything as the entity of anything,
It is not that nothing is observed,
That should not be known as nonexistent;
That is the nature of reality of phenomena.

The counterpart drawn from existence is nonexistence;
The counterpart drawn from nonexistence is existence.
Therefore one should not know it as nonexistent,
Nor imagine it as existent.

When this, which is wholly imagined,
Is examined, it does not have that nature.
Seeing that it does not have that nature,
How could conceptual thought set in motion?

Form has no nature of form,
And likewise pot, cloth, and the rest.
Since appearances do not exist,
Your conceptual thought does not arise.

If your conceiving errs
Regarding the beginningless conditioned,
Who has made entities into real entities
That cycle on? Great Sage, tell me.

Entities have no real entityhood;
It is mind alone that appears.
Because one does not see one's own mind,
Conceptual thought sets in motion.

If the imagined, as the childish imagine it,
Is either existent or nonexistent,
And there were some other mode beside these,
Even the intellect could not conceive it.

If it exists for the noble ones,
It is not what the childish imagine.
If it were contrary to the noble ones,
The noble and the childish would be equal.

Because their minds are fully purified,
The noble ones have no error.
The childish, whose mind-stream is impure,
Conceive of the imagined.

Just as a mother, for her child,
Takes fruits from the sky and says,
"Here, my only child, do not cry;
Take these many kinds of fruit,"

So I, for all sentient beings,
Drawing them on with the various fruits of imagination,
Fully explain the way
That abandons the sides of existence and nonexistence.

If what has not arisen arises
Through conditions and not from connection,
Then what was unborn before is born now,
And it gains no nature of its own.

Without gaining a nature of its own, there is no birth,
Nor is there anything anywhere without conditions.
Those entities that are born, too,
Are nowhere without conditions.

When one sees it thus in this sūtra,
Nowhere is there nonexistence, nor existence, nor their negation.
The wise do not imagine
Entities as arising from conditions.

The non-Buddhists speak to the childish
Of oneness and difference through conditions,
Yet they do not know that beings
Are like dreams and illusions.

The vehicle that is the domain of expression
Is the unsurpassed Great Vehicle.
I have taught its meaning well,
Yet the childish do not know it.

What the miserly śrāvakas
And the non-Buddhists have composed,
Because it is explained through conceptual thought,
Is mistaken about the meaning.

Characteristic, entity, shape,
And name, these four kinds:
Taking these as their objects,
Conceptual thought sets in motion.

Those who hold to one and to many,
Who hold sway in Brahmā's realm,
And over the light of sun and moon as well,
Those who destroy, are not my sons.

Those whose noble vision is perfect,
Who realize and know reality just as it is,
Who are skilled in turning back perception,
And have gone to the far shore of conceptual thought,

They, in my teaching,
Bear the seal of sons who are liberated:
Fully freed from entity and non-entity,
Having abandoned coming and going.

If, when the consciousness of form turns back,
Karma were destroyed,
There would be neither permanence nor impermanence,
And saṃsāra too would cease to be.

What has perished at the time of turning back
Turns back from the object of form;
Karma, free of the faults of existence and nonexistence,
Abides in the foundation of all (ālaya).

In the abode of existence, consciousness
And fallen form are utterly destroyed,
But karma connected with form and consciousness
Is not destroyed.

If a person's karma connected with them
Were destroyed,
Then, since the connection of karma would be severed,
There would be no saṃsāra and no nirvāṇa either.

If, though it has perished, one is born
Into saṃsāra together with it,
Then, since form is connected with it,
It would arise as not different.

Mind, through conceiving of form,
Is neither one nor different.
Because the sides of existence and nonexistence are abandoned,
Entities have no destruction.
