The wholly imagined and the dependent
Are not different from each other in their characteristics;
Like the impermanence of form,
Each also gives rise to the other.

If one does not comprehend the imagined,
Which is fully free of one and different,
How could it become existent or nonexistent?
It is like the impermanence of form.

When the imagined is clearly seen,
The dependent does not arise.

When the dependent is clearly seen,
The imagined becomes suchness.
When the imagined is destroyed,
My way too is brought to destruction.
In this teaching of mine
There is superimposition and denial.
Wherever and whenever there are such people,
Those who disparage the Dharma,
They bring my way to destruction.
Do not keep company with any of them.
Whoever superimposes and denies,
And destroys the imagined,
One should not speak with among the wise;
Monks should truly shun them.

Hair-nets, things like illusions,
Like dreams, like gandharvas,
Conceptions like the appearance of a mirage:
Those who view these as existent or nonexistent,
Who would act to gather them in?
They do not train in what is the Buddha's.
They have fallen into the two extremes,
And they bring others to ruin as well.

Those yogins who see
The imagined thing as wholly void,
Fully liberated from entity and non-entity:
Gather those in and keep their company.

Just as the mines found in the world
From which pearls, jewels and gold come forth
Did not arise from the cause of varied karma,
Yet are the livelihood of people,
So too the classes of sentient beings
Are free of the various kinds of karma.
Because there is no appearance, there is no karma,
Nor are there destinies arisen from karma.

As the noble ones meditate,
Things have no thinghood;
Yet as the childish imagine,
Things do exist.

"If things as the childish imagine them
Did not exist,
Then, since all things would be nonexistent,
Who could ever be afflicted?
Yet from affliction over various things
Comes saṃsāra, the faculties coming forth,
And ignorance and the bond to existence:
The arising of embodied beings takes place."

"Those for whom things do not exist
In the way the childish imagine them,
For them the faculties do not engage;
It is yoga itself that engages."

"Blessed One, if the thing that is
The cause of saṃsāra does not exist,
Then the childish, without any striving,
Would reach liberation, free of activity.
If there are no things, how can there be
A difference between your noble ones and the childish?"

"For the noble ones who practice
The three liberations, there are no things.
There are no aggregates, no person, no phenomena,
No specific and no general characteristics.
The faculties and the conditions
I taught to the śrāvakas.
Causelessness and mind only,
The attainments and the grounds,
And each one's own suchness, the pure,
I taught to the children of the Victor.

Those who disparage my teaching,
Wearing saffron robes,
Proclaiming that a result exists or does not exist,
Will appear in times to come.

There are no things; existence through conditions
Is the domain of the noble ones.
The imagined thing is utterly nonexistent;
The logicians are the ones who imagine it.

In times to come
Kaṇāda, childish by nature, will appear,
And through the evil view of those who say there is no result
He will bring many people to ruin.
'Beings arise from atoms;
Atoms themselves have no cause;
The nine substances too are permanent':
So, with evil view, they will teach.
'Substances are produced by substances,
And qualities likewise by qualities.'
Positing a thinghood of things that is something other,
They bring what exists to ruin.

If what had not arisen were to arise,
The world would have a beginning.
But I have taught that saṃsāra
Has no prior limit.
You who count up the whole of the three realms of existence:
If what had not arisen were to arise,
Then dogs, camels and donkeys too
Would no doubt grow horns.
If form, consciousness
And the eye arose having not arisen,
Then crowns, mats, cloth and the like
Would come forth from a lump of kneaded clay.
A mat is not present in cloth,
Nor is cloth present in reeds.
If one thing arises from one alone,
Why would anything not arise from conditions as well?

'That life and that body,'
And the arising of what had not arisen at all:
All these are what others assert,
And I too have explained them thoroughly.
The opponent's position is to be stated,
And their understanding is to be turned back;
Having turned their understanding back,
I explain my own position.
Thinking, 'My disciples, a deluded crowd,
May come to dwell in positions of existence and nonexistence,'
I have therefore stated
The assertions of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas).

Kapila too, of evil understanding,
Teaches his disciples
That beings are born from the Principal (pradhāna)
And from the transformation of the qualities.

It is neither arisen through conditions nor unarisen.
Conditions do not exist,
And because conditions have no entity,
What has not arisen does not arise.

Free of the positions of existence and nonexistence,
Abandoning causes and conditions,
Without arising and without perishing,
Abandoning the basis of characteristics: this is my own position.

When one constantly sees the world,
Which is like an illusion or a dream,
Free of causes and conditions,
As causeless, conceptual thought does not engage.

When one sees becoming as causeless,
Like a city of gandharvas, like a mirage,
Ever like a net of hairs,
Free of the positions of existence and nonexistence,
Abandoned by cause and condition,
The cause of mind is purified."

"If there are no things,
And mere mind too does not exist,
How could there be mind without things?
It is not tenable even as mind only.
Taking things as their object,
People's minds come to arise.
How could there be mind without a cause?
It is not tenable as mind only."

"In the way things are for the noble ones,
Suchness and mind only exist.
Those who say they do not exist
Are unskilled in my way.
If mind engages in things
As apprehended and apprehender,
That is the mind of the world;
It is not tenable as mind only.
If body, possessions and abode
Arise like a dream,
Mind would become twofold;
But mind has no twofold characteristic.
Just as a sword does not cut its own edge,
And just as a finger
Does not touch its own tip,
So it is with mind viewing itself.
There is no other and no dependence,
And there is no imagined thing.
The five dharmas and the two minds as well,
Being without appearance, do not exist.
What is to be produced and what produces:
The characteristic of things is of these two kinds.
Having the producer in mind,
I taught the absence of intrinsic nature."

If conceptual thought arose
In the shape of objects of manifold aspects,
Then even a rabbit's horn and the sky
Would come to appear as objects.

If there were an object not imagined,
The mind too would appear as an object.
An object imagined
As distinct from mind is never observed at all.

In saṃsāra, which has no beginning,
Objects exist nowhere at all.
If the mind had not been nourished,
How could it come to appear as objects?

If it were nourished by what does not exist,
A rabbit's horn would become so as well.
Conceptual thought nourished
By a nonexistent thing does not arise.

Just as it does not exist now,
So too it did not exist before.
Where there is no object,
How could a mind bound to objects come about?

Suchness, emptiness, the ultimate limit,
Nirvāṇa and the dharmadhātu,
And the nonarising of phenomena:
These are the nature of the ultimate.

Because they conceive of causes and conditions,
And do not fully understand
That existence is causeless and has not arisen,
The childish fall into existence and nonexistence.

"Mind becomes manifest where there is no appearance,
And distinctions arise from a beginningless cause.
If there have been no objects since beginningless time,
By what do distinctions arise?

If it were nourished by what does not exist,
Then even the poor would become wealthy.
How does mind arise without objects?
May the Sage please explain this to me."

"All of these are without cause;
There is no mind and no sphere of activity.
Mind too is not nourished;
The three realms of existence are free of an agent.

In order to refute arising,
Nonarising is established.
The teaching of causelessness
The childish do not understand.

All of these are unarisen,
Yet they are not nonexistent things.
Like dreams, illusions and cities of the gandharvas,
There are things that have no cause."

"Why have you spoken to me
Of the unarisen, of the absence of intrinsic nature,
And of emptiness as well?"

"When things do not appear
Apart from combinations,
Then they are unarisen and empty,
And I have explained them as without intrinsic nature.

Dreams, floating hairs, illusions,
Cities of the gandharvas and mirages
Appear even without a cause;
The diverse world is just like this.

Likewise, in each single combination,
Since there is no appearance, it does not exist.
Combinations are not transformed by destruction
As in the view of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas).

Disputing through the doctrine of causelessness,
Nonarising too is established.
When nonarising is established,
My way is not destroyed.

When the doctrine of causelessness is taught,
Fear arises in the tīrthikas, who say,
'How, from what, by what,
And where does causeless arising occur?'

When conditioned things are seen
Not as from a cause but as causeless,
Then the view that speaks
Of their arising and perishing is turned back."

"Is nonarising the nonexistence of things?
Or is it dependence on conditions?
Or is it a name for things?
Or please tell me, is it something without meaning?"

"Nonarising is not the nonexistence of things,
Nor is it dependence on conditions.
Nor is it a name for things,
Nor is it a name without meaning.

That which is not the domain
Of śrāvakas, of pratyekabuddhas,
Of those who have reached the seventh ground,
Or of tīrthikas: that is the mark of nonarising.

The turning back of causes and conditions,
The negation of agents,
And the establishing of everything as mind only:
These I have explained as nonarising.

Things not arising from causes,
The abandoning of conceiving and conceived,
Freedom from the positions of existence and nonexistence:
This I have explained as nonarising.

Mind free of appearances,
Having abandoned the two natures as well,
The transformation of the bases:
This I have explained as nonarising.

External things are neither things nor nonthings,
Nor is mind grasped at all;
All views are utterly abandoned:
That is the mark of nonarising.

Likewise emptiness and absence of intrinsic nature:
All the words gathered from these should be examined.
It is never empty through emptiness;
It is the emptiness of nonarising.

From the combination of kinds of conditions
Things come forth and cease.
Apart from the combination
There is no arising and no ceasing either.

In terms of oneness and difference,
As the tīrthikas imagine,
Things are not distinct,
And apart from combination they exist nowhere at all.

Existent and nonexistent things do not arise;
Neither nonexistent nor existent, they are nowhere.
Therefore it is otherwise: it is the combination
That comes forth and ceases.

This is mere convention;
It is a linkage of mutual dependence.
Apart from the linkage of conditions
There is nothing to be produced.

Since there is no producing, there is no arising,
Free of the faults of the tīrthikas.
I have explained it as mere linkage;
The childish do not understand.

To say that somewhere, apart from linkage,
There is a production of things
Destroys linkage;
It should be known as the doctrine of causelessness.

If somewhere, apart from linkage,
A thing existed for anyone,
Then substances, like a lamp,
Would illuminate the linkage.

Whatever phenomenon the unskilled imagine
Apart from the linkages
Is unarisen and without essential nature;
Its nature is like space.

The nature of reality attained by the noble ones
Is another nonarising;
The nonarising of that
Is the forbearance of nonarising.

When all these worlds
Are seen as linkage itself,
Thinking, 'This is mere linkage,'
Then the mind settles in equipoise.

Ignorance, craving, karma and the like
Are the inner linkages.
The potter's wheel, clay, rod, churning stick and the like,
Seeds, elements and so on, are the outer.

This is mere linkage.
Whoever holds that any entity
Arises from other conditions
Does not abide in reasoning or scripture.

If there is no thing to be produced,
From what condition does cognition arise?
Since these become conditions for one another,
Therefore they are known as conditions.

The properties of heat, moisture, solidity and motion
Are imagined by the childish.
They are not phenomena in a combination;
Therefore they have no essential nature.

Physicians, according to the patient,
Prescribe different treatments,
Yet their treatise contains no difference;
It differs only as divided by the illness.

Likewise I too, knowing the mental continua of beings,
Stained by the faults of afflictions and defects,
And knowing the strength of their faculties,
Teach the way to the childish.

Though afflictions and faculties differ,
My teaching is not divided by that.
The peaceful path endowed with eight limbs
Is a single vehicle.

Pot, cloth, diadem and horn;
The rabbit's horn is a cause of nonexistence.
What arises from a collection of causes
You should not take to be nonexistent.

By reason of existence there is nonexistence;
Without it, nonexistence could not be nonexistent.
Existence arises from its counterpart, nonexistence;
Each is the cause of its counterpart."

On what does it take its stand,
and who conceives of what as appearing?
What is it that depends on something?
It is causeless, and yet not without cause.

If it depends on something else,
and that in turn appears in something else,
there would be an endless regress,
and whatever there is would become nothing at all.

Just as an illusion appears
in dependence on leaves, wood and the like,
so, in dependence on things,
variety appears to people.

The net of illusion is not leaves,
it is not pebbles, it is not wood.
Fools see the illusion;
the illusionist sees its basis.

Likewise, being based on things,
what difference could there be?
When it appears, there being no duality,
in what, and how, could one examine it?

In conceptual thought there is nothing conceived,
and conceptual thought too does not exist.
Since there is nothing in conceptual thought,
there is no nirvāṇa and no saṃsāra.

Since there is no conceptual thought,
conceptual thought does not operate.
If it does not operate, how could there be mind?
Mind-only would not be tenable.

Because intentions are many and diverse,
the teaching has no core;
since it has no core, there is no liberation,
and there is no world of various kinds.

There is no external appearance
of the kind that fools imagine.
Mind appears like a reflection,
stirred up by habitual tendencies.

Since existence and nonexistence have not arisen,
all things are unborn.
All of these are mind-only,
utterly free of conceptions.
Fools speak of things on account of conditions;
the wise do not.

Intrinsic nature is free of mind,
and mind proceeds to the noble ones.
Sāṃkhyas, naked ascetics, Vaiśeṣikas,
and likewise brahmins and Pāśupatas
fall into views of existence and nonexistence,
bereft of the meaning of the isolated.

Absence of intrinsic nature, the unborn,
emptiness, illusion, the stainless:
the buddhas and you as well,
for whose sake have you taught these?

For yogins who seek the mind,
who are free of views and speculation,
the buddhas have taught yoga,
and I too have fully taught it.

If all this is mind,
on what does the world rest?
Why do people appear
coming and going on the earth?

Just as birds in the sky,
moved about by conception,
without abode and without support,
move there as if upon the ground,

so all embodied beings as well,
moved about by conceptual thought,
roam within their own mind
like birds in the sky.

It is taught that mind operates,
appearing as body, enjoyments and abode.
How and why are there appearance and operation?
Tell me about mind-only.

Body, enjoyments and abodes,
appearance and operation, and habitual tendencies:
through conceptualizing appearance and operation
these arise for those who do not exert themselves.

The object is the thing imagined;
mind arises from the object.
By fully understanding the mind that appears,
conceptual thought does not operate.

When one sees the conditioned
as imputed names, as free of names,
and as devoid of intellect and the knowable,
at that time one is liberated.

That it is the imposing of name upon name:
just this is what is to be known.
Those who know it otherwise
do not know; they are not knowers.

Within the five dharmas, the natures,
the eight consciousnesses,
and the two kinds of selflessness,
the whole of the Great Vehicle is contained.

When beings see
that it is void of intellect and the knowable,
then conceiving name upon name
does not come into play.

Because mind is seen, activity
and the conceiving of letters turn back.
Because one's own mind is not seen,
conceptual thought fully operates.

Like the formless aggregates,
there is no counting among them.
The elements are distinct, and form is a form of characteristics;
how then do forms become many?
Because characteristics are relinquished,
it is not derived from the elements, nor is it the elements.

If form were a characteristic other than these,
why would it not arise through the aggregates?
Free of aggregates and sense sources,
when one sees the absence of characteristics,
because one sees that phenomena lack a self,
then mind turns back.

Because objects and faculties differ,
consciousness arises as eightfold.
In terms of characteristics it is threefold,
and where there is no appearance it turns back.

The universal ground is the self of the mind,
and consciousness itself is what is "mine."
Through dualistic grasping it fully operates;
when it is thoroughly understood, it turns back.

When one sees, free of one and many,
that there is no movement,
then self and what is "mine"
are no longer conceived as two.

What has not entered does not increase,
nor is it the cause of consciousness.
What has ceased, being free of cause and effect,
does not enter again.

Why is it that the worlds
are conceptual thought, mind-only? Tell me.

Free of causes and connections,
free of characteristics and what they characterize,
one's own mind appears in variety
and is imagined as though it were seen.

Knowing the appearances of mind,
and because objects other than mind are grasped,
when the intellect does not see,
one falls into the view of nonexistence;
since one is grasping it as mind,
how would that not ever come about for one?

Conceptual thought is neither a thing nor a non-thing;
therefore existence is not born.
Because the appearances of mind are fully understood,
conceptual thought does not operate.

The non-operation of conceptual thought
is the transformation that is without basis.

Refuting the four alternatives:
if things possessed causes,
that would merely make a different distinction of names;
it would not be proven.

Because causes and conditions come together,
And because a cause has been ruled out,
These things are inferred from their meaning;
Otherwise they would arise from a cause.

If the conditions are impermanent,
The fault of permanence is turned back.
Because, for the childish, things are impermanent,
There is no arising and no destruction.

Whatever is in the very act of perishing
Does not appear as a cause.
How, and through what, could what is unseen be one?
From the impermanent, becoming does not arise.

Beings are trained through the means of gathering;
They are brought under control through moral discipline;
Through wisdom, views are destroyed;
Through liberation they are greatly increased.

All this is Lokāyata;
Whatever the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) teach is false.
Because they view cause and effect as existent,
They have no established tenet of their own.

One such as I alone has a tenet of his own,
Which has utterly abandoned cause and effect
And is completely free of Lokāyata;
I shall teach it to the assembly of disciples.

It is mind only, and nothing appears.
Having abandoned permanence and annihilation,
As the things that are apprehended and the apprehender
Mind appears in two ways.

As long as the mind is active,
So long is there Lokāyata.
When the world is seen as one's own mind,
Conceptual thought does not occur.

Coming together is the result, the aim accomplished;
Exhaustion is the result unseen.
When coming together and exhaustion are fully understood,
Conceptual thought does not occur.

Permanent and impermanent, made
And unmade, other and not other,
All of these and the like
Are the way of Lokāyata.

Gods, asuras, and humans,
Animals, hungry ghosts, and the realm of Yama (the Lord of Death):
These, where embodied beings are born,
Are taught as the six destinies.

Through superior, middling, and inferior karma
One is born among them.
If one guards all that is virtuous,
One will, above all, be liberated.

"To the assemblies of monks you have taught
That in each and every instant
There is life, birth, and death.
Please explain to me the intent of this."

"Just as from one form there comes another form,
So mind, too, comes together and then perishes.
Therefore to the disciples
I teach birth from one instant to the next.

Form, and the conceptualizing of form,
Arise and operate in the same way.
Conceptual thought is birth;
There is nothing other than conceptual thought.

What I have taught as instant by instant
Was taught with reference to conditions.
When grasping at form has been abandoned,
There is no birth and no destruction.

Conditions and what is born from conditions,
Ignorance, suchness, and the rest,
Operate by way of two factors:
Two persons, and likewise two minds."

"If phenomena that are conditions
And those born from conditions are not distinct,
Permanence and the rest would become results,
And conditions, too, would become causes.

Because cause and effect are upheld,
The words of you and of the buddhas
Would be no different from the non-Buddhists';
Therefore, great Sage, they would not be noble."

"Within a body just a fathom long
The world, the origin of the world,
And the path leading to cessation
I have fully taught to the Victor's children.

Grasping at the three natures,
Ordinary beings, with views of apprehended and apprehender,
Conceptualize phenomena,
Both mundane and supramundane.

Therefore, having first examined,
One should speak of the natures;
But in order to turn back views
One does not imagine a nature.

Because of the fault of deterioration there is no vow,
And mind, too, does not become active.
It becomes active because of dualistic grasping;
Suchness is nondual.

From ignorance and craving
Consciousness and the rest have not been born;
Nor are they born from what is endless, from what is made,
Or from the making of becoming.

The destruction of things
Is explained by the unskilled in four ways.
Because twofold conceptual thought is at work;
Yet there is neither existence of things nor their nonexistence.

When one has seen what is free of the four extremes,
In which both views have been abandoned,
The operation of twofold conceptual thought
Does not manifestly occur.

Because the intellect rises up
Toward things that are unborn,
And toward things that are born it is the same,
One does not imagine them."

"How self and others
May never be mixed with existence and nonexistence:
The reasoning that turns back both views,
O Protector, tell me this."

"Unmixed with the doctrines of the non-Buddhists,
Leaving pratyekabuddhas and śrāvakas behind,
So that the Victor's children may not perish,
Is the Victor's practice of realization.

Liberation, the caused and the causeless as well,
Are of one characteristic in being unborn.
Through other designations these deceive;
The wise always abandon them.

Like clouds, like banks of cloud, like rainbows,
Like mirages, tangled hairs, and illusions,
All things arise from one's own conceptions;
The non-Buddhist world imagines them through causes of their own.

The unborn, and suchness,
The limit of reality, and emptiness:
These are names for form;
One does not imagine them as nonexistent.

Like 'hand' and 'doer' in the world,
Like Indra, Śakra, and Purandara,
So, too, the intrinsic nature of all things
Is not to be imagined.

Emptiness is not other than form,
And the unborn is likewise.
Because they are not other, they are not to be imagined;
Doing so leads fully into the fault of views.

Because the characteristics of things are grasped,
There is imagining and conceptual thought;
When thorough imagining is grasped,
There is long, short, round, and the rest.

Mind is imagining;
Mentation is thorough imagining;
Mental consciousness, conceptual thought,
Has abandoned marks and the basis of marks.

Whoever looks on the non-Buddhists' unborn
And on my own way alike,
His conception makes no distinction
And leads fully into the fault of views.

Intent, the unborn,
And the very meaning of the unborn:
Whoever knows these, knowing the reasoning,
They know my way.

The intent that has a purpose in view,
And the unborn, which has no basis:
The full understanding of these two meanings
I have taught as the unborn."

"Do unborn things exist,
Or is it not so? Tell me, great Sage.
Is it unlike the doctrine of no cause,
Or would it be mixed with nonexistence and with those doctrines?"

"The doctrine of no cause and the unborn
Are not the same; that is the view of the non-Buddhists.
Because it is free of existence and nonexistence,
I have taught it as mind only."

Arising and non-arising,
which spring from the causes of views, abandon them!
Non-arising belongs to those who teach that there is no cause,
and arising becomes a ground for causes.
In what is spontaneously accomplished there is no activity;
if there were activity, it would be mixed with views.
Through skillful means, aspiration, and the rest,
you have spoken to me of the view itself.
Since all phenomena are nonexistent,
how then does a maṇḍala arise?
Because it is free of grasped and grasper,
there is no engaging and no turning back.
From one thing another thing is seen,
and mind, too, arises from that.
The non-arising of phenomena:
how have you taught this to me?
If they do not know that all is mind only,
it is for that reason that this is taught.
In all that the great Sage has taught,
what comes earlier and what comes later conflict.
Free of the views of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas),
rejecting unequal causes and the absence of cause,
engaging and turning back:
the best of speakers has taught these to me.
Free of existence and nonexistence,
not destroying cause and result,
the linking of the sequence of the grounds:
the Lotus-Eyed One has taught this to me.
The world, fallen into the two extremes,
is thrown into turmoil by views;
because of arising, non-arising, and the rest,
it does not realize the cause of peace.
I have no maṇḍala whatsoever;
I do not expound the nature of reality.
If there were two, it would be a fault;
the buddhas have purified duality.
Things are empty and momentary,
without intrinsic nature and unarisen.
Proponents of wrong views, obscured, imagine them otherwise;
the Tathāgatas do not.
How, and in what ways,
do objects become directly perceived,
and how does conceptual thought engage
and turn back? Please explain this to me.
Because it is endowed with vivid color,
seeing form gathered up by proliferations,
conceptual thought engages
even toward things outside.
By fully knowing that very thing
and seeing the object exactly as it is,
the mind in accord with the noble lineage
does not engage.
Apart from the elements,
there is no arising of things.
The mind, which seems ever to be arising,
contemplate as unborn.
Do not conceptualize conceptual thought;
the wise are free of conceptual thought.
If one makes conceptual thought an object of examination,
it is not nonduality, it is not nirvāṇa.
The way of asserting non-arising
appears like an illusion;
an illusion arises without a cause,
and so the tenet is overstepped and lost.
The mind, habituated since beginningless time,
appears like a reflection;
it is like an object, yet it is not that there is no object.
Contemplate it correctly, just as it is.
Just as a form in a mirror
appears, free of oneness and difference,
and yet is not nonexistent,
so is the characteristic of arising.
Just as gandharva cities, illusions, and the like
depend on causes and conditions,
so too is the arising of all things;
it is not that they do not arise.
Through being imagined as self and as phenomena,
conceptual thought, like a person,
engages in two ways of engaging;
the childish do not realize this.
The incomplete, those under the power of conditions,
likewise śrāvaka arhats,
the Victors dependent on their own power, and the dependent:
these are taught as the five śrāvakas.
Perishing at another time,
the ultimate, the mutual,
and impermanence: these four kinds
the unskilled, childish imagine.
Through guṇas, atoms, and primal nature (prakṛti) as cause,
the childish, fallen into the two extremes,
holding to the positions of existence and nonexistence,
do not know the means of liberation.
Just as a child takes the fingertip
to be the moon, and nothing else,
so those attached to the letters
do not realize my suchness.
The elements, discordant in their characteristics,
give rise to material things;
that is the abiding of the elements.
What derives from the elements is not made by the elements.
Fire burns form;
the water element has the nature of an emanation;
wind scatters form.
How then could things arise from the elements?
Consciousness and the aggregate of form:
these are two, not five.
The different enumerations of the aggregates
I have also explained in a hundred ways.
The distinctions of mind and mental factors
are an engaging that fully engages,
mingled with the aspect of form;
the form of mind does not derive from the elements.
Blue and the rest are white by their counterpart;
by the counterpart of white and the rest, blue.
Cause and result, production,
existence and nonexistence, emptiness,
the proof, the proven, and what is to be proven,
cold and hot, the mark and its basis,
all these and others like them
the logicians have not established.
Mind, mentation, and the six consciousnesses,
these are accompanied by self;
free of oneness and difference,
the ground of all (ālaya) engages.
Sāṃkhyas, the Naked Ones, Vaiśeṣikas,
and logicians who speak of Īśvara
fall into the positions of existence and nonexistence;
they are excluded from the meaning of solitude.
The distinctions of shape and figure
are born from the elements, but are not elements.
Non-Buddhists say that what derives from the elements
and the elements themselves both arise.
Other non-Buddhists, apart from these,
imagine that they are not born from causes.
Those who rely on the positions of existence and nonexistence,
through delusion, do not comprehend.
Associated together with mind,
yet free of mentation and the rest,
the being whose characteristic is pure
abides together with wisdom.
Whatever form and karma there are,
aggregates arisen through objects as cause,
and beings, too, without appropriation,
do not abide where there is no form.
Because there would be no sentient beings,
those who speak of beings would have no self;
because consciousness, too, would not arise,
those who teach no-self would fall into annihilation.
Its four stations,
how could they exist without form?
Because there are no outer or inner things,
consciousness does not engage.
Just as the logicians accept
aggregates of the intermediate state,
so too, for one born in the formless realm,
why would there be no form of existence?
If beings had no consciousness,
they would be liberated without striving.
Without doubt this is the claim of the non-Buddhists;
the logicians do not understand.
There, form too exists;
where there is no form there is no view;
since that is absent, there is no tenet,
there is no vehicle and no destiny.
Consciousness, arisen from habitual tendencies,
endowed with the sense faculties:
of its eight kinds, one part
does not grasp at the time of the instant.

Just as form does not come into play,
so neither do the faculties and their powers.
Therefore the Blessed One, too,
taught that the faculties and the rest are momentary.
If form has not become an object of conception,
how could it enter consciousness?
And if it has not entered consciousness,
how would saṃsāra come to be born?
That it comes into play the very moment it arises
is not what the Guide teaches.
The conceptualizing of things
stirs in beings without interruption.
The faculties and the objects of the faculties
belong not to the wise but to the foolish.
The childish grasp by way of names;
the noble, through names, are skilled in meaning.
In the six there is no appropriation;
what has appropriation is not taken up.
Free from the fault of existence,
the noble ones speak without drawing distinctions.
Logicians who have abandoned knowledge,
frightened by permanence and annihilation,
childish as they are, draw no distinction
regarding a self in the conditioned and the unconditioned.
In what is one there is giving;
in what is distinct, it does not exist either.
It is one together with mind,
and distinct from mentation and the rest.
When the giving proclaimed of mind and mental factors
is separated from appropriation,
how then is one
to understand it as one?
The object of what has appropriation,
by way of karma, life, agent and the like,
through modes of the similar and dissimilar,
they establish, though it does not exist.
Just as fire burns all at once
both the fuel and the burning,
so too the self with its appropriation:
why do the logicians not teach it?
Whether ever arisen or unarisen,
if mind is utterly luminous,
why do the logicians not make use
of the examples that establish a self?
Logicians who have abandoned the method,
deluded in the thicket of consciousness,
in their wish to speak of a self,
rush this way and that.
To be realized by individual self-awareness,
the self has the characteristic of purity.
That buddha-nature, the tathāgatagarbha,
is not the domain of logicians.
If one comes to know the distinction
between what is appropriated and what appropriates,
and the characteristics of the aggregates,
the wisdom of the method will arise.
What the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) speak of
they conceive as something like an essence of the all-ground;
but the phenomena accompanying the self
they have not expounded.
When these are thoroughly distinguished,
when there is liberation and the seeing of truth,
the mind that purifies even the afflictions
to be abandoned by meditation and by seeing
is luminous by nature;
the tathāgatagarbha is virtuous.
The appropriation of sentient beings
is free of the finite and the infinite.
Just as, through cleansing, the color of gold,
gold ore and sugar
are seen, so likewise
is the sentient being in the aggregates.
There is no person and there are no aggregates;
the buddhas, whose wisdom is undefiled,
have meditated on what is ever peaceful;
in them I take refuge.
That the luminous nature of mind
is accompanied by the afflictions of mentation and the rest,
and by the self,
the supreme of speakers has taught.
Mind is luminous by nature;
what is other than it is beginningless.
The karma accumulated by these,
through it, the two become afflicted.
Because it is joined with beginningless,
adventitious afflictions,
the naturally luminous self is afflicted;
like cloth, it becomes pure.
Just as cloth, or likewise gold,
being stainless, is as though rid of faults,
endures and does not perish,
so is the faultless self.
In a drum, a conch or a lute,
some who are unskilled search
for the perfection of its sweet sound;
so it is with the person in the aggregates.
Treasures and jewels in the earth,
and likewise waters too,
though present, are not seen;
so it is with the person in the aggregates.
Mind and the host of mental factors,
and the qualities of the self, joined with the aggregates,
are not known by the unskilled;
so it is with the person in the aggregates.
Just as the child in a pregnant woman,
though present, is not seen,
so the self in the aggregates
is not seen by those who lack understanding.
Just as the lethal power in a drug,
and just as the fire in the firewood,
are not seen by those who do not know the method,
so it is with the person in the aggregates.
Though impermanence and emptiness
are present in things,
the unskilled do not see them;
so it is with the person in the aggregates.
The grounds, the masteries, the higher knowledges,
the supreme consecration,
and the distinctive meditative absorptions:
if there were no self, these would not exist.
When a destroyer comes along
and says, "If it exists, show it!",
the wise should instruct him,
"Show me your own conceptual thought!"
Those who harm the Buddha's Dharma,
holding views on the side of existence and non-existence,
proponents of no-self, one should not converse with;
they should be excluded from the formal acts of the monks.
Burning down the forest of no-self,
freed from the faults of the tīrthikas,
like the blazing fire at the end of time,
this teaching of the self blazes forth.
Treacle, honey and sugar,
butter, sesame and the like,
though each has its own taste,
are not truly known unless tasted.
Though the self in the aggregates one sees
is sought in five ways,
the unskilled do not see it;
the wise, seeing it, are liberated.
Through examples such as spells
mind cannot be understood;
in what, for what reason and for what purpose
the composites exist is not understood.
Phenomena differ in their characteristics;
mind alone is not grasped.
Into causelessness and non-occurrence
the logicians fall in fault.
The yogin who follows and observes the mind
does not see mind by means of minds.
If the seeing arises from appearance,
from what cause does appearance arise?
I am of the Kātyāyana lineage,
come forth from the Pure Abodes;
I teach sentient beings the Dharma
that leads to the city of nirvāṇa.
This is the ancient path;
I and the tathāgatas
in three thousand sūtras
have thoroughly explained nirvāṇa.
In the desire realm and the formless realm
one does not become a fully awakened buddha;
in Akaniṣṭha of the form realm,
free of desire, you awaken.
The cause of death is not the object;
the cause is being bound to the object.
By wisdom the afflictions are slain;
this is the sword's edge of discipline.
How do selfless phenomena,
like illusions and the rest, come to exist or not exist?
To the childish they appear as real;
how could selflessness itself not exist?
Because of what is made and what is unmade,
a cause does not bring anything into operation.
All these are unborn;
the childish do not know it.
The causes are unborn;
those are made by conditions.

If those two are not produced,
how can they be imagined through causes?
As prior, as subsequent, and as simultaneous:
thus the dialecticians teach causes.
Through appearance, disciple, substance and the like,
they explain the arising of things.

Through marks produced by conditioning
a buddha is not one who bears the marks;
those are the qualities of a wheel-turning monarch,
and by them a buddha is not described.
The mark of a buddha is wisdom,
utterly free of the faults of views,
realized by the vision of individual self-awareness,
utterly destroying every fault.

For the deaf, the mute, the blind, the one-eyed,
the aged with wrinkled skin,
and children in particular,
there is no pure conduct.

By hidden, manifest, and divine
marks one is a wheel-turning monarch.
Some with manifest marks go forth;
the other two are heedless.

Vyāsa, Kaṇāda, Ṛṣabha,
Kapila, and the leader of the Śākyas:
after I have passed into nirvāṇa,
these and others will appear.
A hundred years after my nirvāṇa,
Vyāsa and likewise the Bhārata,
the Pāṇḍavas and the Kauravas;
after the Nandas, the Mauryas will appear.
After the Mauryas, the Nandas and the Guptas
come the basest of barbarian kings.
After the barbarians, turmoil with weapons;
after the weapons, the age of strife.
At the end of the age of strife
the world will not cultivate the true Dharma.

When these and others have passed,
beings revolve like a wheel.
Because fire and the suns come together,
the desire realm is burned up;
and later, where the world had stood,
it becomes a place of divine substance.
The four castes, the lords of men,
the sages, and the Dharma as well,
the Vedas, Dharma-giving, sacrifice,
and the forms of Dharma will come forth.

Through tales, protective rites and the like,
through bare texts, explanations and commentaries,
and through "Thus have I heard" and the rest,
the world will become utterly deluded.

Having made it well and cut it,
one should apply color over it.
With blue, with cow dung, with mud and the like,
the cloth should be thoroughly marked.
The body adorned with these robes,
the marks of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) abandoned,
the yogin expounds the teaching:
that is the banner of the Buddha.
One should drink water strained through a filter
and tie on a waist cord;
at the proper time, as is fitting,
one eats alms food, avoiding the low.

Divine substance comes from the heavens;
the other two come from humans.
Perfect with precious marks,
a lord of beings born as a god,
teaching the Dharma that benefits toward heaven,
he holds sway over the four continents.
Having ruled the continents for a very long time,
through craving he comes to ruin.

The age of the immature and the age when refuge arose,
likewise the twofold age and the age of strife:
I and others belong to the perfect age,
the Śākya Lion to the age of strife.
Siddhārtha, the son of the Śākyas,
Viṣṇu, Vyāsa, Īśvara,
and other non-Buddhists like them
will appear when I have passed into nirvāṇa.

"Thus have I heard" and the rest
are the discourse of the Śākya Lion;
tales and what happened in former times
will be held to be Vyāsa's.
Viṣṇu and Īśvara as well
will proclaim a creator.
From my nirvāṇa onward
such things as these will come about.

My mother is Vasumatī,
my father the brahmin Prajāpati;
I am of the Kātyāyana clan,
and my name is Viraja, the Victor.
My father, grandfather, and I myself
were born in the land of Campā;
his name is Somagupta,
and he was born of the lunar clan.
Having entered upon liberation and practiced austerities,
having explained the ways in a thousand forms,
having empowered Mahāmati
and made the prophecy, I pass into nirvāṇa.

Mati will bestow the Dharma,
and through the Dharma will give it to Mekhala and some others;
because Mekhala's disciples are weak,
at the end of the eon it will perish.

Kāśyapa and Krakucchanda,
the pure guide Kanakamuni,
I, Viraja, and others:
all these are victors of the perfect age.
After them, in the perfect age,
a guide called Mati,
a teacher of the five knowables,
a great hero, will appear.
Not in the other two; no protector has arisen there,
and in the later age of strife too
no protector of the world will appear.
In the perfect age they attain complete awakening.

So that it is not stolen and can be recognized,
uncut, with its hem,
with patterns like the eyes of a peacock's tail
one should draw designs on the upper robe.
Between one eye and the next
make the measure two or three finger-widths.
If it is drawn otherwise,
even the immature will become attached.

Always quench the fire of attachment,
and bathe with the water of wisdom;
diligently, through yoga,
keeping the three junctures and the three restraints.

When arrows, stones, wood and the like
are set in motion by throwing and so on,
when one rises, another falls;
so it is with virtue and nonvirtue.

The single is not many;
because their characteristics differ, it is found nowhere.
All who receive are like the wind;
those who give are like a field.

If the single were to become many,
all would be unmade;
and if made, it would perish.
This is the way of the dialecticians.
It is like a lamp or a seed;
since they are alike, how could it become many?
"In such a way it becomes many":
that is the way of the dialecticians.

From sesame no beans will grow;
rice does not come from the cause of barley.
Wheat and the various kinds of grain:
how could the single become many?

Pāṇini the grammarian,
Akṣapāda, Bṛhaspati,
Brahmā, Garga,
and the founder of the Lokāyatas will appear.
Kātyāyana the composer of sūtras,
Yājñavalkya likewise,
Bhudhuka, and the astrologers
will appear in the age of strife.

For the world that has made merit,
Bali, the glory of creatures, will appear,
the protector of all dharmas,
King Bali, the giver of the earth.

Vālmīki and Masurākṣa,
Kauṭilya and Āśvalāyana,
and Ṛṣabha too, of great and pure glory,
will appear in times to come.
Siddhārtha, the son of the Śākyas,
Bhūtānta and Pañcaśikha,
Vāgbali and Medhāvin
will appear in a later time.

When I dwell in the forest,
Brahmā will give me
a staff, a skin,
a girdle and a water pot.
A great one endowed with yoga,
the sage called the Stainless,
will become a teacher who expounds liberation.
Saying, "This is the banner of the Sage,"
Brahmā, with hundreds of Brahmās
and with many gods,
will cast it down to me from the lofty sky,
and then that lord himself will vanish.
Indra together with the gods,
Virūḍhaka and the others,
will offer me in my forest dwelling
various robes and all the alms bowls.

What the proponents of non-arising hold to be a cause,
whether it has arisen or is yet to arise,
they establish as the unarisen;
it is spoken of as mere words.

The cause that makes that mind arise
is ignorance.
As long as forms are not known,
for that long does it remain?
The very instant it has perished,
other minds arise.
If form does not endure in time,
on what object do they focus as they arise?
The mind that passes from one thing to another
arises from a mistaken cause.
If that is not established at all,
how is it known to perish in an instant?

The meditative absorption of yogis,
gold, the relics of the Victor,
and the celestial palaces of the Ābhāsvara abode
are not destroyed by worldly causes.
The abidings and the dharmas attained,
the perfect wisdom of the buddhas,
monkhood and the attainments of knowledge:
how can these be seen as momentary?
How are the forms of illusions,
of cities of gandharvas and the like, momentary?
The elements do not become elements;
in beings they hold no elements.

Mind arises from the cause of ignorance
and has been accumulated since beginningless time.
The logicians imagine
that in arising it perishes and is wholly connected.

The doctrine of the Sāṃkhyas is twofold:
transformations come from the principal (pradhāna);
when the principal exists, the result exists;
they establish the result as its very self.
In the principal and in the operation of entities
they proclaim the distinctions of the qualities.
The variety of causes and results
is absent in transformation.

Just as pure mercury
is not tainted by stains,
so too the abode of the ālaya
of all embodied beings is pure.
The smell of asafoetida and of garlic,
what is seen in the child of a pregnant woman,
the taste in salt and the like:
why do they not become like seeds?

The same, different,
likewise both, and neither:
there is no cause for existence;
in the conditioned it does not exist, yet it is not nonexistent.
The self exists just as the horse does,
the aggregates being devoid of the nature of the cow.
As for the conditioned and the unconditioned,
their nature is inexpressible and unutterable.

Seeing wrongly in terms of scripture and reasoning,
stained by the views of logic,
without analysis they speak of a self.
It is not without cause, and it does not exist elsewhere.
Theirs is an accumulation of faults;
one does not contemplate the aggregates as a self.
Identity and difference from them
the logicians do not understand.
Just as a reflection appears
in mirrors, in water and in eyes,
free of being one or different,
so is the person in relation to the aggregates.

Meditation, thorough meditation, and the meditator;
the path, the truths and seeing:
when these three are thoroughly cultivated,
one is liberated from wrong views.
Like lightning that perishes as soon as it is seen,
like the wheel in the opening of a house,
so too, regarding the changes of all phenomena,
do not conceptualize as fools do.

Nirvāṇa through the absence of entities
bewilders the minds of fools.
Because there is seeing things just as they are,
and the seeing of the noble ones,
one should cultivate a transformation
free of arising and perishing,
rid of the existence and nonexistence of entities,
free of characteristic and the characterized.
Free of the talk of non-Buddhists,
having abandoned name and shape,
cultivate the transformation
of the bases of inner views.

Through contact and torment
come the bodies of gods and of hell beings;
for them there is no intermediate existence;
they are produced by consciousness.
Those who arise in the intermediate existence,
born of womb, egg, warmth, moisture and so forth,
like the many various beings,
contemplate as going and as destinies.

The talk of the views of non-Buddhists,
devoid of scripture and reasoning,
which brings the side of affliction and decline,
the wise do not engage in.

First, from appropriation,
one discerns distinctions by analyzing the self.
To discern distinctions without analysis
is to discern the features of a barren woman's son.

Beings seen through miraculous power
are beyond the fleshly eye and wisdom;
the form of all embodied beings
is abandoned in the aggregate of formations.
Turning good and bad in color,
distinguished as liberated and not liberated,
one clearly sees the abiding of formations
that belong to the formations of divine substance.
Those with form at the junctures of destinies
are not the domain of logicians.
I have passed beyond the domain of humans,
but the other, base logicians have not.

If there is no self and yet mind arises,
from what does it arise?
Like a river, a lamp or a seed:
why is its arising not explained thus?
When consciousness has not arisen,
there is no ignorance and so forth.
Without that, how could consciousness
come to arise as a continuum?

The three times, the timeless,
and the inexpressible: these five kinds
the logicians declare
to be what is called the Buddha.

Knowledge that arises with formations as its cause
is not expressed by formations;
knowledge accomplished by formations
is held to be included among formations.
"Because this exists, this arises":
this teaches, making it clear,
that conditions have no cause.
Since that is absent, there is no agent.

Wind moves fire and clouds,
but it does not produce them.
If, once moved, they are stilled by it,
how are beings brought about?
The conditioned and the unconditioned
are said to be free of appropriation.
How then do fools imagine
that fire is produced in it?
If for people fire arises
through mutual strength and power,
then since beings are to be examined like fire,
by whom have they been brought forth?

The cause of the aggregates and the assemblages of sense sources
is mentation and the rest.
Without a self, always like a house of many guests,
it operates together with mind.
These two are always luminous,
free of cause and result.

They are not established as fire is established;
The dialecticians do not understand.
Mind and sentient beings are nirvāṇa,
Luminous by their very nature;
Through the faults of mentation and the rest,
The afflictions, like space, are not different.
Like the elephant's bed and the rest, it existed before;
It is stained by the views of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas),
Veiled by the mental consciousness,
And refined by fire and the like.
By that one sees correctly, just as it is,
And having seen, one destroys the afflictions too.
Fully abandoning the examples of what is dense,
These are the domain of the noble ones.
By distinguishing knowing and the knowable,
Those two are not to be imagined as other.
The foolish do not understand this,
And speak about what cannot be expressed.
Just as children take a drum made of sandalwood
To be something else,
So bad dialecticians take
The knowledge that is like sandalwood and aloeswood.

Having risen, one does not take food afterward.
With bowl, dwelling and felt mat,
Free of faults such as a changing face,
One should partake of food in purity.

Whoever weighs this way with reasoning,
Faithful, a diligent yogin free of conceptual thought,
Who has come to rely on the meaning of non-abiding,
Should practice the modes of the Dharma as one treats gold.

The fool conceives conditions as possible or impossible;
That is the net of bad views, together with its stains.
Turning away from attachment, aversion and delusion,
The one without attachment is empowered by the Buddha's hand.

The non-Buddhists are corrupted by the position of a cause;
Others again are confused by conditions;
Others, since things arise without a cause,
Hold to the meaningless, which is annihilation.

Mentation, consciousness,
And the transformation of maturation:
Mentation arises from the ground of all,
Consciousness arises from mentation.
From the ground of all, every mind
Arises like waves.
All arise from the cause of habitual tendencies,
And arise in accordance with conditions.
Distinct moments are linked by connection;
One's own mind grasps at objects.
The characteristic of shape, completely pure,
Arises from mentation, the eye and the rest.
Linked together from beginningless time,
The appearance of objects arises from habitual tendencies.
Whatever appears is the appearance of mind.
Reversing the views of the non-Buddhists,
Arising from its cause, and otherness too,
Taking that as their object, come into operation.
From that, views and cognitions
Enter fully into saṃsāra.

Things are like dreams and illusions,
Like the cities of the gandharvas,
Like a mirage and the moon in water;
Meditate on your own conceptuality.
In the mode of engagement is suchness;
Correct wisdom is its basis.
The illusion-like samādhi, the Heroic Progress and the rest,
Other meditative absorptions,
The superknowledges and the powers,
The illusion-like wisdom,
And empowerment by the Tathāgata
Are attained on entering the grounds.
When one sees beings as conceptuality,
Then the mind turns back,
And one attains the Joyous ground,
The ground of a buddha, and the grounds.
Because the basis has been transformed,
Like a jewel of many colors,
One acts for the benefit of sentient beings,
Just like a reflection in water.
Free from the positions of existence and nonexistence,
Free from duality and nonduality,
When one has passed beyond the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas,
One comes to the seven grounds.
By seeing one's own individual Dharma,
The true ground is purified,
And the Great Vehicle, which has abandoned
The outsiders, the non-Buddhists, shall be taught.
When one is fully transformed in conceptuality,
Death, transmigration and destruction are abandoned;
Like a jewel of hare's hair,
The way is also taught to the liberated.
Just as words rest upon words,
So too, if reasoning rests on reasoning;
Therefore it is the reasoning of reasoning.
Do not conceive of it otherwise!
The eye, karma, craving,
And ignorance likewise are improper;
Name and form and the minds,
And the turbid mind as well, are likewise.

The Great Vehicle sūtra called The Noble Descent into Laṅkā, as much of it as there is, is complete.

Gö Chödrup, a follower of the tradition of the Blessed One, translated it from a Chinese text and finalized it.

This concludes the translation. The following are translator's notes.

Rāvaṇa: the Tibetan name used here literally means "Screamer," the standard rendering of Rāvaṇa, who in the verses also names himself Daśagrīva. The line rendered "graced by Śuka and Sāraṇa" reads literally "adorned by parrot and sāra"; it is taken as Śuka (translated as "parrot") and Sāraṇa (transliterated), Rāvaṇa's ministers in the Rāmāyaṇa tradition. The line "not speaking of it openly to him" is obscure in the Tibetan and is translated literally. "Kapriyaṅgu" in the description of the ochre cloth is transliterated in the Tibetan and its exact sense is uncertain. In the list of the bodhisattvas' qualities, "masteries" renders the term otherwise used for empowerment, here in the sense of the bodhisattva masteries (vaśitā).  "He abode in the stirring of conceptuality" renders the Tibetan as it stands; the sense in context (after the transformation of the basis) would rather be abiding where conceptuality no longer operates, and the Sanskrit is commonly understood that way, so a negation may have been lost in transmission. "The three continua" is rendered literally; it is usually understood as the three continua of mind, mentation and consciousness, or as the defilement-, karma- and birth-continua. "The Dharma that is seen" means the Dharma realized in this very life.  "Thorough transformation" renders ཤིན་ཏུ་འགྱུར་བ, likely reflecting the Laṅkāvatāra's notion of the transformation of the basis (parāvṛtti). The phrase rendered "a miraculous power that has turned away from characteristics" is syntactically difficult in the Tibetan; the rendering follows the most natural reading.  The phrase rendered "You whose crown sways, ask me without delay" is uncertain; the Tibetan wording is terse and could also be read as a description of Rāvaṇa's manner. The "tathāgatas in their essential nature" contrasts with the "emanations conjured by magical power", corresponding to the Sanskrit distinction between the fundamental buddhas and their magically created emanations. The three grounds named are the eighth, ninth and tenth bodhisattva grounds.  "Differences of pleasant form, of speech and of movement" renders a compressed Tibetan phrase that literally reads "differences of form of pleasure, of expression, and of going"; the exact sense of the first member is uncertain. "Never attained a body" translates the Tibetan literally; it means that these things lack any substantial reality.  The phrase rendered "together with the bodhisattva Mati" could also be read as "together with bodhisattvas of intelligence"; the Tibetan is ambiguous. The term rendered "masteries" in the question on attainments is here taken as vaśitā, which fits the sūtra context better than the glossary's "empowerment". The syllables rendered "ever" in "Is ever like a dream" are read as an equivalent of "constantly", since the written form is unclear.  The verse lines beginning "Best of speakers, tell me!" and similar are the questioner's (Mahāmati's) requests; in the Buddha's reply the refrain བདག་ལ་གསུངས recurs, here rendered "this you asked me" as he restates the questions. ལྷ་ཆེན here is plainly an epithet of the Buddha, who hears and answers the questions, so it is rendered "the great god" without a gloss as Śiva. ཡ་མཚན་ཅན ("strange ones") is rendered "heterodox ones," following the sense of the corresponding Sanskrit list. རི་མོ ("drawings") may reflect a reading of Sanskrit citra where the original intended citta ("mind"). The phrase "king of transformation" and "king of the imageless" is translated literally and is obscure. ཤུ་རྡོག is an unidentified unit or numeral, given phonetically as "shudok." The weight units ri, nam, zho and sang are Tibetan measures standing in for Indian ones and are kept in phonetics. "Peppercorn" for འོལ་མ་སེ (Zanthoxylum) is approximate. "Flesh-eaters" (ཤ་ཟ) is taken here as the general class of meat-eaters rather than specifically piśācas.  "Statement" renders ཚིག (Skt. pada), the term for the hundred and eight propositions. The verse line on the lowly and Akaniṣṭha is terse in the Tibetan. It is rendered literally, and the sense is that buddhahood is attained in Akaniṣṭha rather than in the desire realm. The phrase rendered "the essence and the classes" (སྙིང་པོ་སྡེ་བ) is obscure, and the rendering is tentative.  "Characteristic of its own kind" renders rigs kyi mtshan nyid (Skt. jātilakṣaṇa), the consciousness's own original nature, as distinct from its functioning and its karmic aspect. The pair "the lowest and Akaniṣṭha" plays on Akaniṣṭha, the name of the highest heaven, which literally means "not the lowest". In the pair "gods and non-gods", ལྷ་མ་ཡིན་པ is taken in its literal negating sense to match the pattern of the list, rather than as "asuras".  "The domain where the sons of the buddhas pass beyond" renders the terse སྲས་ཀྱིས་ལས་འདའ་བའི་སྤྱོད་ཡུལ; the Sanskrit tradition reads it as the domain of progressing through the stages of the buddhas' sons (the bodhisattva grounds). The phrase "in which the conditions for conceptuality do not return" is a literal rendering of a compressed expression whose exact construal is uncertain.  "Bodhisattvas endowed with that activity": the Tibetan reads "endowed with arising and activity." The Sanskrit appears to say these bodhisattvas are free from the activity of arising. I have translated the Tibetan as it stands.  Several verses form a dialogue between Mahāmati and the Blessed One; the Tibetan marks no speakers, so quotation marks indicate the alternation (the humble verbs and "Great Sage, please explain" identify Mahāmati's questions). "Like a lame donkey" follows the Tibetan simile as it stands; it describes how the yogin leaves behind the characteristics of mind, wisdom and gnosis.  The final sentence follows the Tibetan, which says that "in them too there is no space." The Sanskrit is commonly read as "nor is space absent from them." Mahāmati's objection is likewise kept as a statement, as the Tibetan has it, although the Sanskrit frames it as a rhetorical question. "The natures" in Mahāmati's request refers to the three natures, here unnumbered.  The terms rendered "buddha of natural outflow" (niṣyandabuddha), "buddha who flows forth in accordance with the nature of reality" (dharmatāniṣyandabuddha) and "buddha of the nature of reality" (dharmatābuddha) follow the sūtra's distinctive classification of buddhas. Here རྒྱུད is rendered "continuum" (the mind-stream), not "tantra". The phrase མིར་ལྡན་པ, "together with a person who wields it", is compressed in the Tibetan and seems to mean the illusionist's human effort working with the spell. The sentence about the illusory person in the paragraph beginning "Furthermore" is syntactically dense; its rendering follows the Tibetan word order as closely as sense allows.  The text reads ནམ་བྱས་པའི་ནམ་མཁའ, which makes no sense; it is translated as མ་བྱས་པའི་ནམ་མཁའ, "unconditioned (unmade) space," the standard example paired with nirvāṇa and cessation. The phrase rendered "in which inconceivable habitual tendencies are transformed and which is free from death and transmigration" is grammatically ambiguous in Tibetan. It may also be read as "free from the death and transmigration that is the inconceivable transformation of habitual tendencies." That reading echoes the doctrine of "inconceivable transformational death." The translation follows the Tibetan word order.  The opening argument is compressed in the Tibetan; it is rendered closely as it stands, as a hypothetical reductio against the tīrthikas' inferred permanence. "The wisdom of individual self-awareness that has become the all-base" follows the Tibetan literally; the point is the transformed all-base consciousness, which the śrāvakas do not take as their basis.  'Dod chen pa is rendered "icchantika," the standard Sanskrit equivalent in this sūtra. In the sentence on the dependent nature, the Tibetan reads "abiding and non-apprehension" (gnas shing mi dmigs pa); the extant Sanskrit has "basis and object" (āśraya, ālambana), so mi dmigs is taken as a slip for dmigs and translated as "basis and object."  The "two natures" in the opening verse are the imputed and the dependent natures, contrasted with the perfected nature. In "devoid of mind, mentation and consciousness, of the five dharmas and of the natures," the natures are understood as the three natures (svabhāva). The heading "The Noble Descent into Laṅkā, the third fascicle" marks a fascicle division in the Tibetan text and has been kept. The sentence defining denial is terse in the Tibetan; it is rendered according to its most natural sense: when what was superimposed is not found, failure to discern leads to rejecting everything.  In the verse, the Tibetan "a body that is an assembly" and the word rendered "fashioned" are obscure; the Sanskrit of this verse is generally understood to speak of an emanation body free of activity and conditioning. The Tibetan is translated as it stands.  In the list of attributes the non-Buddhists ascribe to the self, the Tibetan as written reads "without permanence" (rtag pa med pa). This contradicts the argument, which turns on both doctrines calling their principle permanent, and the corresponding Sanskrit has "permanent." I have translated "permanent" as the evident reading.

The clause "this is because there is a cause of distinct characteristics" is rendered as the Tibetan stands. The Sanskrit is commonly understood with a negation here, but the Tibetan has none.  The term rendered "masteries" in the description of the mind-made body is the Tibetan word glossed elsewhere as empowerment; here it stands in the standard list of powers, masteries and superknowledges and corresponds to Sanskrit vaśitā, so "masteries" is used. The verse items are technical: "the Principal" is the Sāṃkhya pradhāna (primal matter), "the Lord" is Īśvara, and "the Creator" is the agent posited by other schools. The Tibetan does not give a separate explanation of the fourth quality; the account of the third quality runs directly into the aspiration toward the noble ones' personal wisdom, and this is translated as it stands.  In Mahāmati's first request the Tibetan has a double negative, literally "would not become non-conceiving of all things as arising gradually and all at once"; the sense required by context, and attested in the Sanskrit, is that they would not conceive of things as arising gradually or simultaneously, and it is translated accordingly. In the verse "Exist nowhere and at no time," the syllable written gag is read as gang/gar ("anywhere"), an evident copyist's slip. Tibetan ma red is read as ma rnyed, "not found." The six causes follow this text's own list, which differs from the Abhidharma list of six causes.  In the dream-speech clause, the Tibetan "the absence of the object of realization" is taken as "the absence of the object once one has awakened," with "realization" in its sense of waking. The animals in the simile are rendered as made of clay. The Tibetan as written has a word meaning "frightening." A similar-looking word meaning "clay" plainly fits the toy animals used to lure children, and that evident reading is translated here.  The unstated subject of "they will set up the components of the three means of valid cognition" is supplied from context as the non-Buddhists of the preceding passage. The list of graspings translates the standard Sanskrit series ātman, poṣa, puruṣa and pudgala: self, sustainer, cosmic man, person.  In the echo simile the Tibetan reads "name" (མིང), which makes no sense among the causes of an echo; it is read as "person" (མི), as the Sanskrit parallel also has. In the passage on the hundred and eight nonappearances, the phrase rendered "gradually establish" reads literally "establish the final ground"; it is taken as "gradually" in line with the Sanskrit krameṇa. "The three continua" translates རྒྱུད་གསུམ, here meaning streams of continuity, not tantras.  The line rendered "the divine hands of the buddhas" reads literally "the hands of the buddha-gods." The Sanskrit parallel speaks of the buddhas' hands consecrating the yogin's head. "Blessing" renders the Tibetan for adhiṣṭhāna, the empowering sustenance of the buddhas.  The phrase rendered "has not taught it in a manner of his own" is obscure in the Tibetan (it contains the word གླེན་པ, "foolish"); the rendering follows the general sense of the argument, in which Mahāmati objects that the Buddha's account of causation does not differ from that of the tīrthikas. The "distinguished ground" in Mahāmati's question refers to the tenth ground, Cloud of Dharma, where the empowerment of the hand is conferred.  The name of the world-system has been translated from the Tibetan as "Unblinking, Sweet Fragrance," since a Sanskrit form could not be confirmed with certainty. The word rendered "ordinary" translates a Tibetan term meaning something common or of little worth. The noble ones regard delusion only as such, not as an object of noble wisdom. The exact sense of the term in this sentence is somewhat uncertain. "Delusion" translates the Tibetan for error, Sanskrit bhrānti.  "Negative propensities" renders gnas ngan len (Skt. dauṣṭhulya), the latent dysfunction of body and mind. In the sentence beginning "Mahāmati, an illusion arises through the power of another person's spell," the Tibetan has no explicit subject before ni; the context and the parallel Sanskrit show that "illusion" (sgyu ma) is meant, and it has been supplied.  In the first verse, the third line reads in this Tibetan "like what comes from causes and the rest" (rgyu las sogs). The Sanskrit of the Laṅkāvatāra here reads "like an illusion and the rest" (māyādi). This suggests an original sgyu ma that became rgyu, but the line is translated as it stands.

The statement that elephants, horses and other animals that go upon the ground are called the "group of phrases" rests on a Sanskrit pun. The word pada means both "phrase" and "foot" or "footprint," so padakāya also means "a set of tracks."

Here, lung ston and lung bstan mean "to answer" a question, not "to prophesy." The four kinds of answer are, traditionally, the direct answer, the answer by distinguishing, the answer by counter-question, and setting the question aside.  The phrase rendered "has at most seven more lives in existence" reads པོ་ཟོས་ཀྱང་, which is obscure as written. The translation follows the evident sense, a stream-enterer of the lowest kind with up to seven more births, which is also the standard doctrine. Here འགྲོ་བ (gati) is rendered "course", in the sense of a path of progress, rather than "destiny".  In the final paragraph རྒྱུ is rendered "reasons" rather than "cause," since it stands alongside thesis and example in a list of the elements of logical argument (hetu as logical reason). The list of sensual acts (fist, palm, "joining the lower bodies") is obscure in the Tibetan and is rendered literally.  In the final paragraph, དབང་རྣམས is rendered "the masteries" (Skt. vaśitā), the bodhisattva's powers of control, rather than "empowerments," since the context lists attainments of the higher grounds. "Gone Far" is the seventh bodhisattva ground (Dūraṅgamā).  In the list the eighth item is written "rig pa" (knowledge), but in its explanation it is "rigs pa" (reasoning), which fits the definition and the Sanskrit yukti; I have rendered "reasoning" in both places. The word "rgyud" in this passage means "continuum" or "stream," not "tantra."  In the verse "its cutting off is the domain of the noble ones," the Tibetan verb is read as "cutting off" in line with the Sanskrit of this verse; it could also be read as "explaining it." In the verse on the perfected entity, the Tibetan is ambiguous and could also be read as "if the perfected entity is existent or nonexistent." The reading "if there is no perfected entity" follows the sense of the Sanskrit.  The phrase rendered "when they have abandoned all the habitual tendencies of faults" reads ན་མ་ཉེས་པ in the Tibetan, which does not construe. It is taken as a slip for ནམ་ཉེས་པ ("when ... of faults"), which fits the sense and the following clause. In this sūtra, དབང in the mind-made body passage and verses is rendered "mastery" (vaśitā) rather than "empowerment," as the context requires.  In "particular and general" the Tibetan seems to lack the noun "characteristics" after "general". I supplied it from the sense of the passage and from the Sanskrit parallel. The name ང་འཛིན is rendered Māndhātṛ following the Sanskrit parallel (rājā māndhātā), although the usual Tibetan for Māndhātṛ is different. "Death and transference of inconceivable transformation" refers to the subtle death of the noble ones beyond ordinary rebirth (acintyapariṇāmikī cyuti).  The phrase "they conceive of an escape where there is no escape" renders འབྱུང་བ in the sense of release or escape (Skt. niḥsaraṇa), as the parallel Sanskrit indicates. In "not apprehended as internal or external," the Tibetan reads ང ("I"), taken as a slip for ནང ("internal"), matching the preceding sentence.  "Destroyer" renders the Tibetan for the Sanskrit vaināśika, the nihilist who denies everything, continuing the discussion of the preceding piece. "The way of the established truth" and "the way of teaching" render the paired terms siddhānta-naya and deśanā-naya. The line rendered "being inverted, they do not see" could also be read as "they do not see its reversal."  "The supreme brahman" renders ཚངས་པའི་མཆོག; here ཚངས་པ most likely stands for the neuter brahma, the pure or holy state, rather than the god Brahmā. The phrase ལོ་རྒྱུས་ལས་བྱུང་བ, rendered "what has come down from the past," is obscure in this context.  སྒྲ is rendered "word" throughout (Skt. ruta, the spoken sound or letter), in contrast to དོན, "meaning" (artha). In "the characteristic of accumulating," དེ་ལ་སོགས་པ is read as "accumulating" (Skt. upacaya), in contrast to བྲི་བ, "diminishing" (apacaya), rather than as "and so forth." The "three coming together" refers to the meeting of sense faculty, object and consciousness.  The verse line on śrāvakas, "those who have attained the dharma of conceptuality," is translated as the Tibetan reads; Sanskrit witnesses and commentators read the verse differently, and its exact sense is debated. "Fields" in the verse beginning "In the distinction of fields and patience" follows the Tibetan, which may reflect a different Sanskrit reading. The ornament rendered "pendants" is uncertain; the Sanskrit parallel appears to list a svastika-shaped ornament. "Connection" renders the term used throughout this section for the binding linkage (saṃdhi) between dharmas and attachment.  "Linkage" renders the Tibetan term corresponding to Sanskrit saṃdhi, here the binding or attachment that ties beings to concepts and, in the rebirth context, links them into the destinies. "The meeting of the three" refers to the conjunction of sense faculty, object, and consciousness. One occurrence of the name Mahāmati is misspelled in the Tibetan and was read as Mahāmati.  "Utter solitude" renders རབ་ཏུ་དབེན་པ (Skt. vivikta), the isolation or freedom from conceptual constructs that the Laṅkāvatāra associates with the realization of noble wisdom. In the verses, "ground of all" renders ཀུན་གཞི (ālaya). The line "To propose that all things are unborn does not hold" renders མི་རྟག, literally "is not stable"; the sense is that the proposition is not established.  "Mere cognition" renders rnam par rig pa tsam (vijñaptimātra). The verse line "Through non-otherness and lack of characteristics" is terse in the Tibetan and its exact construction is uncertain. The final quotation continues into the next piece.  The Tibetan here reads ཟད་པ་མེད་པ ("absence of depletion") in the definition "is destruction"; the sense requires plain ཟད་པ, "depletion," and it is translated so. The phrase སློབ་པའི་མྱ་ངན in the list of sufferings is rendered simply as "sorrow"; སློབ་པའི looks like a scribal intrusion. The phrase "of a hundred thousand" applied to the Lokāyata is kept as literally as the Tibetan allows; it apparently refers to the vast extent of that body of teaching.  Lokāyata is the Indian materialist or "worldly" school of argument; its adherent is rendered Lokāyatika. "Material things" renders the Tibetan for Sanskrit āmiṣa, worldly gain or carnal enjoyment, contrasted with Dharma. The speaker of the line "Is not whatever teaches the unestablished itself Lokāyata?" is not marked in the Tibetan; it is taken here as the brahmin's interjection. In the list of what is Lokāyata, the single word rendered "fixation" is terse and its exact sense is uncertain. "Caressing" in the list of material things is tentative.  Here དབང་པོ, earlier rendered Indra, stands for the creator god Īśvara in the Sanskrit parallel (īśvara as independent agent), so it is rendered "the Lord (Īśvara)". In the list of nirvāṇa theories, "seeing signs and longing for bliss" follows the Tibetan as written; the Sanskrit parallel speaks of longing for the bliss of signlessness. "The six qualities" refers to the six measures of statecraft (ṣāḍguṇya).  In "all effects would also become tathāgatas," the Tibetan reads འབྲས་བུ་འོ་ཅག, where འོ་ཅག ordinarily means "we." The rendering "all effects" follows the evident sense of the argument, namely that if the Tathāgata were a made thing, every made effect would be a tathāgata. A reading such as "we effects" is possible but unlikely.  "If he were not distinct from the aggregates": the Tibetan as written reads "if he were not one with the aggregates," which contradicts the argument (identity with produced aggregates would entail impermanence); the evident sense, matching the Sanskrit, is translated. Similarly, "because a distinction in length is seen, they are not different" is rendered as written, though the Sanskrit parallel says such a distinction shows difference. In the verse "is not to be conceived as nonexistent," the Tibetan reads "impermanent," evidently a slip for the near-identical "should not be conceived," which the context requires. In the sentence about the Buddha's many names, the Tibetan lacks the negative of the Sanskrit parallel (that the childish do not know these as the Tathāgata's names); it is translated as it stands. The lists of synonyms (hand, body, earth, sky) render Sanskrit synonym sets with English equivalents.  The Tibetan reads that the childish "grasp" or "understand" (ཁོང་དུ་ཆུད) the Tathāgata through minds fallen into the two extremes. The Sanskrit parallel has a negation ("do not understand"), but the Tibetan is translated as it stands.

The names in the first list are given in their Sanskrit forms following the Sanskrit parallel: རྩེ་བ་པོ is Rāma, རྣམ་པར་འདོར is Vyāsa, སྟོབས་ཅན is Bali, ཡང་དག་མུ is Bhūtānta, and མི་གནོད་མུ་ཁྱུད is Ariṣṭanemi. "The ten masteries" renders དབང་བཅུ (the ten vaśitā).  "Cessation not brought about by analysis" renders the Abhidharma unconditioned dharma apratisaṃkhyānirodha. In Mahāmati's final argument the Tibetan reads "my doctrine"; contextually it seems to mean the doctrine as Mahāmati is construing it (the Buddha's teaching seen as indistinct from the tīrthikas'), and it is rendered "if, as I say, the doctrine has no distinction." "Illusory man" here translates the ordinary word for person in the phrase "magical man," not the Sāṃkhya puruṣa.  "Solitude" renders དབེན་པ (Skt. vivikta), the isolation or voidness of things from imagined nature. The line "External things are not nonentities" follows the Tibetan literally; the Sanskrit parallel speaks of neither external existence of things nor grasping by mind, and the Tibetan line is terse. In the verse on the lamp, the construal follows the sense of the Sanskrit, in which dependent connection would reveal substances as a lamp does. The quoted verse at the end continues into the next piece, where the quotation is closed.  The phrase rendered "impermanence itself does not cease" is terse in the Tibetan ("does not itself engage/come into play"); the rendering follows the evident sense of the argument. The paragraph on the "impermanence of shape" echoes the earlier listed view that "form itself is impermanent"; the Tibetan here reads "shape," and this is kept as written.
