The phrase rendered "even the person of their discourse, which was never established in the first place" is obscure in the Tibetan, and the sense is uncertain. On the seventh ground, the Tibetan leaves the subject of "absorption is entered moment of mind by moment of mind" unstated, and the passage could refer either to bodhisattvas or to śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas; I have kept it impersonal. "The first ground and the sixth" follows the Tibetan literally; it may be meant as the range from the first through the sixth grounds.  Maheśvara here names the highest abode at the summit of the form realm (associated in this sūtra with Akaniṣṭha and the final ground), not the god Śiva as such. In the verses, "abodes" renders the term for the stages of dwelling (vihāra). The phrase "the bliss of delight in the masteries" follows the Tibetan, which seems to read a Sanskrit compound slightly differently from the extant Sanskrit, where it refers to consecration in the masteries.  In the sentence about the accumulations for omniscient wisdom becoming pointless, the text reads "because of childish beings," which makes no sense here; the translation follows the evident reading "because of being made," which matches the argument that a made Tathāgata would render the accumulations pointless. In the final paragraph, the point is that such yogins mistakenly take the mere cessation of the seven consciousnesses for liberation.  "The ten paths that are gateways of the truth of samādhi" follows the Tibetan as written. The corresponding Sanskrit is generally understood as the gateways of the bliss of samādhi, so the Tibetan "truth" may reflect a different reading of the source. "Intrinsic natures" translates the term for svabhāva, which in this context refers to the three natures of Yogācāra doctrine.  "Correct wisdom" renders ཡང་དག་པའི་ཡེ་ཤེས (samyagjñāna), one of the five dharmas. The phrase ཐུག་པ་མེད་པའི་གནས་ལ་ཤིན་ཏུ་འབྲེལ་བའི་ཆོས་ཅན is obscure. It is rendered here as "his nature wholly bound up with the limitless state," and that reading is tentative. In this non-ritual context, དབང is taken as "masteries" (vaśitā) rather than "empowerment."  "Pleasant" or "armor" renders the Tibetan literally as examples of names uttered; the corresponding Sanskrit has different examples, and the Tibetan words may reflect a misreading in the translator's source. In the passage on showing that buddhahood is easy to attain, the Tibetan as it stands has "the birth of tathāgatas is like the udumbara flower" without a negation; the sense in the Sanskrit is that tathāgatas are not as rare as the udumbara, and I have translated the Tibetan as written. "Clear and free of stains" reads the evident dwangs ("clear") for the written "dang sa." Here dbang is rendered "masteries" (vaśitā), not "empowerment," since it stands in a list with the powers and superknowledges.  The phrase rendered "Otherwise" corresponds to Sanskrit anyatra, which can also mean "except"; the sense is that conceptuality operates only because one's own mind's appearances are not understood. In "to go forth is the meaning of going," the Sanskrit parallel reads "going means destruction"; the Tibetan here says that entering or going forth is what going means, and I have followed the Tibetan.  The phrase describing the tathāgatagarbha as "not experiencing pleasure and pain yet accompanied by their cause" follows the Tibetan wording, which is compressed and could also be read as the tathāgatagarbha being the cause, together with which pleasure and pain are experienced. The Sanskrit reads more simply that it transmigrates and proceeds together with the cause of pleasure and pain. In the final verse, the Tibetan word that the glossary gives for a class of spirits (bhūta) here clearly means the material elements.  The clause "because, being without earlier and later, it does not newly arise" renders a terse Tibetan phrase whose exact construction is uncertain. In the passage on the actual Tathāgata, the word rendered "faculties" is the ordinary word for sense faculties, not the name Indra. The "alms bowl that came back as though washed clean" refers to the episode in which the Buddha returned with an empty bowl after his alms round.  The opening verses close the preceding chapter; their sense is compressed, and the reading of the first stanza's last two lines follows the parallel Sanskrit sense (beings go by that path, and so they do not reach final cessation). The four habitual tendencies are taken as those of existence, desire, form and views. Here འབྱུང་པོ is rendered "living creatures" rather than the spirit class, since the context is ordinary animals. The quoted criticism continues into the next piece.  ཞི་ལྡན is taken as King Śibi of the well-known story of the hawk and the pigeon, since the narrative plainly refers to it; the Tibetan form is unusual. ཁ་འདའ is taken as sugar candy (Skt. khaṇḍa) and ཧྭགས as treacle (Skt. phāṇita), on the basis of the parallel Sanskrit list of foods. The clause about those who "indulge heavily" is compressed in the Tibetan and has been rendered according to its evident sense.  The phrase rendered "whose view of the transitory collection has grown strong in their disputation" is obscure in the Tibetan; the sense is that these monks hold to the view of a real self (satkāyadṛṣṭi) and dispute accordingly. Where the Tibetan has "would I also have forbidden" without an explicit negative, it is read as a rhetorical question, which agrees with the argument that meat was never permitted. "The ten that die naturally" refers to the meat of ten kinds of animals that have died of natural causes.  "Ever like a dream" renders ཁོར་ཟུག, which is not a clear word; read as ཁོར་ཡུག, "continually," matching the Sanskrit sadā. ཕྲ་མེན, which recurs in the spirit lists, is rendered as ḍāka and ḍākinī; ལུས་སྲུལ as pūtana; གནོན་པོ as ostāraka; and བརྗེད་བྱེད as apasmāra, following the standard equivalents. The phrase "pure in three respects" follows the Tibetan list of unconceived, unrequested and unurged.  "The domain of the noble gods" renders འཕགས་པའི་ལྷ་ཡི་སྤྱོད་ཡུལ literally; the intended sense may be the domain of the noble ones' divine (meditative) abiding. "The Principal of the world" takes གཙོ as the Sāṃkhya pradhāna, listed with the "makers" as imagined creators. The final line opens a verse that continues in the next piece.  "In the view of mere cognition" reads the unusual ལྟ་ཤྭ as ལྟ་བ, "view." The line "conceptuality is but the word 'fire'" renders the obscure རྟོག་པ་མེ་ཡི་ཚིག; ཚིག might instead mean "burning," giving "conceptuality is the burning of fire." In the bird simile, the two bird names, བྱ་གག and ཐི་བ, could not be identified with confidence. They are rendered generally as "a bird" and "a bird of another kind."  In the verse on Akaniṣṭha, the Tibetan reads "speech" (ngag), which makes little sense; I have read it as "pure" (dag), a similar-looking syllable that matches the sense of the parallel Sanskrit verse. "A body belonging among the classes of beings" renders an obscure expression that likely refers to the emanation body taken on by bodhisattvas. In the verse on the Vaiśeṣikas and Sāṃkhyas, the term usually meaning "indeterminate" refers here to the questions left unanswered, as the following verse on the four kinds of answer shows.  In the verse beginning "These phenomena are unborn," the second line reads literally "nor are they pure" (de dag pa), which does not fit; the sense of the parallel verse and the metre suggest a dropped syllable for "nor are they nonexistent" (de dag med pa'ang), and it has been translated so. The three characteristics (imputed, dependent, perfected) and "transformation" (parāvṛtti, for ཤིན་ཏུ་གྱུར་པ) are Yogācāra technical terms.  The three embryological terms (སླ་གར, རྨན་བུ, འབྲས) correspond to the standard fetal stages (Skt. peśī, arbuda, ghana); their exact correspondence here is uncertain, so they are rendered descriptively. བེ་ད (the southern land of Nāga's appearance) corresponds to Vedalī in the Sanskrit; I keep the Tibetan form. In the verse on the masteries, དབང renders vaśitā ("mastery"), not empowerment; མཐའ in the final list is the "ultimate limit" (bhūtakoṭi), not an "extreme".  The verse listing "the tenth is the first, the first is the eighth" and so on refers to the bodhisattva grounds (bhūmis): in the imageless there is no real sequence among them. The Tibetan uses collective forms with པོ for several numbers, but the sense is plainly ordinal and they have been rendered so. In "the heirs who are pratyekabuddhas" the Tibetan reads རང་རྒྱལ་སྲས་པོ, apparently the "sons who are pratyekajinas," set beside the "children of the Victor" (bodhisattvas) of the earlier verse. "Those within the schools" renders རིས་སུ་གཏོགས, Buddhists of the sectarian schools who are placed here alongside the non-Buddhists.  In the verse on birth and cause, the Tibetan reads "like causes and the rest" (rgyu), which makes little sense. I have followed the evident reading "illusions" (sgyu), a one-letter difference.

The Sanskrit of the famous verse on nonappearance says the yogin abiding there "sees the Mahāyāna." This Tibetan has the negative, and the translation follows the Tibetan.

The verse beginning "Seeing, with conceit, the positing of truth" and the one that follows it ("the single one is established as fivefold") are obscure, and the rendering is tentative.

In the line "no karma and no fire," "fire" (me) is odd in a list of doctrinal categories. It is kept as written, but it may be a scribal slip.  In the second stanza the Tibetan reads "one comes to see permanence and annihilation" with no negation; the Sanskrit parallel probably means that one abiding in mind only does not see them. The positive reading is translated as it stands. The six-line stanza on the śrāvakas' discernment is syntactically compressed in the Tibetan. The rendering follows the likely sense that the śrāvakas' discernment operates through clinging to entities, while the stainless discernment of the Sugata is free of appearance and of the two vehicles. "Those given to desire" probably renders icchantika, those cut off from the roots of virtue, though the Tibetan wording here is not the usual one.  "Curved" renders the obscure syllables at the start of the line on the cloth-cutting knife; the reading is uncertain. In the line on improper food, the Tibetan has "wood" (shing), which makes no sense there; it has been read as "death" (shi), giving the standard prohibition of food from houses of death or birth.  The text as written has a word meaning "by the foolish" where "the childish" (ordinary beings) is plainly required; it is translated as "the childish." In the line "the masteries and the ten grounds," the term usually rendered "empowerment" here denotes the bodhisattva masteries (vaśitā); in the three-nature verses the same word is short for the dependent nature. In "The five imagine what appears," the Tibetan reads "the fifth," evidently for "the five," the five sense consciousnesses. The quotation marks follow the dialogue between Mahāmati and the Buddha implied by the context; the Tibetan gives no explicit speaker labels.  In the line "grasp at hairs and the forms of dreams," the syllable ska (སྐ) makes no clear sense here; it has been read as a near-duplicate of skra, "hair," in the sense of the illusory hair-nets mentioned later.
The phrase "do not occur on the seventh ground" renders ས་བདུན, which could also be read as "the seven grounds." It is taken as the seventh ground because the following line contrasts it with the eighth.
Excellent Intelligence, Gone Afar and Cloud of Dharma are the ninth, seventh and tenth bodhisattva grounds. "Dependent" and "imagined" are two of the three natures (paratantra and parikalpita), and "the natures" refers to these three natures.  The simile rendered "like a plantain trunk" translates a Tibetan phrase meaning literally "a water-born heap." The plantain is the usual image of hollowness, but the reading is not certain. In the verse on conditions, "craving" follows the Tibetan as written. A reading "existence" (a similar spelling) would also fit the argument. The line on the logicians, "the absence of things is no extreme," is obscure in the Tibetan and is rendered literally. Here "brahman" denotes the supreme state, not the god Brahmā.  In the verse beginning "Not knowing that external objects," the word usually meaning "extreme" is taken as "thesis" (the proposition of a formal proof), since the following verse lists reason, thesis, examples and members of proof. The question "Who has made entities into real entities that cycle on?" renders an elliptical phrase; it may also be read as asking how entityhood brings about saṃsāra. The double negative in "It is not that nothing is observed" is kept as the Tibetan has it. "Those who destroy" renders an obscure phrase whose object is not stated.  The word rendered "people" in "the livelihood of people" is written བུས་པ, which is unclear; it has been taken as "those born, people." The line "There is no other and no dependence" appears to split the term "dependent nature" (paratantra) across the line for metrical reasons. The epithet ཡན་ལག before Kapila is obscure and has been left untranslated. Speakers are inferred: the quoted objections and the question addressed to the Blessed One, marked by humble verbs, come from the questioner, and the replies from the Buddha.  "Nourished" renders རྒྱས་པ, in the sense of mind being fostered by habitual tendencies; here it is not the name Vyāsa. The line pairing the rabbit's horn with "a cause of nonexistence" is terse in the Tibetan. It seems to mean that the rabbit's horn serves as the stock example of what does not exist, in contrast to pots, cloth and the like, which arise from collections of causes.  The line "Intrinsic nature is free of mind, and mind proceeds to the noble ones" is obscure in the Tibetan; the term rendered "intrinsic nature" could instead be the Sāṃkhya "primal nature" (prakṛti), given the Sāṃkhyas named in the next line. The verse beginning "Knowing the appearances of mind" is syntactically difficult and is rendered tentatively. "The five dharmas, the natures" refers to the standard Laṅkāvatāra set of five dharmas and three natures (svabhāva). "The transformation that is without basis" reflects the doctrine of the transformation of the basis (āśrayaparāvṛtti).  In the line rendered "Because cause and effect are upheld," the Tibetan reads "good" (bzang ba); this has been read as "held, upheld" (bzung ba), which fits the argument. The verse "Like 'hand' and 'doer' in the world, like Indra, Śakra, and Purandara" plays on synonyms (two Sanskrit words for "hand," and three names of Indra) to show that different names refer to a single thing. "The three natures" and "the natures" here are the three natures (trisvabhāva) of Yogācāra, not the primal nature of Sāṃkhya. Several verses are terse, and the speaker changes without any marker. The questions are taken as Mahāmati's and the answers as the Buddha's, following the vocatives and humble or honorific verbs.  The stanza on the "five śrāvakas" is obscure in the Tibetan; its items are rendered literally and their exact identification is uncertain. "The water element has the nature of an emanation" renders the Tibetan as written; one would expect a quality such as moisture or cohesion, and the original sense is unclear. "The meaning of solitude" renders the term for the isolated, ultimate meaning (viviktārtha).  The repeated word rendered "giving" translates the Tibetan as written; its sense in this discussion of self and appropriation is obscure, and the underlying Sanskrit may have meant something other than generosity. "The lethal power in a drug" renders a phrase that literally reads "the killing of medicine". Throughout this passage the text voices a qualified, tathāgatagarbha-based teaching of a pure "self" set against non-Buddhist and nihilist views; the translation keeps the Tibetan wording without harmonizing it.  ཁུར་འཕེལ is taken as the Bhārata (bhāra "burden"), matching the list of epic and dynastic names; སྐྱབས in the dynastic list is taken as the Guptas. The verse on the ages (བྱིས་པའི་དུས, སྐྱབས་བྱུང་དུས, གཉིས་པོ) is obscure. The Tibetan seems to be rendering the four yugas, with བྱས་དུས as the kṛta or "perfect" age, but the first terms are translated literally. The lines about Mati and Mekhala are terse; the rendering follows the most natural reading of the Tibetan. "Bhudhuka" is kept as the Tibetan transliterates it, since its identity is unclear. Somagupta is taken as the father's name.  The verses of this closing section are terse and in places obscure in Tibetan. "Ṛṣabha, of great and pure glory" may instead name a separate figure (perhaps a Vimalaśrī) rather than describe Ṛṣabha. In "the fleshly eye and wisdom," the Tibetan has only "of flesh," taken here as shorthand for the fleshly eye. "The wheel in the opening of a house" and "a house of many guests" render obscure similes literally. "In beings they hold no elements" is a tentative reading.  This version of the sūtra was translated from Chinese, and these closing verses are terse and in places obscure. "The elephant's bed" renders the Tibetan literally; the image is unclear. "The Heroic Progress" renders the samādhi known as Śūraṅgama. "The seven grounds" follows the Tibetan, where one might expect a reference to the eighth ground as the stage beyond the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. The quatrain on taking food appears to be a monastic rule embedded among the verses.

The following is a note about this translation.

This is an AI-generated translation from the AI Stupa project. It was produced by a careful, doctrine-aware process aiming at a high degree of accuracy, but it has not been reviewed by a human translator and may contain errors. It is offered as a provisional translation for those texts of the Tibetan Buddhist canon that have not yet received an authoritative human translation. Where a published translation exists — above all from 84000: Translating the Words of the Buddha (84000.co) — that translation is definitive and should be preferred; please consult it instead. These translations are released into the public domain (CC0) in the spirit of spreading the Dharma to all corners of the earth.

Just as the Bodhi tree now standing at Bodh Gayā neither is nor is not the same tree beneath which the Buddha attained awakening, so too this stupa is intended as the next generation of stupa — dependently arising according to the conditions of its environment, and ceaselessly proclaiming the Dharma for the benefit of all sentient beings.

May whoever encounters these words derive great benefit.
